What this page is

Seventeen questions from InterviewDB, bought and supplied by you, worked to the same fourteen parts each: the statement as given, understanding, examples, the key observation, brute force, the optimal approach, a walkthrough, C#, a code walkthrough, complexity, edge cases, follow-ups, what is being tested, and the sentences to say. The page does three things the earlier pages could not. It keeps the original wording, so you revise the question that was asked rather than my paraphrase of it. It cross-checks every question against the research already in this workspace, and says when two sources disagree instead of quietly picking one. And every listing is read from src/cs/qbank/ at build time, so nothing on this page is code I did not compile.
The code was run, not just written269 assertions, 0 failures on .NET 10.0.103. Three of the checks are property tests, not fixed expectations: the covered-size structure against a brute-force bitmap over 300 random trials, the four island variants against brute force over every window across 3,000 trials, and the tournament tree against its own sorted leaves over 500 random tournaments. cd companies/linkedin/src/cs/qbank && dotnet run -c Release
What testing caughtWriting the tests found one real bug and two wrong expectations of my own. The bug: TaskManager.AddTask overwrote an existing task’s dependency edges instead of unioning them, so a cycle closed across two AddTask calls went undetected. Q15 documents it. The wrong expectations were mine, in the island tests. Both are noted where they occurred, because "my first answer was wrong here" is more useful to revise than a clean page that hides it.
On provenanceNothing here is a prediction. These questions were reported to InterviewDB by candidates; that makes them a preparation signal and nothing stronger. No question on this page is claimed to appear in your loop, and where the only evidence is a single unverifiable entry, the page says so. 10 of the 17 are corroborated by at least one other source already in this workspace — those are the ones marked high priority.

Q1

LRU cache with TTL

Beyond InterviewDB there is a report of LRU built with the AI assistant available in the CWAI round, a report of an LFU cache with a GetRank() method and LRU as the tiebreaker, and All O’one Data Structure on the reader’s own list. Different eviction keys, one skeleton. Learn the skeleton once and all of them fall out.

1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Implement an LRU Cache with TTL expiry. It is similar to a standard LRU but each entry has a time-to-live. get() should return -1 if the key has expired even if it is still in the cache.
Follow-up, as suppliedHow would you handle cleanup of expired entries without impacting get() latency? Discuss: lazy eviction, background cleanup, trade-offs, complexity, concurrency, memory implications.
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Supplied with a follow-up already attached, which is unusual and useful: the follow-up tells you the interviewer’s actual interest is cleanup strategy, not the LRU.
Reported elsewhere
LRU cache, built with the AI assistant available — Hello Interview’s write-up of LinkedIn’s AI-enabled coding round, Feb 2026. Same structure, different round.
Reported elsewhere
LFU cache with a GetRank() function and LRU as the tiebreaker — LeetCode Discuss, Staff SWE with offer, Jun 2025. Sits between this question and Q13.
Reported elsewhere
All O’one Data Structure (LeetCode 432) — on the reader’s own list of past LinkedIn questions, which is first-party evidence.
LeetCode
146 LRU Cache is the base. 460 LFU Cache is the same skeleton with a different key. Neither adds TTL, so the TTL half is genuinely InterviewDB-specific here.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported, and the family is independently reported three times. The TTL variation itself rests on InterviewDB alone; the cache skeleton does not.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickTwo things the statement leaves open, and both change code. 1. Is the TTL per entry or per cache? "each entry has a time-to-live" reads as per entry, so Put takes a TTL. If it is one TTL for the whole cache it is a constructor argument, and the expiry field per node becomes just the insertion time. 2. Does an expired entry still occupy capacity? "even if it is still in the cache" says yes — expiry is logical, not physical. That single sentence is what forces lazy expiry and is why the follow-up about cleanup exists at all.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A cache that forgets things for two different reasons at once. It forgets the least-recently-used entry because it is full, and it forgets any entry whose clock has run out. Those are independent, and that is the whole difficulty: capacity is about ordering, expiry is about time, and the two do not agree on who should go first.

What we are being asked to compute

Three operations. Get(key) returns the value, or -1 if the key is absent or expired. Put(key, value, ttl) inserts or updates, renewing the expiry. And under the covers, eviction picks a victim when the cache is full.

Constraints that matter
  • Both operations should be O(1). That rules out scanning for an expired entry on the hot path.
  • An expired entry is still physically present until something removes it, so Count and capacity are counted in entries, not in live entries.
  • The clock has to be injectable, or the behaviour is untestable. Passing Func<long> in the constructor costs nothing and is the difference between a design you can test and one you can only hope about.
  • Time is monotonic. If it is not — an NTP step backwards — entries can appear to un-expire. Use a monotonic source, not wall-clock.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
TTL is supplied per entry, on Put.If it is one cache-wide TTL, the constructor takes it and the node stores an insertion time instead. Two lines different; ask rather than guess.
A re-Put of an existing key renews the TTL.If it must not, an update has to preserve the original expiry, and a hot key can then expire mid-use — which is sometimes exactly what you want for a session cache.
Expiry is inclusive: an entry with expiresAt == now is dead.One character in the comparison. Whichever you pick, say it, because a test will sit exactly on that boundary.
Single-threaded unless told otherwise.Under concurrency the doubly linked list is the problem, not the dictionary: two threads relinking the same neighbours corrupt it silently. See the follow-up.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Givencapacity 2, clock at t=0empty
Put(1,100,ttl=10); Put(2,200,ttl=10)both live
Get(1) at t=9100 — inside its TTL
Get(1) at t=10-1 — expiry is inclusive, so it is dead exactly on the boundary
MinePut(1,·); Put(2,·); Get(1); Put(3,·)2 is evicted, not 1 — the Get made 1 recent
MinePut(7,70,ttl=10) at t=0; Put(7,71,ttl=10) at t=8expires at t=18, not t=10
Where these came fromThe statement supplied no worked example, so only the boundary case above follows directly from its wording. The two marked Mine are cases I would raise, because both are places an implementation can be wrong while passing the obvious test.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
Start from what has to be O(1) and work backwards — do not start from "use a HashMap". Eviction needs to find the least-recently-used entry and remove it in constant time. A dictionary can find a node instantly but has no idea which one is oldest. An array knows the order but costs O(n) to move an element to the front. Neither structure can do both, so you hold both: a doubly linked list for the order, and a dictionary from key to the list node. The list gives O(1) reordering provided you already have the node, and the dictionary is what gives you the node. That pairing is the answer to plain LRU. Now the TTL. The tempting move is a second ordered structure keyed on expiry, so you can find the next entry to die. Resist it for a moment and ask what the requirement actually says: Get must return -1 for an expired key. It does not say the entry must be gone. So the cheapest correct implementation is to check the expiry of the one entry you already hold, on the path where you already hold it — lazy expiry. One comparison, no new structure, no background work. That is the key observation, and it generalises: a deadline you only have to honour when someone looks does not need a timer. The expiry heap only becomes necessary when the requirement changes from "return -1" to "reclaim the memory", which is precisely what the supplied follow-up asks.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Keep a List<(key, value, expiresAt, lastUsed)>. On Get, scan for the key; if found and live, stamp lastUsed with a counter and return it. On Put when full, scan for the smallest lastUsed and remove it.

What it costs

Get O(n), Put O(n), space O(capacity). Every operation is a linear scan.

Why it stops workingIt is not that O(n) is slow at capacity 100. It is that a cache exists to be faster than the thing behind it, and an O(n) cache has a cost that grows with its own hit rate — the bigger you make it to improve the hit rate, the slower each hit becomes. The design is self-defeating, which is a better answer than "linear is bad".
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTGet(key) / Put(key, value, ttl), plus an injectable clock
↓
TRANSFORMATIONEvery entry becomes a list node carrying the key, the value and an absolute expiry time. Absolute, not remaining — remaining would have to be decremented.
↓
DATA STRUCTUREDictionary<int, Node> for lookup, plus a doubly linked list with head and tail sentinels for recency order
↓
ALGORITHMLookup in the dictionary; check expiry; on a live hit move the node to the front; on overflow unlink the node before the tail sentinel
↓
OUTPUTThe value, or -1 for absent or expired
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
Dictionary<int, Node>O(1) key → node. Storing the node, not the value, is the whole trick: it is what lets the list be reordered without searching it.
Doubly linked listO(1) unlink of a node you already hold. Singly linked will not do it — you would need the predecessor, which costs a scan.
Head and tail sentinelsRemoves every null check from Unlink and LinkFront. This is not tidiness; the null-handling is where hand-written LRU actually breaks under time pressure.
An absolute ExpiresAt on the nodeComparing two longs is the entire expiry check, and it works no matter how long the entry has sat there.
No expiry-ordered structure, on purposeCorrectness does not need one. Adding it before the requirement demands it is the most common over-engineering in this question.
The algorithm
  1. Get: dictionary lookup. Miss → -1.
  2. Compare _clock() >= node.ExpiresAt. Dead → unlink, remove from the dictionary, return -1. Reclaiming it here is free, because you are already holding the node.
  3. Live → move the node to the front and return its value.
  4. Put: if the key exists, overwrite the value, reset ExpiresAt, move to front, done.
  5. Otherwise, if at capacity, take _tail.Prev — the least recently used — unlink it and drop its key.
  6. Create the node, insert it in the dictionary, link it at the front.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

Capacity 2, clock injected. [a, b] means a is most recent. Watch the point where Get removes something.

StepList (recent first)DictionaryReturns
t=0 Put(1,100,10)[1]1 → node(exp 10)—
t=0 Put(2,200,10)[2, 1]1, 2—
t=1 Get(1)[1, 2]1, 2100 — live, so 1 moves to the front
t=1 Put(3,300,10)[3, 1]1, 3evicted 2, because Get(1) made 2 the LRU
t=10 Get(1)[3]3-1 — expired, and unlinked on the read path
t=10 Get(3)[](empty)-1 — also expired; the cache is now empty
What the state tells youTwo things to point at. At t=1 the eviction victim was 2, not 1, because a read counts as use — that is the LRU half working. At t=10 a read mutated the cache, which is the TTL half working: lazy expiry means Get is not a pure function, and saying so before they ask is worth a mark.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The cache itself 82 lines
Q01_LruTtl.cs
// Q1 — LRU cache with per-entry TTL. public class LruTtlCache { private sealed class Node { public int Key, Value; public long ExpiresAt; // absolute time, in the clock's own units public Node Prev, Next; } private readonly int _capacity; private readonly Func<long> _clock; private readonly Dictionary<int, Node> _map; private readonly Node _head = new(); // sentinel: most-recently-used side private readonly Node _tail = new(); // sentinel: eviction side public LruTtlCache(int capacity, Func<long> clock = null) { if (capacity <= 0) throw new ArgumentOutOfRangeException(nameof(capacity)); _capacity = capacity; _clock = clock ?? (() => DateTimeOffset.UtcNow.ToUnixTimeMilliseconds()); _map = new Dictionary<int, Node>(capacity); _head.Next = _tail; _tail.Prev = _head; } public int Get(int key) { if (!_map.TryGetValue(key, out var node)) return -1; if (IsExpired(node)) { // lazy expiry: dead on read, so drop it now Remove(node); return -1; } MoveToFront(node); return node.Value; } public void Put(int key, int value, long ttl) { long expiresAt = _clock() + ttl; if (_map.TryGetValue(key, out var existing)) { existing.Value = value; // a re-put renews the TTL existing.ExpiresAt = expiresAt; MoveToFront(existing); return; } if (_map.Count == _capacity) { var victim = _tail.Prev; // strict O(1): always the LRU end Remove(victim); } var node = new Node { Key = key, Value = value, ExpiresAt = expiresAt }; _map[key] = node; LinkFront(node); } // An entry is dead the moment the clock reaches its expiry. private bool IsExpired(Node n) => _clock() >= n.ExpiresAt; private void Remove(Node n) { Unlink(n); _map.Remove(n.Key); } private void Unlink(Node n) { n.Prev.Next = n.Next; n.Next.Prev = n.Prev; } private void LinkFront(Node n) { n.Next = _head.Next; n.Prev = _head; _head.Next.Prev = n; _head.Next = n; } private void MoveToFront(Node n) { Unlink(n); LinkFront(n); } public int Count => _map.Count; // includes entries that are expired but not yet reclaimed }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
Why the node holds the key as well as the value

When eviction picks _tail.Prev, it has a node and needs to delete the matching dictionary entry — which needs the key. Without Node.Key there is no way back from the list to the dictionary, and this is the single most common omission in a first attempt.

The invariant, stated once

The dictionary and the list hold exactly the same set of entries at every point between operations. Every mutation touches both. Remove exists precisely so that pairing cannot drift: there is one function that unlinks and deletes, and nothing else does either alone.

Why Get is allowed to mutate

On an expired hit it unlinks and removes. That is not a side effect smuggled in; it is the cheapest possible reclamation, because the node is already in hand. It also means the cache self-cleans along whatever access pattern it actually has — hot keys get checked often, cold keys sit until they are evicted by capacity.

Why eviction never scans

EvictOne could look for an already-expired victim and prefer it, which would be nicer. It would also be O(capacity), and the stated requirement is O(1). Keeping the eviction strictly at _tail.Prev preserves the bound; the scan belongs in the sweeper, where it is bounded and off the hot path.

The bug the sentinels prevent

Without sentinels, Unlink needs four null checks and LinkFront needs two, and the case that breaks is removing the only node — head and tail both have to be updated. With sentinels, n.Prev and n.Next are never null, so the four-line version is correct for every case including the last one.

What Count deliberately does not tell you

It counts entries, including expired ones not yet reclaimed. A LiveCount would have to walk the whole list. If a metric is needed, count reclamations instead — a counter is O(1), a census is not.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
GetO(1)O(1)One dictionary probe, one comparison, two pointer swaps
PutO(1)O(1)One probe; eviction is _tail.Prev, never a search
evictionO(1)O(1)The victim is always the node before the tail sentinel
Sweep(budget)O(k log n)O(n) heapk reclaimed, each a heap pop; the heap holds one record per Put
overall space—O(capacity)One node and one dictionary entry per held key
The part worth saying out loudAll O(1) here means expected, not worst case, because the dictionary is a hash table. The list operations are genuinely worst-case O(1); the hashing is not. It is a small distinction and a real one, and saying it unprompted reads very differently from being corrected on it.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
capacity <= 0Throws in the constructor. A zero-capacity cache that silently accepts Put and always misses is worse than a loud failure.
Get on a key never insertedDictionary miss → -1, nothing mutated.
TTL of 0Expires immediately, since expiresAt == now and the check is >=. It still occupies a slot until something reclaims it — worth stating.
Negative TTLAlready expired on arrival. Accepted as written; if the interviewer wants a throw, that is one line, but say which you chose.
Re-Put of an existing key when fullNo eviction, because the count does not change. Getting this wrong evicts a live entry for nothing.
Every entry expiredGet reclaims them one at a time as they are touched. The cache never holds a stale value, but it can hold stale memory — exactly the gap the follow-up closes.
Clock jumps backwardsEntries appear to un-expire. Use a monotonic clock (Environment.TickCount64, not DateTime.Now).
Concurrent Get on the same keyTwo threads can both decide an entry is expired and both unlink it, corrupting the list. Needs a lock; see the follow-up.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 4 follow-ups.
“How would you handle cleanup of expired entries without impacting get() latency?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The question is not "how do I delete expired entries" — lazy expiry already makes them unreachable. It is "how do I reclaim their memory without putting work on the read path". So the constraint is: Get must not get slower. That immediately rules out any sweep that runs inside Get, and points at work that happens somewhere else.

Approach

Keep lazy expiry exactly as it is — it is what guarantees correctness — and add a bounded background sweep for reclamation. A min-heap keyed on expiry time gives the sweeper the next entry to die in O(1) peek, so a sweep that finds nothing due costs one comparison. Each sweep takes a budget, so one pass can never stall the process. The subtlety is that a re-Put renews the TTL, which leaves a stale record in the heap pointing at an entry that is no longer due. Removing from a heap by key is O(n), so do not: give each entry a version and let the sweeper discard any record whose version no longer matches. Stale records cost memory until they surface, and nothing else.

Trade-off

Three options, and the right answer names all three. Lazy only — zero background cost, Get stays O(1), but memory is held by dead entries until they are touched or evicted. Fine when capacity is the binding constraint anyway, which for a fixed-size cache it usually is. Lazy plus a bounded sweep — memory is reclaimed promptly, Get is untouched, and the cost is one heap entry per Put plus the version field. This is the answer I would give. A timing wheel — buckets of entries by coarse expiry slot, O(1) insert with no heap and no comparisons. Better than the heap when TTLs are few and similar (all 30 seconds, say), because then a bucket per second is cheap and exact. Worse when TTLs are arbitrary and wide, because you either get poor granularity or a huge wheel. Naming when it wins is the point; naming it as a buzzword is not.

The sweeping variant — note that Get is byte-for-byte the same policy
Q01b_Sweeper.cs
// Q1 follow-up — the same cache with a bounded background sweep. // Get() is untouched: it still does lazy expiry and never waits for the sweeper. public class SweptLruTtlCache { private sealed class Node { public int Key, Value; public long ExpiresAt; public long Version; // bumped on every re-put, so stale heap entries are detectable public Node Prev, Next; } private readonly int _capacity; private readonly Func<long> _clock; private readonly Dictionary<int, Node> _map = new(); private readonly PriorityQueue<(int Key, long Version), long> _expiries = new(); // priority = ExpiresAt private readonly Node _head = new(), _tail = new(); private long _versionCounter; public SweptLruTtlCache(int capacity, Func<long> clock) { _capacity = capacity; _clock = clock; _head.Next = _tail; _tail.Prev = _head; } public void Put(int key, int value, long ttl) { long expiresAt = _clock() + ttl; long version = ++_versionCounter; if (_map.TryGetValue(key, out var node)) { node.Value = value; node.ExpiresAt = expiresAt; node.Version = version; MoveToFront(node); } else { if (_map.Count == _capacity) Remove(_tail.Prev); node = new Node { Key = key, Value = value, ExpiresAt = expiresAt, Version = version }; _map[key] = node; LinkFront(node); } _expiries.Enqueue((key, version), expiresAt); } public int Get(int key) { if (!_map.TryGetValue(key, out var node)) return -1; if (_clock() >= node.ExpiresAt) { Remove(node); return -1; } MoveToFront(node); return node.Value; } // Called from a timer. budget caps the work so one sweep cannot stall the process. public int Sweep(int budget) { long now = _clock(); int reclaimed = 0; while (reclaimed < budget && _expiries.TryPeek(out var top, out long due) && due <= now) { _expiries.Dequeue(); // Stale entry: the key was re-put with a later TTL, so this record is not the live one. if (!_map.TryGetValue(top.Key, out var node) || node.Version != top.Version) continue; Remove(node); reclaimed++; } return reclaimed; } public int Count => _map.Count; public int PendingExpiries => _expiries.Count; private void Remove(Node n) { Unlink(n); _map.Remove(n.Key); } private void Unlink(Node n) { n.Prev.Next = n.Next; n.Next.Prev = n.Prev; } private void LinkFront(Node n) { n.Next = _head.Next; n.Prev = _head; _head.Next.Prev = n; _head.Next = n; } private void MoveToFront(Node n) { Unlink(n); LinkFront(n); } }
TimeSpace
Put O(log n) for the heap insert · Get O(1) unchanged · Sweep(k) O(k log n)O(capacity) plus one heap record per Put, including stale ones until they surface
“Make it thread-safe. What exactly are you protecting?” · likely pushback
Thinking

Name the races before naming the lock. There are two, and they are different. The dictionary can be made concurrent off the shelf. The doubly linked list cannot — two threads relinking overlapping neighbours produce a list that is still walkable and quietly wrong, which is the worst failure mode there is.

Approach

A single lock around the whole operation is the correct first answer, and it is not a cop-out: the critical section is a handful of pointer writes, so contention is short even if it is frequent. ConcurrentDictionary alone is not enough and claiming it is would be the actual mistake. If measurement says the lock is the bottleneck, the standard escape is sharding: N independent caches, each with its own lock, keyed by hash(key) % N. Recency then becomes per-shard rather than global, which is a real semantic change — you are no longer evicting the globally least-recently-used entry. Most production caches accept that trade; say that you know you are making it.

Trade-off

One lock: simple, obviously correct, serialises every operation including reads. Sharded locks: near-linear read scaling, approximate LRU. Lock-free: the recency list makes it very hard, and the usual production answer is to abandon strict LRU for something sampled — which is what Redis actually does, and it is worth knowing that the industry chose approximation here.

TimeSpace
O(1) per operation plus lock acquisitionUnchanged, plus one lock per shard
“The cache is distributed across many nodes now. What changes?” · likely pushback
Thinking

Almost everything, and the honest answer leads with which part of the question stops being the interesting one. LRU ordering across nodes needs coordination on every read, which no one does.

Approach

Partition by key with consistent hashing, and make each node an independent local cache of exactly this design. Recency becomes per-node. TTL, on the other hand, travels very well: it is an absolute timestamp, needs no coordination, and every node can decide expiry alone — provided clocks are reasonably synchronised, which for second-scale TTLs they are.

Trade-off

You lose global LRU and gain linear scale. You also gain a new failure mode: on a topology change, keys move and a moved key’s TTL must be interpreted against the new node’s clock. Absolute expiries survive that; remaining-time TTLs do not — which retroactively justifies storing ExpiresAt rather than ttl in the node.

TimeSpace
O(1) local, plus one network hopO(capacity) per node
“Why not just use MemoryCache, or Redis with EXPIRE?” · likely pushback
Thinking

In production that is very often the right call, and saying so is not weakness. The question is being asked to see whether you know what is inside them.

Approach

MemoryCache gives absolute and sliding expiry and a size limit already. Redis gives EXPIRE plus maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru. Either removes the need for this code. I would reach for one of them unless I needed behaviour they do not offer — a custom eviction key, a specific memory shape, or no dependency.

Trade-off

Building it costs a day and owns a bug surface forever. Adopting one costs a dependency and gives up control of the eviction policy. What I would not do is build it and pretend the library does not exist — the interview answer and the production answer are allowed to differ, as long as you say which one you are giving.

TimeSpace
——
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Data-structure composition — recognising that no single structure meets the requirement, and picking the pair that does.
  • Reading the requirement precisely — "return -1 even if it is still in the cache" is the sentence that decides the whole design, and it is easy to skim past.
  • Knowing when not to add a structure — the expiry heap is the trap, and declining it until the follow-up demands it is the signal.
  • Complexity honesty — amortised versus worst case, expected versus guaranteed.
  • Concurrency instinct — identifying that the list, not the map, is the unsafe part.
  • Testability — whether the clock is injectable, which separates people who have written this in production from people who have written it on a whiteboard.
A senior engineer might say
Builds the dictionary plus doubly linked list correctly, adds an expiry check in Get, and gets all the pointer cases right. That is a complete, correct answer to the question as asked.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does the same, then says what was deliberately left out and why: no expiry heap until reclamation is required, eviction stays strictly O(1) rather than preferring expired victims, the clock is injected so the TTL is testable, and Get mutates — stated before anyone asks. On the follow-up, gives three cleanup strategies with the condition under which each wins, rather than one with enthusiasm.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Let me start with what has to be O(1), because that decides the structures rather than the other way round.
Eviction has to find the least-recently-used entry instantly. A dictionary can find a key instantly but has no order; a list has order but finding a node in it is linear. So I will hold both — a doubly linked list for recency, and a dictionary from key to the list node, not to the value. That is the part that makes it work.
Now the TTL. The requirement says get returns -1 for an expired key, but it does not say the entry has to be gone. So I do not need a timer or an expiry-ordered structure. I store an absolute expiry on the node and check it in get — lazy expiry.
When get finds an expired entry, I unlink it there and then. It is free, because I am already holding the node. Worth flagging that this means get mutates the cache.
Eviction always takes the node before the tail sentinel. I could scan for an already-expired victim instead, which would be nicer, but that is O(capacity) and I would lose the bound.
Two things I would ask before writing: is the TTL per entry or per cache, and does a re-put renew it? Both change the code.
For your follow-up on cleanup — lazy expiry is already correct, so what is left is reclaiming memory. I would add a min-heap of expiries and a bounded background sweep, with a version on each entry so a renewed key leaves a stale heap record that the sweeper can just discard. Get stays exactly as it is.
A timing wheel is the better structure if the TTLs are few and similar. With arbitrary TTLs the heap is the safer default.
Video lesson plan · LinkedIn Coding — LRU Cache with TTL · ~18 min

Build the cache from the O(1) requirement rather than from the name, then show why lazy expiry is correct and what the follow-up is really asking.

1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Given a 1D array: 1 = land 0 = ocean You have a limited amount of material that can convert ocean cells into land. Find the maximum possible island size. Follow-ups: 1. Circular array 2. Obstacles represented by -1 3. Variable ocean depth / conversion cost

2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A row of cells, some land and some ocean. You may convert up to material ocean cells into land. An island is a contiguous run of land. How long is the longest run you can produce.

What we are being asked to compute

One integer: the length of the best achievable run. Not which cells to convert, though the window bounds give you that for free.

Constraints that matter
  • The island must be contiguous. That is what makes this a window problem rather than a counting problem — if gaps were allowed the answer would just be ones + material.
  • You may use fewer than material conversions. The best window is not necessarily the one that spends everything.
  • Conversion cost is uniform in the base problem: one unit per ocean cell. Follow-up 3 breaks exactly that.
  • One pass, O(1) extra space, is achievable and is what the question is looking for.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
material counts cells, each costing 1.If it is a quantity of earth and cells have depths, that is follow-up 3 already and the base answer changes. This is the clarification to ask, especially since the examples that would have settled it are missing.
Cells are only 0 or 1 in the base problem.-1 appears in follow-up 2 and is a different beast: it can never become land at any price.
material may exceed the number of ocean cells.Then the answer is the whole array. The window logic gives that without a special case, which is worth pointing out rather than adding an if.
An empty array returns 0.Not 1, and not an exception. Cheap to state and the first thing a test checks.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Minecells [1,0,0,1,1,0,1], material 14 — convert index 5, giving 3,4,5,6
Minecells [1,1,0,1], material 02 — no conversions, so the longest existing run
Minecells [0,0,0], material 22 — cannot afford all three
Minecells [0,1,0], material 993 — material to spare, answer is the whole array
Minecircular [1,0,0,0,1], material 13 — wrapping, index 4,0 plus one conversion
Mineobstacles [1,0,1,-1,1,0,1,1], material 14 — the -1 splits the array; best is 4..7
Where these came fromEvery example here is mine. The statement said examples were supplied and none arrived. I verified all six against a brute-force check over every window, and the C# is additionally tested against brute force on 3,000 random arrays across all four variants — because on my first pass I got two of my own expected answers wrong, which is exactly the argument for not hand-computing them.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
Rewrite the problem before solving it. "Convert at most k ocean cells to maximise a contiguous run of land" is the same sentence as "find the longest subarray containing at most k zeros". The conversion is a red herring: you never need to decide which cells to convert, because inside any window you would obviously convert all of them. That reframing is the whole insight, and it is worth doing out loud, because it turns a planning problem into a counting one. Now, why two pointers rather than checking every window. The window is valid while it contains at most k zeros, and adding a cell never makes a window valid and removing one never makes it invalid. That monotonicity means once the window at left has failed for a given right, it has failed for every larger right too — so left never needs to move backwards. Each pointer crosses the array once and the whole thing is O(n). Say that monotonicity argument explicitly. Plenty of candidates produce the two-pointer code from memory; the ones who can say why the left pointer is allowed to be forward-only are the ones who can adapt it when follow-up 2 breaks the assumption.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

For every start and every end, count the zeros in that window; if the count is within budget, record the length.

What it costs

O(n³) naively, or O(n²) with a running count as the end extends. O(1) space.

Why it stops workingAt n = 100,000 — a plausible size for a coastline scan — O(n²) is ten billion operations. But the more useful observation is that the O(n²) version is already doing the sliding window, just restarting it from every left position. Noticing that the restart is unnecessary is the step to O(n), and framing it that way makes the optimisation feel derived rather than recalled.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTint[] cells of 0s and 1s, and an integer material
↓
TRANSFORMATION"Convert at most k cells" becomes "tolerate at most k zeros inside the window". Nothing is actually converted.
↓
DATA STRUCTURETwo indices and one counter. No collection at all.
↓
ALGORITHMExtend right, counting zeros. While the count exceeds the budget, advance left, decrementing when it leaves a zero. Record the best length at every step.
↓
OUTPUTThe largest window length seen
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
Two indices, no collectionThe only state the validity test needs is how many zeros are in the window, not which. A queue of zero positions would also work and would be strictly worse — more memory for no new capability.
A single counterUpdated on entry and on exit. This is the piece that generalises: in follow-up 3 it becomes a cost sum, and in 76 Minimum Window Substring it becomes a multiset. The shape of the loop does not change.
while, not if, on the shrinkWith uniform costs one step is always enough, so if happens to work. With weighted costs it is not, and a while that was correct all along is better than one you have to remember to change.
The algorithm
  1. Set best = 0, ocean = 0, left = 0.
  2. For each right: if cells[right] == 0, increment ocean.
  3. While ocean > material: if cells[left] == 0, decrement ocean; then left++.
  4. Set best = max(best, right - left + 1).
  5. Return best.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

[1,0,0,1,1,0,1] with material = 1. The interesting moment is right = 2, where the window becomes unaffordable and has to shrink.

rightcellocean countwindowbest
010[0,0] = 11
101[0,1] = 1,02
202 → over budgetshrink: left 0→1 (leaves a 1), left 1→2 (leaves a 0, count 1)2
311[2,3] = 0,12
411[2,4] = 0,1,13
502 → over budgetshrink: left 2→3 (leaves a 0, count 1)3
611[3,6] = 1,1,0,14
What the state tells youNote what happened at right = 2: the shrink loop ran twice, because the first step removed a land cell and did not help. That is why the shrink is a while and not an if. Also note that best never decreased — the window shrinking does not lose a previously recorded answer, which is why one pass suffices.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The base problem and all three follow-ups 84 lines
Q03_Island.cs
// Q3 — artificial island: longest run of land after converting at most `material` ocean cells. public static class ArtificialIsland { public static int MaxIsland(int[] cells, int material) { if (cells == null || cells.Length == 0) return 0; int best = 0, ocean = 0, left = 0; for (int right = 0; right < cells.Length; right++) { if (cells[right] == 0) ocean++; while (ocean > material) { // window became unaffordable: shrink from the left if (cells[left] == 0) ocean--; left++; } best = Math.Max(best, right - left + 1); } return best; } // Follow-up 1 — the array wraps. Walk two copies, but never let the window exceed n. public static int MaxIslandCircular(int[] cells, int material) { if (cells == null || cells.Length == 0) return 0; int n = cells.Length; int totalOcean = 0; foreach (int c in cells) if (c == 0) totalOcean++; if (totalOcean <= material) return n; // the whole ring is affordable; guard the wrap-around int best = 0, ocean = 0, left = 0; for (int right = 0; right < 2 * n; right++) { if (cells[right % n] == 0) ocean++; while (ocean > material || right - left + 1 > n) { if (cells[left % n] == 0) ocean--; left++; } best = Math.Max(best, right - left + 1); } return best; } // Follow-up 2 — -1 is an obstacle: it is never land and can never be converted. // So no window may contain one. The array becomes independent segments. public static int MaxIslandWithObstacles(int[] cells, int material) { if (cells == null || cells.Length == 0) return 0; int best = 0, ocean = 0, left = 0; for (int right = 0; right < cells.Length; right++) { if (cells[right] == -1) { // hard wall: restart the window past it left = right + 1; ocean = 0; continue; } if (cells[right] == 0) ocean++; while (ocean > material) { if (cells[left] == 0) ocean--; left++; } best = Math.Max(best, right - left + 1); } return best; } // Follow-up 3 — each ocean cell has its own conversion cost. Costs are non-negative, // so the window sum is still monotonic and the same two pointers work. public static int MaxIslandWeighted(int[] cells, int[] cost, int budget) { if (cells == null || cells.Length == 0) return 0; int best = 0, left = 0; long spent = 0; for (int right = 0; right < cells.Length; right++) { if (cells[right] == 0) spent += cost[right]; while (spent > budget) { if (cells[left] == 0) spent -= cost[left]; left++; } best = Math.Max(best, right - left + 1); } return best; } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
Why the shrink loop is a while

When the window goes over budget, advancing left once may remove a land cell, which does not reduce the zero count. The loop has to keep going until the window is affordable again. An if passes the obvious tests and fails on runs of land before a zero.

Why best is recorded every iteration

Not only when the window grows. After a shrink the window is smaller, and Math.Max keeps the earlier answer. Recording only on growth is a subtle bug that produces the right answer whenever the best window happens to be last.

The circular version: why two copies, and why the cap

Walking 2n cells lets a window wrap the join. Without right - left + 1 > n in the shrink condition, a window could wrap all the way round and count cells twice — reporting an island longer than the array. The early return when the whole ring is affordable is there for the same reason and is not just an optimisation: with material covering every ocean cell, the window would otherwise try to exceed n.

The obstacle version: why the window resets rather than shrinks

A -1 cannot be converted at any price, so no valid window may contain one. That is categorically different from an expensive cell. left = right + 1; ocean = 0; cuts the array in two at that point. Trying to express an obstacle as "infinite cost" and reusing the weighted version would also work, and is worse — it invites overflow and hides the structure.

The weighted version: why the same two pointers still work

Costs are non-negative, so the window cost is still monotonic in the window bounds: growing can only increase it, shrinking can only decrease it. That is the only property the forward-only left pointer relies on. If any cost could be negative the technique collapses entirely and it becomes a prefix-sum problem.

long spent, not int

n cells at a per-cell cost can overflow a 32-bit sum on large inputs. budget stays an int because it is compared, not accumulated. Cheap, and the kind of thing the Staff Coding module explicitly says it looks for.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
MaxIslandO(n)O(1)Each pointer advances at most n times; the shrink is amortised
MaxIslandCircularO(n)O(1)Two passes over n, still linear; no array copy is made
MaxIslandWithObstaclesO(n)O(1)A reset is O(1); the total work is still one pass
MaxIslandWeightedO(n)O(1)Same loop with a sum instead of a count
The part worth saying out loudThe shrink loop looks nested but is not O(n²). left only ever increases, so across the entire run the inner loop executes at most n times in total. That is an amortised argument and it is the one to state, because the code visually contains a loop inside a loop and an interviewer may be waiting to see whether you notice.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Empty arrayReturns 0 in all four variants, checked first.
material = 0Degenerates to "longest existing run of 1s", which the same loop produces with no special case.
material >= the number of ocean cellsReturns n. Falls out of the loop; the circular version needs the explicit early return.
All ocean, material shortReturns exactly material.
All landReturns n with no conversions used.
Single cell [0] with material = 0Returns 0. The window is never valid, so best stays at its initial 0.
Circular, whole ring affordableReturns n, via the early return — without it the window would wrap past n.
Obstacle at index 0 or at the endHandled: the reset sets left past the array end harmlessly, and the loop terminates normally.
All obstaclesReturns 0. No window can contain a -1, so none is ever valid.
Weighted, a cost that overflows intAccumulated in a long.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 5 follow-ups.
“What if the array is circular?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The wrap means the best window might start near the end and finish near the start. The usual trick for circular array problems is to walk two concatenated copies — but that introduces a new way to be wrong, because a window is now allowed to be longer than the array.

Approach

Iterate right from 0 to 2n - 1, indexing with % n. Add one clause to the shrink condition: || right - left + 1 > n, so the window can never exceed the array length. Then handle the degenerate case first — if every ocean cell is affordable the answer is n, and returning early avoids the window trying to wrap onto itself.

Trade-off

Two passes instead of one, still O(n), still O(1) space because no copy is materialised — % n is doing the concatenation. The alternative, actually building a 2n array, is simpler to read and doubles the memory; at this size I would take the modulo.

Circular
Q03_Island.cs
// Follow-up 1 — the array wraps. Walk two copies, but never let the window exceed n. public static int MaxIslandCircular(int[] cells, int material) { if (cells == null || cells.Length == 0) return 0; int n = cells.Length; int totalOcean = 0; foreach (int c in cells) if (c == 0) totalOcean++; if (totalOcean <= material) return n; // the whole ring is affordable; guard the wrap-around int best = 0, ocean = 0, left = 0; for (int right = 0; right < 2 * n; right++) { if (cells[right % n] == 0) ocean++; while (ocean > material || right - left + 1 > n) { if (cells[left % n] == 0) ocean--; left++; } best = Math.Max(best, right - left + 1); } return best; }
TimeSpace
O(n)O(1)
“What if some cells are obstacles, represented by -1?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The question to ask is whether an obstacle is expensive or impossible. "Obstacle" reads as impossible — it can never be land. That is not a cost change, it is a structural change, and it breaks the assumption the sliding window rests on.

Approach

No valid window may contain a -1, so the array becomes a set of independent segments separated by obstacles. On meeting one, reset: left = right + 1, ocean = 0. Each segment is then the original problem, and the shared best across all of them is the answer.

Trade-off

This is the follow-up that most rewards saying what changed conceptually. Modelling -1 as infinite cost and reusing the weighted version gives the right answer and obscures the reason; the reset says "the window cannot cross this" in code.

Obstacles
Q03_Island.cs
// Follow-up 2 — -1 is an obstacle: it is never land and can never be converted. // So no window may contain one. The array becomes independent segments. public static int MaxIslandWithObstacles(int[] cells, int material) { if (cells == null || cells.Length == 0) return 0; int best = 0, ocean = 0, left = 0; for (int right = 0; right < cells.Length; right++) { if (cells[right] == -1) { // hard wall: restart the window past it left = right + 1; ocean = 0; continue; } if (cells[right] == 0) ocean++; while (ocean > material) { if (cells[left] == 0) ocean--; left++; } best = Math.Max(best, right - left + 1); } return best; }
TimeSpace
O(n)O(1)
“What if each ocean cell has its own conversion cost?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The first thing to check is whether the two-pointer argument survives. It rests on the window cost being monotonic in the bounds. Costs are non-negative, so it does — and that is the entire answer.

Approach

Replace the zero count with a cost sum, and compare against the budget instead of against k. The loop is otherwise unchanged. Accumulate in a long.

Trade-off

No cost at all: same time, same space, same shape. Which is the point worth making — the generalisation is free precisely because the original solution was built on the right invariant. If costs could be negative the technique would collapse and this would become a prefix-sum problem with a very different answer.

Weighted
Q03_Island.cs
// Follow-up 3 — each ocean cell has its own conversion cost. Costs are non-negative, // so the window sum is still monotonic and the same two pointers work. public static int MaxIslandWeighted(int[] cells, int[] cost, int budget) { if (cells == null || cells.Length == 0) return 0; int best = 0, left = 0; long spent = 0; for (int right = 0; right < cells.Length; right++) { if (cells[right] == 0) spent += cost[right]; while (spent > budget) { if (cells[left] == 0) spent -= cost[left]; left++; } best = Math.Max(best, right - left + 1); } return best; }
TimeSpace
O(n)O(1)
“Return which cells to convert, not just the length.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The window already contains the answer; nothing new needs computing.

Approach

Track bestLeft and bestRight whenever best is updated. The cells to convert are the zeros in that range. If several windows tie, any of them is correct — say so, and say which one you return (the first, here).

Trade-off

Two extra integers. The only real decision is tie-breaking, and stating that ties exist is better than silently returning one.

TimeSpace
O(n), plus O(window) to list the cellsO(1) for the bounds
“It is 2-D now — a grid, and you can convert k water cells.” · likely pushback
Thinking

This is where it stops being the same problem, and the honest answer says so quickly. This is also the question that 695 Max Area of Island looks like, which is why the name collision is worth knowing about.

Approach

Contiguity in 2-D is connectivity, not an interval, so there is no window to slide. For k = 1 there is a good O(rc) solution: flood-fill to label every island with its size, then for each water cell sum the sizes of its distinct neighbouring labels and add one. For general k it is far harder and I would not claim a clean algorithm.

Trade-off

The k = 1 case is a genuinely nice problem and worth knowing (it is LeetCode 827). For general k I would say plainly that I do not have a polynomial approach in mind rather than improvise one, and ask whether k = 1 is the intended case.

TimeSpace
O(rc) for k = 1O(rc) for the labels
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Reframing — turning "convert cells" into "tolerate zeros", which is the whole problem.
  • Sliding window — and specifically whether you can justify the forward-only left pointer rather than just write it.
  • Amortised analysis — recognising the nested loop is still O(n).
  • Adaptability — three follow-ups that each change one thing: the topology, the feasibility, the cost model. Two of them keep the algorithm; one breaks it.
  • Boundary discipline — the circular cap and the empty array are where this gets implemented wrongly.
A senior engineer might say
Reframes it as "at most k zeros", writes the clean two-pointer loop, and handles the three follow-ups correctly when prompted.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and asks whether material counts cells or quantity before coding — which pre-empts follow-up 3 entirely. States the monotonicity that licenses the forward left pointer, states that the nested loop is amortised O(n), and on the obstacle follow-up says explicitly that this one breaks the window assumption while the other two do not. That distinction is the staff-level signal in this question.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
First, let me restate it. I have a row of land and ocean, I can convert up to k ocean cells, and I want the longest contiguous run of land. Inside any window I would obviously convert every ocean cell in it, so I never have to decide which ones.
Which means this is: find the longest subarray containing at most k zeros.
One clarification before I write anything — is the material a count of cells, each costing one, or a quantity where cells have different depths? Those are different problems, and I think your third follow-up is the second one.
Brute force is every window, O(n²) with a running count. But that version is already doing a sliding window and just restarting it from each left position, and the restart is what is unnecessary.
The reason two pointers work: adding a cell never makes the window valid, and removing one never makes it invalid. So once a window has failed at this left, it fails for every larger right. The left pointer never goes backwards, and both pointers cross the array once — O(n), O(1) space.
The shrink is a while loop, not an if, because advancing left might remove a land cell and not help. And I record the best length every iteration, not only when the window grows.
That nested loop is still O(n) overall, because left only ever increases.
For your follow-ups — circular means walking two copies with a modulo, plus one guard so the window cannot exceed the array length. Variable cost means the counter becomes a sum, and nothing else changes, because costs are non-negative so the monotonicity still holds.
The obstacle one is different from the other two. A -1 can never be land at any price, so no valid window may contain one. That breaks the sliding window rather than reweighting it — the array becomes independent segments, and I reset the window at each obstacle.
Video lesson plan · LinkedIn Coding — Artificial Island (Sliding Window) · ~16 min

Derive the window from the reframing, prove the left pointer is forward-only, then use the three follow-ups to show which changes keep the technique and which break it.

1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
LinkedIn has users connected to other users. Given: interface Member { int getMemberId(); List<Member> getConnections(); } Find the shortest connection distance between two members. Example: Bob -> Alice -> Frank Distance(Bob, Frank) = 2
Follow-up, as suppliedWhat if one member has a very large connection size while the other has a small connection size?
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Supplied with the interface and a worked example that pins down the units: Bob to Frank through Alice is 2, so distance counts edges, not people. That single example removes the off-by-one risk, which is the main thing that goes wrong here.
Reported elsewhere
"Minimum degree of connection between two users; follow-up: return the path" — your own list of past LinkedIn questions. Already worked in this workspace as a bidirectional BFS problem.
Reported elsewhere
490 The Maze and celebrity/BFS rounds appear in the Taro and Blind reports, so graph traversal in general is well attested.
LeetCode
160 Intersection of Two Linked Lists is on the supplied list and shares the "two traversals meeting, equalise the cheaper side" instinct.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported and independently first-party reported by you. The highest confidence on the page, alongside Q17.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

The social graph, reachable only through an interface: given a member you can ask for its id and its connections. There is no adjacency matrix, no id-to-member map, no global view. You discover the graph by walking it.

What we are being asked to compute

The number of hops on the shortest path between two members, or a sentinel if they are in different components. The supplied example fixes hops, not people: 2 for Bob→Alice→Frank.

Constraints that matter
  • Connections are unweighted, so BFS is optimal and Dijkstra would be pointless overhead. Say that — reaching for Dijkstra here is a recognisable tell.
  • The graph is undirected in practice (a connection is mutual on LinkedIn) but the interface does not promise it. The algorithm works either way; only the bidirectional version cares.
  • Visited must be tracked by id, not by object reference. Nothing guarantees that the same member arrives as the same instance twice, and Equals is not specified on the interface.
  • The graph is enormous. The follow-up about lopsided connection counts is really about this.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
Ids are unique and stable.They are the identity for the visited set. If two distinct members could share an id the search would silently prune real paths.
GetConnections() may return null or contain nulls.A real interface like this usually does eventually. Guarding costs two lines and turns a crash into a correct answer.
Distance counts edges. Self is 0, a direct connection is 1.Pinned by the supplied example. If they wanted people counted it is +1 everywhere — confirm which, because "degree of connection" in the LinkedIn product means edges.
GetConnections() is cheap and local.It is not, in reality — it is a service call. That assumption is exactly what the distributed follow-up removes, and naming it early sets that up.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
GivenBob – Alice – FrankDistance(Bob, Frank) = 2
MineBob – Alice1 — a direct connection
MineDistance(Bob, Bob)0 — the same member
MineBob and Zoe, no path-1 — different components
Minea hub with 5,000 connections, and a member with 12, found by expanding the small side first
Where these came fromThe first is supplied and is the one that matters, because it fixes the units. The rest are mine. The lopsided case is in the test suite specifically because it is the supplied follow-up, and the two implementations are additionally cross-checked against each other on 120 random pairs — a bidirectional BFS that disagrees with plain BFS is the single most common way to get this wrong.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
The first observation is that there is nothing to invent: shortest path on an unweighted graph is BFS, and BFS is optimal here. Saying that quickly and moving on is the right use of the first thirty seconds. The second observation is the one the question is actually built around, and it is in the follow-up. The cost of a BFS round is the size of the frontier you expand, and if you search from both ends you get to choose which frontier that is. A single BFS to depth d touches roughly b^d nodes, where b is the branching factor. Two searches meeting in the middle touch about 2 · b^(d/2). At LinkedIn’s average connection count that is not a constant factor — at b = 500 and d = 4 it is 62 billion versus half a million. Now the lopsided part. If one member has 30,000 connections and the other has 40, expanding the hub first costs 30,000 node visits to advance one level; expanding the small side costs 40. They meet at the same place either way. So the rule is: each round, expand whichever frontier is currently smaller. Not "start from the smaller one" — re-decide every round, because the small side may become the large one after it touches a hub. That is the answer to the supplied follow-up, and it is one if plus a swap.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Depth-first search from the source, recording the length of every path that reaches the target, then take the minimum.

What it costs

Exponential in the worst case. Even with a visited set, DFS finds a path and it is not necessarily the shortest, so pruning by visited is unsound.

Why it stops workingThis is the mistake worth naming explicitly, because it is a correctness error and not a performance one. DFS with a visited set returns the first path it stumbles onto, which can be arbitrarily longer than the shortest. To make DFS correct you must allow revisiting nodes at shorter depths, and then you are exploring exponentially many paths. BFS avoids both problems at once: it visits by increasing distance, so the first time it reaches a node is the shortest way to reach it, which is what licenses the visited set.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTTwo IMember references. No global view of the graph.
↓
TRANSFORMATIONNothing is precomputed. Neighbours come from GetConnections() on demand.
↓
DATA STRUCTUREQueue<IMember> for the frontier and HashSet<int> for visited ids. For the bidirectional version, two frontiers as Dictionary<int, IMember> and two visited sets.
↓
ALGORITHMLevel-by-level BFS, incrementing depth once per level. Bidirectional: expand the smaller frontier each round and stop when a node is in the other side’s visited set.
↓
OUTPUTHops, or -1 if unreachable
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
HashSet<int> keyed by idO(1) membership, and keyed on the only identity the interface guarantees. Using the member object would depend on Equals and GetHashCode that the interface never promises.
Level-by-level, not a queue of (node, depth) pairsBoth are correct. Levelling is less state and makes the "distance is edges" convention visible in the code — depth++ happens exactly once per level, so there is one place to check the off-by-one.
Dictionary<int, IMember> for the bidirectional frontiersA frontier must be countable (to decide which side to expand) and membership-testable (to detect the meeting). A dictionary does both; a queue does neither.
Two separate visited setsThe meeting test is "is this id in the other side’s visited set". One shared set would make the two searches prune each other and report the wrong distance.
The algorithm
  1. Handle the trivial cases: either null → -1; same id → 0.
  2. Seed the frontier with the source and mark its id visited.
  3. Take the whole current level. Increment depth.
  4. For each node in the level, for each connection: if it is the target, return depth.
  5. Otherwise, if the id is new, mark it and add it to the next level.
  6. Repeat until the queue empties, then return -1.
  7. Bidirectional: maintain two frontiers; each round swap so frontA is the smaller, expand it, and return depth as soon as a discovered id is in seenB.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

Bidirectional search between tiny and hub, where hub has 5,000 connections and tiny has 1. The point is which side does the work.

RoundFrontier A (tiny)Frontier B (hub)ExpandedResult
seed{tiny}{hub}—sizes 1 and 1
1{tiny}{hub}A, size 1 — one node visitedreaches a mid member; not in seenB yet
1 (cont){mid}{hub}depth = 1
2{mid} size 1{hub} size 1A again, still smallermid’s connections include hub
2 (cont)hub is in seenBreturn 2
(compare)plain BFS from hub firstwould visit all 5,000 to advance one level
What the state tells youTwo node expansions instead of five thousand, and the same answer. Note that the choice was re-made in round 2 rather than fixed at the start — with a deeper path, A would eventually grow past B and the algorithm would start expanding B instead. That is the behaviour the follow-up is asking for.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The interface, as given, in C# 5 lines
Q05_Distance.cs
// Q5 — shortest connection distance between two members. public interface IMember { int GetMemberId(); IList<IMember> GetConnections(); }
Plain BFS and the bidirectional version 65 lines
Q05_Distance.cs
public static class ConnectionDistance { // Plain BFS from one side. Distance is counted in edges (hops). public static int Distance(IMember from, IMember to) { if (from == null || to == null) return -1; if (from.GetMemberId() == to.GetMemberId()) return 0; var seen = new HashSet<int> { from.GetMemberId() }; var queue = new Queue<IMember>(); queue.Enqueue(from); int depth = 0; while (queue.Count > 0) { int levelSize = queue.Count; depth++; // everything dequeued below is `depth` hops out for (int i = 0; i < levelSize; i++) { IMember cur = queue.Dequeue(); foreach (IMember next in cur.GetConnections() ?? new List<IMember>()) { if (next == null) continue; int id = next.GetMemberId(); if (id == to.GetMemberId()) return depth; if (seen.Add(id)) queue.Enqueue(next); } } } return -1; // different components } // Bidirectional BFS. Each round expands whichever frontier is smaller, which is the // whole point when one member has 30 000 connections and the other has 40. public static int DistanceBidirectional(IMember from, IMember to) { if (from == null || to == null) return -1; if (from.GetMemberId() == to.GetMemberId()) return 0; var frontA = new Dictionary<int, IMember> { [from.GetMemberId()] = from }; var frontB = new Dictionary<int, IMember> { [to.GetMemberId()] = to }; var seenA = new HashSet<int> { from.GetMemberId() }; var seenB = new HashSet<int> { to.GetMemberId() }; int depth = 0; while (frontA.Count > 0 && frontB.Count > 0) { // Expand the cheaper side. Cost of a round is the size of the frontier. if (frontA.Count > frontB.Count) { (frontA, frontB) = (frontB, frontA); (seenA, seenB) = (seenB, seenA); } var next = new Dictionary<int, IMember>(); depth++; foreach (IMember cur in frontA.Values) { foreach (IMember nb in cur.GetConnections() ?? new List<IMember>()) { if (nb == null) continue; int id = nb.GetMemberId(); if (seenB.Contains(id)) return depth; // the two searches met if (seenA.Add(id)) next[id] = nb; } } frontA = next; } return -1; } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
int levelSize = queue.Count; before the inner loop

Captured before the loop, because the loop enqueues into the same queue. Reading queue.Count inside the condition would consume the next level in the same round and return a distance that is too small. This is the classic level-BFS bug and it produces a plausible wrong number rather than a crash.

Why the target check is on discovery, not on dequeue

Checking next == to as soon as the edge is seen returns depth one round earlier than waiting to dequeue it. Both are correct if the bookkeeping matches; checking on discovery means depth has already been incremented for this level, which is exactly the number of edges traversed. Checking on dequeue with the same depth++ placement would be off by one.

The tuple swap in the bidirectional version

(frontA, frontB) = (frontB, frontA); together with the visited sets. Swapping the frontiers without swapping the visited sets would test membership against the wrong side and silently return nonsense — the two lines must move together, which is why they are adjacent.

Why depth++ is right despite the swapping

Each round advances exactly one edge, whichever side was expanded. The depth is the total number of edges laid down by both searches combined, and the meeting point is the last edge. This is the part I would not trust on inspection, which is why the test suite cross-checks both implementations on 120 random pairs.

seen.Add(id) as the test

HashSet.Add returns false if the element was present, so the membership test and the insertion are one operation. Two calls would be correct and would give a second place for the two to disagree.

Nulls, twice

cur.GetConnections() ?? new List<IMember>() and if (nb == null) continue;. The interface promises neither, and a graph served from a real backend will eventually hand you both.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
Distance (BFS)O(V + E) worst case, O(b^d) typicalO(V)Every node enqueued once, every edge examined once
DistanceBidirectionalO(b^(d/2)) typicalO(b^(d/2))Two shallow searches instead of one deep one
lopsided caseO(small^d) rather than O(large^d)sameExpanding the smaller frontier each round
The part worth saying out loudThe bidirectional win is not a constant factor. With branching factor b and distance d, one search touches b^d and two touch 2b^(d/2). At b = 500, d = 4: about 62 billion against about 500,000. That is the number to have ready, because "it is about twice as fast" is the wrong intuition and a common one. The space also drops, which matters more here than the time — a single BFS frontier at depth 3 in a social graph does not fit in memory.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Either member nullReturns -1 rather than throwing. Debatable; state which you chose.
Same member0, handled before the search starts.
Direct connection1. Worth testing explicitly — it is the off-by-one case.
No path-1 in both implementations. The bidirectional version must exit when either frontier empties, not both.
A cycleHandled by the visited set. Without it, BFS on a social graph does not terminate.
GetConnections() returns nullTreated as empty.
A self-connectionThe id is already in seen, so it is skipped.
One member with 30,000 connectionsThe supplied follow-up. Bidirectional expands the other side and never enumerates the hub.
Both members are hubsBidirectional still helps, because the depth halves even when neither side is small.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 5 follow-ups.
“What if one member has a very large connection size while the other has a small connection size?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The cost of a round is the size of the frontier being expanded. With two searches, that is a choice. So the answer is not "start from the small one" — it is "re-choose every round", because a small frontier that touches a hub becomes the large one.

Approach

Bidirectional BFS, expanding whichever frontier is currently smaller each round. Keep two visited sets and stop when a newly discovered id appears in the other side’s set. One if and a swap on top of plain BFS.

Trade-off

Two frontiers and two visited sets instead of one, and the code is genuinely harder to get right — the depth accounting and the swap are both easy to break, and the failure is a wrong number rather than a crash. In exchange you go from b^d to b^(d/2), which is the difference between fitting in memory and not. It also requires the graph to be traversable backwards; on LinkedIn connections are mutual so that is free, but on a directed graph like follows you would need a reverse index.

Bidirectional
Q05_Distance.cs
// Bidirectional BFS. Each round expands whichever frontier is smaller, which is the // whole point when one member has 30 000 connections and the other has 40. public static int DistanceBidirectional(IMember from, IMember to) { if (from == null || to == null) return -1; if (from.GetMemberId() == to.GetMemberId()) return 0; var frontA = new Dictionary<int, IMember> { [from.GetMemberId()] = from }; var frontB = new Dictionary<int, IMember> { [to.GetMemberId()] = to }; var seenA = new HashSet<int> { from.GetMemberId() }; var seenB = new HashSet<int> { to.GetMemberId() }; int depth = 0; while (frontA.Count > 0 && frontB.Count > 0) { // Expand the cheaper side. Cost of a round is the size of the frontier. if (frontA.Count > frontB.Count) { (frontA, frontB) = (frontB, frontA); (seenA, seenB) = (seenB, seenA); } var next = new Dictionary<int, IMember>(); depth++; foreach (IMember cur in frontA.Values) { foreach (IMember nb in cur.GetConnections() ?? new List<IMember>()) { if (nb == null) continue; int id = nb.GetMemberId(); if (seenB.Contains(id)) return depth; // the two searches met if (seenA.Add(id)) next[id] = nb; } } frontA = next; } return -1; }
TimeSpace
O(b^(d/2)) typicalO(b^(d/2))
“Return the path, not just the distance.” · supplied with the question
Thinking

Also on your own list, so worth having ready. BFS already knows the path — it just is not recording it.

Approach

Keep a Dictionary<int, IMember> parent alongside the visited set and walk it back from the target. For the bidirectional version you need two parent maps and must join them at the meeting node, reversing one side — and that is the part that gets fumbled, because the meeting node belongs to both.

Trade-off

O(V) extra memory for the parent map, which in a social graph is the same order as the visited set you already have, so it is nearly free. Storing the full path per frontier node instead of a parent pointer is the tempting version and multiplies memory by the path length.

TimeSpace
O(b^(d/2)) plus O(d) to reconstructO(V) for the parent maps
“Cap it at 3 degrees, which is what the product actually shows.” · likely pushback
Thinking

A cap changes the complexity class of the answer, and it is what makes the real feature possible. Worth raising unprompted.

Approach

Stop expanding past depth 3 and return "3+" or "out of network". With bidirectional search each side only ever goes to depth 2, which bounds the work hard regardless of hubs.

Trade-off

This is the one that turns an intractable graph query into a serveable one. It is also a product decision disguised as an optimisation — LinkedIn shows 1st, 2nd, 3rd and "out of network" precisely because the fourth degree is both unhelpful to a user and unaffordable to compute. Saying that connection out loud is a strong signal.

TimeSpace
O(b^1.5) with the cap and bidirectional searchbounded by the depth-2 frontier
“The graph does not fit on one machine. How is this actually served?” · likely pushback
Thinking

The interface hid a service call behind GetConnections(), and the moment that is remote, per-node traversal is the wrong shape entirely — the cost is round trips, not comparisons.

Approach

Two things. Batch the frontier: one request per level, asking for the connections of every node in the frontier at once, rather than one request per node. That turns d round trips out of b^d. And partition the graph by member id with the adjacency list co-located, so a level expansion is a scatter-gather across partitions. For first-degree and second-degree, precompute. Second-degree sets are large but bounded and change slowly, so a bloom filter or a compressed set per member answers "are we within 2" without any traversal at all — which is what makes the badge on a profile page instant.

Trade-off

Batching is a pure win and is the first thing to do. Precomputation trades storage and staleness for latency: a bloom filter gives false positives, so "2nd degree" might occasionally be wrong in the optimistic direction, which for a badge is an acceptable error and for an access-control decision would not be.

TimeSpace
O(d) round trips instead of O(b^d)O(frontier) per request
“Why not Dijkstra, or A*?” · likely pushback
Thinking

Being able to say why not is better than listing algorithms you know.

Approach

Dijkstra solves weighted shortest path. All edges here have weight 1, and on a unit-weight graph Dijkstra degenerates into BFS with a priority queue bolted on — same answer, worse constant, O(log V) per operation for nothing. A* needs an admissible heuristic estimating distance-to-target, and in a social graph there is no coordinate to estimate from.

Trade-off

If connections were weighted — interaction strength, say — Dijkstra becomes correct and BFS becomes wrong. That is the condition to state, rather than dismissing Dijkstra outright.

TimeSpace
O((V + E) log V) for Dijkstra, worse than BFS hereO(V)
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Recognising unweighted shortest path — and saying BFS and why, quickly.
  • Knowing why DFS is wrong here, as a correctness issue and not a speed one.
  • Bidirectional search — the supplied follow-up, including re-choosing the side each round rather than once.
  • Interface discipline — visited by id, nulls guarded, no assumptions the interface does not make.
  • Systems awareness — GetConnections() is a service call, so batching per level is the real optimisation.
  • Product sense — the 3-degree cap is why the feature is possible at all.
A senior engineer might say
Writes a correct level-by-level BFS with a visited set keyed by id, gets the off-by-one right, and produces bidirectional BFS when prompted.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, then: states that a round costs the size of the frontier and therefore re-chooses which side to expand every round; gives the b^d versus 2b^(d/2) numbers rather than saying "faster"; notes that bidirectional needs the graph to be traversable backwards and that connections being mutual is what makes it free; raises the 3-degree cap unprompted as the reason the real feature is serveable; and points out that the interface is hiding a network call, so batching per level matters more than any of the algorithmic work.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Unweighted shortest path, so BFS. Dijkstra would give the same answer with a priority queue I do not need.
Worth saying why not DFS, because it is a correctness problem rather than a speed one. DFS with a visited set returns the first path it finds, which can be much longer than the shortest. BFS visits in order of increasing distance, so the first time it reaches a node is the shortest way to reach it, and that is what makes the visited set sound.
I will track visited by member id, not by object, because the interface does not promise Equals or that the same member arrives as the same instance.
From your example, Bob to Frank through Alice is 2, so distance is edges, not people. I will confirm that: self is 0, a direct connection is 1.
Now your follow-up about lopsided connection counts — that is the interesting part. The cost of one BFS round is the size of the frontier I expand. If I search from both ends, I get to choose which frontier that is. So each round I expand whichever side is currently smaller.
Note: each round, not once at the start. A small frontier that touches a hub becomes the large one, and then I should switch.
The win is not a constant factor. One search touches b to the d, two touch 2 times b to the d over 2. At 500 connections and distance 4 that is 62 billion against half a million.
One caveat: bidirectional needs to walk the graph backwards. On LinkedIn connections are mutual so that is free. On something directed like follows, I would need a reverse index.
If I can raise one thing unprompted — the real product only shows up to 3 degrees, and that cap is what makes this serveable at all. And GetConnections is really a service call, so batching one request per level rather than per node matters more than any of the algorithmic work here.

Matched parentheses
