20. Valid ParenthesesEasyClosest public twin — not in the supplied list
1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Given a string containing alphanumeric characters and parentheses, determine whether the parentheses are correctly matched. Examples: ()()()() → true ((45+)*a3) → true
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Two examples supplied, and the second one contradicts the first sentence — see below. That is the most interesting thing in this question.
Reported elsewhere
Valid Parentheses is on your own list, tagged by LinkedIn in the six-month window.
Reported elsewhere
A 2025 report describes a Valid Parentheses variant with integers and special characters rather than brackets alone. That is an independent description of this same mixed-content framing.
LeetCode
20 Valid Parentheses is the multi-bracket version. This question is easier than LeetCode 20, because it has only one bracket type — which changes the right answer, and most candidates do not notice.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported, independently reported twice, and first-party on your own list. The best-attested question on the page. Also the easiest, which is exactly why it is worth being flawless on.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickThe first sentence says the string contains "alphanumeric characters and parentheses". The second example is ((45+)a3), which contains + and — neither of which is alphanumeric. So either the description is loose and it means "parentheses plus other stuff", or the examples are illustrative of a wider input. The safe implementation treats every non-parenthesis character as irrelevant, which satisfies both readings. Say that you noticed and that you chose the permissive reading; the alternative — validating the character set and rejecting + — would fail the supplied example.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

Scan a string and decide whether its round brackets nest correctly. Everything that is not a bracket is noise.

What we are being asked to compute

One boolean. Not the position of the error, not the depth — though both are natural follow-ups and nearly free.

Constraints that matter
  • One bracket type only. That is the constraint that changes the answer, and it is easy to miss because the problem looks like LeetCode 20.
  • Two failure modes, and they are different: a ) arriving with nothing open, and the string ending with something still open.
  • Non-bracket characters never affect validity.
  • A single pass, and with one bracket type, O(1) space.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
Only ( and ) are brackets; [ and { are ordinary characters.If square and curly brackets must also match, the counter is wrong — ([)] has balanced counts and is invalid. That needs the stack. This is the clarification worth asking.
Every non-bracket character is ignorable, including + and *.Forced by the second supplied example. Validating the character set instead would reject it.
An empty string is valid.Vacuously true. Cheap to state and the first thing a test checks.
Null is valid rather than an exception.Debatable. Whichever you choose, say it — silently dereferencing null is the actual error.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Given()()()()true
Given((45+)*a3)true — and it contains + and *, which are not alphanumeric
Minetrue — vacuously
Mineabc123true — no brackets at all
Mine)(false — a close arrives with nothing open
Mine(()false — ends with one still open
Mine([)]true under the stated single-type rule — see the note
Where these came fromThe last one is the case that decides which implementation is right. With only round brackets in scope, ([)] is valid — the round brackets nest correctly and the square ones are ordinary characters. If the interviewer expects false, they want multiple bracket types and the counter is the wrong answer. That is a thirty-second clarification that changes the code.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
The reflex answer to any bracket question is "use a stack", and here the more useful observation is why you do not need one. A stack exists to remember what was opened, so that a closing bracket can be matched against the right kind. With exactly one bracket type there is nothing to remember: every ( is identical to every other (, so a ) can only ever be matched against "the most recent open one", and all you need to know is how many are open. A single integer replaces the stack, and the space goes from O(n) to O(1). Two conditions make it correct, and they are genuinely different. The counter must never go negative during the scan — that catches )(, which has a final count of zero and is invalid. And it must be exactly zero at the end — that catches ((). Checking only the final value accepts )(; checking only for negatives accepts ((). Both are needed, and being able to name the string each one catches is the whole of this question. Then say where it stops working. The moment a second bracket type exists, counting fails, because ([)] has balanced counts of both and is still wrong. Order now matters, and the stack is what records order. Knowing which constraint forces the stack is worth more than reaching for it by default.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Repeatedly scan the string deleting every adjacent () pair, until a pass makes no change. Valid if the remaining string has no brackets.

What it costs

O(n²) time — each pass is O(n) and there can be n/2 passes on a nested string — and O(n) space for the rewritten string.

Why it stops workingIt is correct, which is worth noting, and it is a reasonable first thing to say. It is quadratic on exactly the input you would use to test it, ((((())))). The single-pass version is not an optimisation of it so much as the observation that the repeated deletion is only ever tracking a depth, and the depth can be tracked directly.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTA string of mixed characters
↓
TRANSFORMATIONIgnore everything that is not ( or )
↓
DATA STRUCTUREOne integer. For the multi-type case, a Stack<char>.
↓
ALGORITHMIncrement on (, decrement on ); fail on a negative count; require zero at the end
↓
OUTPUTA boolean
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
A single counter, not a stackWith one bracket type the identity of the open bracket carries no information, so only the count does. O(1) space instead of O(n), and the early-exit check is one comparison.
Stack<char>, only for the multi-type versionOnce there are several types, order matters, and the stack is the structure that records order. Introducing it before that is over-engineering; knowing exactly when it becomes necessary is the point.
A Dictionary<char, char> of closer → opener in the multi-type versionRather than a switch. It makes adding a bracket type a data change instead of a code change, which is the kind of thing the Staff Coding module explicitly says it scores under extensibility.
The algorithm
  1. Null or empty → true.
  2. Walk the string. On (, increment.
  3. On ), decrement; if the count is now negative, return false immediately.
  4. Ignore every other character.
  5. At the end, return whether the count is exactly zero.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

((45+)*a3) — the supplied example with the non-alphanumeric characters in it.

CharActionOpen countNote
(increment1
(increment2
4 5 +ignored2+ is not alphanumeric and is ignored anyway
)decrement1still non-negative
*ignored1
a 3ignored1
)decrement0
endcheck0true
What the state tells youCompare with )(: the first character takes the count to -1 and the scan returns false there, even though the final count would have been 0. And (() ends at 1, which the final check catches. Two different tests, two different strings — that pairing is what the question is looking for.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The counter version — the right answer for one bracket type 36 lines
Q06_Parens.cs
// Q6 — are the parentheses in a mixed string correctly matched? public static class ParenCheck { // One bracket type, so a signed counter is enough: no stack, O(1) space. // Every other character is irrelevant and is skipped. public static bool IsBalanced(string s) { if (string.IsNullOrEmpty(s)) return true; int open = 0; foreach (char c in s) { if (c == '(') open++; else if (c == ')') { open--; if (open < 0) return false; // a close with nothing open: fail immediately } } return open == 0; // anything left open at the end is unmatched } // The moment there is more than one bracket type, the counter stops working: // "([)]" has balanced counts and is still wrong. That needs the stack. public static bool IsBalancedMultiType(string s) { if (string.IsNullOrEmpty(s)) return true; var partner = new Dictionary<char, char> { [')'] = '(', [']'] = '[', ['}'] = '{' }; var stack = new Stack<char>(); foreach (char c in s) { if (c == '(' || c == '[' || c == '{') stack.Push(c); else if (partner.TryGetValue(c, out char want)) { if (stack.Count == 0 || stack.Pop() != want) return false; } } return stack.Count == 0; } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
Why the negative check is inside the loop

Moving it after the loop would accept )(, whose final count is zero. The invariant is "the count is never negative at any prefix", and an invariant about every prefix has to be checked at every prefix. Returning early is not an optimisation here; it is the correctness condition.

Why the final check is == 0 and not <= 0

The count cannot be negative at the end, because the loop would have returned. So <= 0 would be equivalent and misleading — it suggests a case that cannot happen. == 0 states the intent: everything opened was closed.

The else if, and what falls through

if ( ... ) else if ( ... ) with no final else. Every other character does nothing, which is the permissive reading of "alphanumeric characters and parentheses" and the only reading that accepts the supplied example.

Why IsBalancedMultiType is kept alongside it

Not as the answer, but to make the boundary concrete. It differs in one respect that matters: a closing bracket is checked against the specific opener on top of the stack, so ([)] fails. Having both in front of you is the fastest way to answer "what if there were three bracket types" — and the test suite asserts that the counter says true to ([)] while the stack says false, so the difference is documented rather than described.

stack.Count == 0 at the end of the multi-type version

Same second failure mode as the counter’s == 0. Both versions need two checks; the stack version just spells the first one as stack.Count == 0 || stack.Pop() != want.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
IsBalancedO(n)O(1)One pass, one integer
IsBalancedMultiTypeO(n)O(n)The stack can hold every character on ((((
The part worth saying out loudThe space difference is the whole point of the question. O(1) versus O(n) is not a micro-optimisation here — it follows from a property of the input, and noticing that property is what is being tested. It also matters for the streaming follow-up: a counter can validate an arbitrarily long stream in constant memory, and a stack cannot.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Empty stringtrue.
Nulltrue as written. State the choice.
No brackets at alltrue — the count never moves.
(false — caught by the final check.
)false — caught by the negative check on the first character.
)(false. The case that requires the in-loop check.
(()false. The case that requires the final check.
Deeply nested, 1,000,000 charactersFine. The counter is O(1) space; the stack version would allocate a million entries but still not overflow, since it is iterative.
([)]true under the single-type reading. The case to clarify before coding.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 4 follow-ups.
“Now there are three bracket types.” · likely pushback
Thinking

This is where the counter dies, and being able to state the counterexample immediately is the answer. ([)] has balanced counts of both types and is invalid, so counts are not enough — order is now information.

Approach

A Stack<char>. Push openers. On a closer, fail if the stack is empty or if the top is not the matching opener. Succeed only if the stack is empty at the end. Keep the pairing in a dictionary so a fourth bracket type is a data change.

Trade-off

O(n) space instead of O(1), for the ability to enforce ordering. There is no way around it: any structure that validates nesting of multiple types must remember the sequence of unclosed openers, and in the worst case that is the whole input.

Multiple bracket types
Q06_Parens.cs
// The moment there is more than one bracket type, the counter stops working: // "([)]" has balanced counts and is still wrong. That needs the stack. public static bool IsBalancedMultiType(string s) { if (string.IsNullOrEmpty(s)) return true; var partner = new Dictionary<char, char> { [')'] = '(', [']'] = '[', ['}'] = '{' }; var stack = new Stack<char>(); foreach (char c in s) { if (c == '(' || c == '[' || c == '{') stack.Push(c); else if (partner.TryGetValue(c, out char want)) { if (stack.Count == 0 || stack.Pop() != want) return false; } } return stack.Count == 0; } }
TimeSpace
O(n)O(n)
“The input is a stream and does not fit in memory. Can you still validate it?” · likely pushback
Thinking

The question is what state the validator needs to carry, not how the input arrives.

Approach

For one bracket type, yes, trivially: the counter is the entire state, so an arbitrarily long stream validates in O(1) memory and you can reject the moment it goes negative. For multiple types you need the stack of unclosed openers, which in the worst case is the full stream — so the honest answer is that single-type streams are O(1) and multi-type streams are not.

Trade-off

If bounded memory is a hard requirement with multiple types, you have to bound the nesting depth instead and reject beyond it. That is a real protocol design decision — it is why JSON parsers ship with a maximum depth — and saying so connects the toy question to something real.

TimeSpace
O(n) streamingO(1) single type; O(depth) multi type
“Return the index of the first unmatched bracket.” · likely pushback
Thinking

Both failure modes need an index, and they come from different places, which is the only subtlety.

Approach

For an unexpected closer the index is the current position. For an unclosed opener, the counter cannot tell you where it was — so this follow-up forces a stack even with one bracket type, holding indices rather than characters. Return the top of the stack at the end.

Trade-off

This is the cleanest example of a requirement forcing a structure. Nothing about validity needed the stack; wanting to report the location does. Worth saying explicitly — it shows the structure choice was driven by requirements rather than habit.

TimeSpace
O(n)O(n) — now unavoidable
“What about the `*` wildcard version, where `*` can be an opener, a closer or empty?” · likely pushback
Thinking

That is LeetCode 678 and it is a genuinely different problem. Counting still works, but a single counter does not.

Approach

Track a range of possible open counts: lo and hi. A ( increments both, a ) decrements both, a * decrements lo and increments hi. Clamp lo at zero, fail if hi ever goes negative, and succeed if lo reaches zero at the end.

Trade-off

Still O(n) time and O(1) space, which is surprising and is why the problem is liked. The alternative is two-directional greedy or DP, both heavier. I would not volunteer this unless asked, but recognising it as the interval-of-counts trick rather than a new idea is the useful part.

TimeSpace
O(n)O(1)
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Not reaching for the obvious structure — the question is whether you notice that one bracket type makes the stack unnecessary.
  • Reading the specification against its examples — + and * are not alphanumeric.
  • Naming both failure modes and the string that exposes each.
  • Knowing the boundary — exactly which change forces the stack, with the counterexample ready.
  • Warm-up discipline — this is usually the first fifteen minutes, and fumbling an easy question costs more signal than solving a hard one gains.
A senior engineer might say
Writes the stack version, correctly, quickly. That is a perfectly good answer and it is the most common one.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Notices one bracket type and uses a counter, says why, and states the counterexample ([)] that would force the stack. Raises the + and * discrepancy between the description and the example before writing anything. Names both failure modes with the string each one catches. Then, for the index follow-up, points out that reporting the location forces a stack even though validity did not — which is the clearest demonstration on the whole page of a structure chosen from a requirement.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Quick clarification first: your description says alphanumeric characters and parentheses, but the second example has a plus and a star in it. I will treat anything that is not a round bracket as ignorable, which satisfies both.
And the more important one — is it only round brackets? Because if square and curly brackets also have to match, my answer changes.
Assuming just round brackets: I do not need a stack. A stack remembers what kind of thing was opened, and with one bracket type there is nothing to remember. Every open paren is interchangeable, so all I need is how many are open. That is one integer instead of O(n) space.
Two checks, and they catch different strings. The count must never go negative during the scan — that catches close-paren-then-open, which ends at zero and is invalid. And the count must be exactly zero at the end — that catches open-open-close, which never goes negative.
If I only did the final check I would accept the first one; if I only checked for negatives I would accept the second.
Where this stops working: the moment there are two bracket types. Square-open, round-open, round-close, square-close has balanced counts of both and is invalid. Order becomes information, and the stack is what records order. So the structure is driven by the number of types, not by the problem being about brackets.
If you wanted the index of the first unmatched bracket, that forces a stack even with one type — the counter knows something is open but not where it was. Nice example of a requirement, rather than a habit, choosing the structure.

Sources disagree — preserved, not resolvedLeetCode 127 and this question return different numbers for the same input. LeetCode returns the length of the transformation sequence (the number of words); this returns the number of transformations (the number of edges). For hit → cog LeetCode says 5 and this says 4. Both are correct under their own definition. Do not silently convert one to the other — say which you are returning. The test suite asserts the edge count, and documents the LeetCode difference in the assertion message.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A dictionary is secretly a graph. Two words are neighbours if they differ in exactly one position. You are asked for the shortest path between two of them, walking only through real words.

What we are being asked to compute

The number of single-character changes on the shortest chain, or a sentinel if no chain exists.

Constraints that matter
  • Every intermediate word must be in the dictionary. That is what makes it a graph search rather than a string-distance calculation.
  • All words are the same length in the base problem. The third follow-up removes that.
  • Every step costs 1, so BFS is optimal and Dijkstra is unnecessary.
  • The dictionary can be large — tens of thousands of words — which is why the neighbour function, not the search, is the design problem.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
The start word need not be in the dictionary; the end word must be.LeetCode 127 makes the same choice. If the start must also be present, that is one validation line. If the end need not be, the question is unanswerable — you could never legally arrive.
Case and alphabet are consistent.The example is uppercase. Mixed case would need normalising, and the wildcard index would have to be built on the normalised form or it silently misses neighbours.
The answer counts edges.Fixed by the supplied example: six words, output 5. This is where LeetCode differs, so state it.
The dictionary is static in the base problem.The online-service follow-up makes it dynamic, which is why the index is built behind a method rather than inline in the constructor.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
GivenAPE → APT → OPT → OAT → MAT → MAN5 — six words, five transformations
MineAPE → APT1
MineAPE → APE0
Mineend word not in the dictionary-1
MineAAA → XXX with dictionary {AAA, AAB, XXX}-1 — no chain of real words exists
LeetCodehit → cog, dictionary {hot,dot,dog,lot,log,cog}4 here, 5 on LeetCode 127
Where these came fromThe last row is the one to remember. It is in the test suite with that discrepancy written into the assertion message, because it is the single most likely way to lose marks on a question you otherwise know. The two implementations — plain and bidirectional — are also cross-checked against each other on 200 random pairs over a 250-word generated dictionary.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
The words are not the problem. Finding the neighbours is the problem, and that is the whole question. Once you see that two words are adjacent when they differ in one position, the search is textbook BFS. But how do you find the neighbours of APE? The obvious way is to compare it against every word in the dictionary: O(N · L) per expansion, which at N = 50,000 makes each step of the search a scan of the whole dictionary. The trick is to index by wildcard. For each word, generate the patterns you get by blanking each position in turn — APE gives *PE, A*E, AP* — and store the word under all three. Now two words are neighbours exactly when they share a pattern, so the neighbours of APE are the union of three dictionary lookups. Each expansion drops from O(N · L) to O(L) lookups, and the cost moves to a one-off O(N · L) build. That move — precompute a canonical key so the expensive comparison becomes a hash lookup — is the same idea as Q11’s keypad signature and the same idea behind LeetCode 244. It is worth naming as a pattern rather than as a trick for this problem, because it is what makes the online-service follow-up answerable: the build happens once at startup, and every query is then pure BFS over hash lookups. The second observation is for the follow-up, and it is the same one as Q5: searching from both ends turns b^d into 2 · b^(d/2). Here the branching factor is genuinely large — each word has up to 25 · L neighbours — so bidirectional BFS matters more than it does on a social graph.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

BFS, and for each word in the frontier scan the entire dictionary looking for words that differ in exactly one position.

What it costs

O(N² · L) in the worst case — every one of N words compared against all N, at L character comparisons each. O(N) space.

Why it stops workingAt N = 50,000 and L = 5 that is over ten billion character comparisons for one query, and it repeats the same comparisons on every query. The useful framing is that the brute force recomputes adjacency per query something that is a property of the dictionary — and anything that depends only on the dictionary should be computed once. That reframing is what leads to the wildcard index, and it is also exactly the argument the online-service follow-up wants.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTA dictionary, a start word and an end word
↓
TRANSFORMATIONEvery word is indexed under L wildcard patterns, one per blanked position
↓
DATA STRUCTUREDictionary<string, List<string>> from pattern to words; HashSet<string> for the dictionary and for visited; Queue<string> for the frontier
↓
ALGORITHMLevel-by-level BFS, neighbours via L pattern lookups. Bidirectional: expand the smaller frontier and stop when the two searches meet.
↓
OUTPUTThe number of transformations, or -1
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
Dictionary<string, List<string>> keyed on wildcard patternsTurns neighbour generation from a dictionary scan into L hash lookups. The one-off build cost is paid back on the first query.
HashSet<string> with StringComparer.OrdinalOrdinal comparison is both faster and correct here — culture-aware comparison can treat different strings as equal, which in a word-graph silently merges nodes. This is a real C# footgun and worth one sentence.
Level-by-level BFS rather than (word, depth) pairsLess state, and steps++ happens in exactly one place, which is where the edge-versus-word convention lives.
HashSet frontiers in the bidirectional version, not queuesA frontier has to be countable (to pick the smaller side) and membership-testable (to detect the meeting). A queue is neither.
Index as a separate methodSo AddWord can reuse it. The dynamic-dictionary follow-up is then two lines instead of a redesign.
The algorithm
  1. Build: for each dictionary word, for each position, blank it and append the word to that pattern’s list.
  2. Guard: different lengths → -1; identical words → 0; end word absent → -1.
  3. Seed the frontier with the start word, mark it visited, steps = 0.
  4. Take the whole level. steps++.
  5. For each word, for each of its L patterns, for each word in that bucket: if it is the end word, return steps; if unseen, mark and queue it.
  6. Queue empties → -1.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

The supplied example. Dictionary {APE, APT, OPT, OAT, MAT, MAN, OPE, CAT, COT}, from APE to MAN. Only the words that matter are shown per level.

stepsFrontierPatterns looked upDiscoveredNote
0{APE}——seeded
1{APE}*PE, A*E, AP*OPE, APTboth are one change from APE
2{OPE, APT}*PE/O*E/OP* and *PT/A*T/AP*OPTOPT reachable from both
3{OPT}*PT, O*T, OP*OAT, COT
4{OAT, COT}*AT, O*T, OA* …MAT, CAT
5{MAT, CAT}*AT, M*T, MA*MANMA* contains MAN → return 5
What the state tells youFive levels, five transformations, matching the supplied output. Note the mechanism at the last step: MAT blanked at position 2 gives MA*, and MAN is in that bucket. No comparison against any other word happened — the whole expansion was three hash lookups. That is the wildcard index doing the work.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The service: index once, then BFS per query 101 lines
Q08_WordLadder.cs
// Q8 — minimum single-character transformations from start to end, every step a dictionary word. public class WordTransformer { private readonly Dictionary<string, List<string>> _buckets; // "*PE" -> words matching it private readonly HashSet<string> _words; // Preprocessing once, so each query is pure BFS with O(1) neighbour lookup. public WordTransformer(IEnumerable<string> dictionary) { _words = new HashSet<string>(dictionary, StringComparer.Ordinal); _buckets = new Dictionary<string, List<string>>(StringComparer.Ordinal); foreach (string w in _words) Index(w); } private void Index(string word) { var chars = word.ToCharArray(); for (int i = 0; i < chars.Length; i++) { char keep = chars[i]; chars[i] = '*'; string pattern = new string(chars); if (!_buckets.TryGetValue(pattern, out var list)) _buckets[pattern] = list = new List<string>(); list.Add(word); chars[i] = keep; } } // Returns the number of TRANSFORMATIONS (edges), not the number of words. -1 if impossible. public int MinTransformations(string start, string end) { if (start == null || end == null || start.Length != end.Length) return -1; if (start == end) return 0; if (!_words.Contains(end)) return -1; var seen = new HashSet<string>(StringComparer.Ordinal) { start }; var queue = new Queue<string>(); queue.Enqueue(start); int steps = 0; while (queue.Count > 0) { int levelSize = queue.Count; steps++; for (int i = 0; i < levelSize; i++) { string cur = queue.Dequeue(); foreach (string next in Neighbours(cur)) { if (next == end) return steps; if (seen.Add(next)) queue.Enqueue(next); } } } return -1; } private IEnumerable<string> Neighbours(string word) { var chars = word.ToCharArray(); for (int i = 0; i < chars.Length; i++) { char keep = chars[i]; chars[i] = '*'; if (_buckets.TryGetValue(new string(chars), out var list)) foreach (string w in list) if (w != word) yield return w; chars[i] = keep; } } // Follow-up — bidirectional BFS. Same answer, far fewer nodes touched: two searches of // depth d/2 instead of one of depth d, so b^(d/2) + b^(d/2) instead of b^d. public int MinTransformationsBidirectional(string start, string end) { if (start == null || end == null || start.Length != end.Length) return -1; if (start == end) return 0; if (!_words.Contains(end)) return -1; var frontA = new HashSet<string>(StringComparer.Ordinal) { start }; var frontB = new HashSet<string>(StringComparer.Ordinal) { end }; var seenA = new HashSet<string>(StringComparer.Ordinal) { start }; var seenB = new HashSet<string>(StringComparer.Ordinal) { end }; int steps = 0; while (frontA.Count > 0 && frontB.Count > 0) { if (frontA.Count > frontB.Count) { // always expand the smaller frontier (frontA, frontB) = (frontB, frontA); (seenA, seenB) = (seenB, seenA); } var next = new HashSet<string>(StringComparer.Ordinal); steps++; foreach (string cur in frontA) foreach (string nb in Neighbours(cur)) { if (seenB.Contains(nb)) return steps; if (seenA.Add(nb)) next.Add(nb); } frontA = next; } return -1; } // Follow-up — the dictionary changes while the service is running. public void AddWord(string word) { if (_words.Add(word)) Index(word); } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
Why Index reuses one char[] and restores it

chars[i] = '*' then chars[i] = keep. Allocating a fresh array per position would be L times the garbage for no benefit. new string(chars) still allocates the pattern — unavoidable, since it is the dictionary key — but the buffer is reused. Small, and it is the difference between a build that scales and one that pressures the GC on a large dictionary.

StringComparer.Ordinal, deliberately

Passed to every HashSet and Dictionary. Without it, .NET uses the default comparer, and for strings the culture-sensitive paths can treat distinct sequences as equal. In a word graph that means two different words collapse into one node and the shortest path can come out wrong. It is also measurably faster.

The neighbour function is an iterator

yield return means the BFS stops as soon as it finds the end word, without materialising the rest of the neighbours. On a word with 100 neighbours where the second one is the target, that is 98 fewer set operations.

if (w != word) inside the bucket

A word is in its own wildcard buckets, so it would otherwise be its own neighbour. Harmless (the visited set catches it) and worth excluding anyway, because "a word is one change from itself" is not true and the code should not say it is.

Why the end-word check is on discovery

Returning steps the moment the end word is seen as a neighbour, rather than waiting to dequeue it, is what makes steps the edge count. steps++ has already happened for this level, so it equals the number of edges traversed to reach the neighbour. Moving the check to the dequeue point without moving steps++ gives an answer one too large — which, not coincidentally, is the LeetCode 127 answer.

The bidirectional swap

(frontA, frontB) = (frontB, frontA); together with (seenA, seenB). Both must move, or the meeting test checks the wrong set. steps++ once per round is still correct because each round lays down exactly one edge, whichever side expanded — and that is the part I would not trust by inspection, which is why 200 random pairs are cross-checked against plain BFS.

AddWord is three lines

if (_words.Add(word)) Index(word); — the dynamic-dictionary follow-up, for free, because indexing was factored out. Removal would be harder: it needs the word pulled from L buckets, so the buckets would want to be sets rather than lists.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
buildO(N · L²)O(N · L²)N words × L patterns, each pattern a string of length L
MinTransformationsO(V + E) worst caseO(N)E is bounded by the total bucket sizes; each word is visited once
neighbours of one wordO(L + bucket sizes)O(1) extraL hash lookups instead of a dictionary scan
MinTransformationsBidirectionalO(b^(d/2)) typicalO(b^(d/2))Two shallow searches rather than one deep one
AddWordO(L²)O(L²)L new pattern keys for one word
The part worth saying out loudThe build is O(N · L²), not O(N · L) — each of the L patterns is itself a string of length L that has to be constructed and hashed. At N = 50,000 and L = 5 that is about 1.25 million character operations, which is nothing; at L = 20 it starts to matter, and a rolling hash would be the fix. Getting the L² right rather than saying L is a small piece of precision that is easy to be sloppy about.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Start equals end0, before any search.
Different lengths-1. Single-character substitution cannot change length in the base problem.
End word not in the dictionary-1, checked up front — otherwise the search runs to exhaustion for nothing.
Start word not in the dictionaryAllowed. Its neighbours still come from the buckets, which contain only real words, so the path is legal.
No path exists-1 when the queue empties. The bidirectional version must exit when either frontier empties.
Empty dictionary-1, via the end-word check.
Single-character wordsWorks; every word is one change from every other, so the answer is almost always 1.
Duplicate words in the inputHashSet de-duplicates, so buckets hold each word once.
Mixed caseTreated as distinct under ordinal comparison. Normalise at the boundary if that is wrong.
A very large bucketA pattern like *AT can hold many words. That is the real branching factor, and it is the argument for searching from both ends.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 5 follow-ups.
“Convert this to an online service with minimal latency.” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The question is which work depends on the dictionary and which depends on the query. Anything in the first category should happen once, at startup, not per request. That single split is most of the answer.

Approach

Four things, in order of payoff. Build the wildcard index once at startup, not per request — that is already how the class is written, and it is the whole reason the constructor takes the dictionary. Use bidirectional BFS per query, since it is the single biggest constant reduction available and the branching factor here is large. Cache answers. Word pairs repeat heavily in any real workload, and the result is immutable for a fixed dictionary, so an LRU cache keyed on the unordered pair is a near-free win — which is Q1, and worth pointing out that the two questions compose. Shard by word length. Words of different lengths can never be neighbours, so the graph is already partitioned. Each shard is independent, holds a smaller index, and can be served by a different process with no coordination at all.

Trade-off

Memory for latency, and staleness for speed. The index is roughly L² strings per word, so a 500,000-word dictionary at L = 8 is a real amount of memory and is the reason sharding by length is attractive. The cache introduces an invalidation problem the moment the dictionary changes — and since adding one word can shorten arbitrarily many paths, the honest answer is to version the dictionary and key the cache on the version, so an update invalidates everything at once rather than trying to be clever about which entries changed.

TimeSpace
O(1) on a cache hit; O(b^(d/2)) otherwiseO(N · L²) for the index
“Will cycles be output?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

Two readings, and the useful answer covers both rather than picking one. Either "can the algorithm loop forever" or "can the returned path revisit a word".

Approach

No, to both, and for the same reason. The visited set means each word is enqueued at most once, so the search cannot loop. And BFS explores in order of increasing distance, so the first time it reaches a word is via a shortest path — a path that revisited a word would be strictly longer than the same path with the loop removed, so it can never be the one found. The graph itself is full of cycles, of course. The point is that a shortest path in any graph is always simple, and BFS finds shortest paths.

Trade-off

None here — it is a property of the algorithm, not a design decision. Where it would matter is if you dropped the visited set to enumerate all paths: then cycles become possible and you need per-path tracking rather than a global set, and the cost goes exponential.

TimeSpace
unchangedunchanged
“What if letters can be added or removed?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

This is the follow-up that changes the graph rather than the search, and it is LeetCode 72 arriving through the back door — which is presumably why 72 is on the supplied number list.

Approach

Adjacency becomes "edit distance exactly 1", so words of different lengths are now neighbours and the graph is no longer partitioned by length. BFS still applies; only the neighbour function changes. The wildcard index needs a second half. For substitution, keep the existing A*E-style patterns. For insertion and deletion, index each word under every deletion of one character: APE also goes under PE, AE, AP. Then a word of length L and a word of length L+1 are neighbours exactly when the shorter one equals one of the longer one’s deletion patterns — so deletion neighbours are, again, hash lookups rather than comparisons.

Trade-off

The index roughly doubles, and the branching factor grows a lot, because a word is now adjacent to longer and shorter words as well. Length-based sharding is lost — which is a real cost, since it was the cleanest scaling axis. Bidirectional search becomes more valuable, not less, because b went up. If the question were the distance rather than the dictionary-constrained path, this stops being a graph problem and becomes the O(L¹ · L²) DP of LeetCode 72 — worth distinguishing the two out loud, because they sound the same and are not.

TimeSpace
O(V + E) with a larger Eroughly 2× the index
“Return the path, not just the length.” · likely pushback
Thinking

BFS already knows it; it just is not recording it. Same as Q5.

Approach

A Dictionary<string, string> parent populated when a word is first discovered, walked back from the end word. For the bidirectional version, two parent maps joined at the meeting word, with one side reversed — and the meeting word belongs to both, so it must not be emitted twice.

Trade-off

O(N) extra memory, which is the same order as the visited set. Storing the whole path per frontier entry instead is the tempting shortcut and multiplies memory by the path length. If all shortest paths are wanted — which is LeetCode 126 — the parent becomes a list of parents and the reconstruction becomes a DFS over the resulting DAG, and the output can be exponentially large.

TimeSpace
O(b^(d/2)) plus O(d) to reconstructO(N)
“The dictionary changes while the service is running.” · likely pushback
Thinking

Additions are easy and removals are not, which is worth saying in that order.

Approach

AddWord indexes the new word into its L buckets — already implemented, O(L²). Removal is harder: the word has to be pulled out of L buckets, and with List<string> buckets that is a linear scan of each. If removal matters, make the buckets HashSet<string> so it is O(L) instead. The bigger problem is the cache. One added word can shorten unboundedly many paths, so there is no cheap partial invalidation — version the dictionary and key cached answers on the version.

Trade-off

List buckets are faster to build and to iterate, and slower to delete from. HashSet buckets are the opposite. Choose on the read/write ratio, which for a dictionary is overwhelmingly reads — so List is the right default and switching is the right answer only if removals are real.

TimeSpace
O(L²) add; O(L) removal with set bucketsunchanged
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Modelling — seeing a dictionary as a graph, which is the step everything else depends on.
  • Locating the real cost — the neighbour function, not the search.
  • Precompute-versus-per-query reasoning — the same instinct as Q11 and LeetCode 244, and exactly what the online-service follow-up is testing.
  • Bidirectional BFS — including why the branching factor makes it matter more here than elsewhere.
  • Convention precision — edges versus words, where LeetCode disagrees with the question.
  • Knowing the neighbouring problem — that the add/remove follow-up is edit distance, and that the DP and the BFS answer different questions.
A senior engineer might say
Models it as a graph, builds the wildcard index, writes a correct level-by-level BFS, and produces bidirectional BFS when prompted.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and: states the edge-versus-word convention and that LeetCode 127 differs; gets the build cost right as O(N · L²) rather than O(N · L); answers the online-service follow-up by splitting dictionary-dependent from query-dependent work rather than by listing optimisations; notices that sharding by word length is free in the base problem and that the add/remove follow-up destroys it; recognises the third follow-up as edit distance and distinguishes the dictionary-constrained path from the unconstrained DP; and uses StringComparer.Ordinal for a stated reason. The sharding observation and its loss is the sharpest signal here — it shows the follow-up was evaluated against the earlier design rather than answered in isolation.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Let me restate the structure: a dictionary is a graph where two words are adjacent if they differ in one position. All edges cost 1, so shortest path means BFS.
From your example — six words, output five — the answer counts transformations, not words. Worth pinning down, because LeetCode 127 is the same problem and returns the word count, so the same input gives a different number.
The search is the easy part. The real problem is finding the neighbours of a word. Comparing against every dictionary entry is O(N times L) per expansion, and it redoes on every query something that is a property of the dictionary, not of the query.
So I index by wildcard. APE gets stored under star-P-E, A-star-E and A-P-star. Two words are neighbours exactly when they share a pattern, so neighbours become three hash lookups instead of a scan. The build is O(N times L squared) — L patterns, each a string of length L.
Then BFS level by level, and I return the level count the moment I see the end word as a neighbour.
On your online-service follow-up — the key question is what depends on the dictionary and what depends on the query. The index depends only on the dictionary, so it is built once at startup. Then bidirectional BFS per query, because the branching factor here is large — up to 25 times L neighbours per word — so halving the depth is worth a lot. Then cache by word pair, since the result is immutable for a fixed dictionary. And I would shard by word length, because words of different lengths can never be neighbours, so the graph is already partitioned for free.
On cycles — no, and for one reason. The visited set means each word is enqueued once, so the search cannot loop. And any path that revisited a word would be longer than the same path with the loop cut out, so BFS would never return it. A shortest path is always simple.
On adding and removing letters — that changes the graph, not the search. Adjacency becomes edit distance one, so different-length words are now neighbours. I would index each word under every single-character deletion as well, so PE, AE and AP for APE, and then a shorter word is adjacent to a longer one when it matches one of its deletion patterns.
One consequence worth flagging: that kills the length-based sharding I just proposed, because the graph is no longer partitioned by length. So that follow-up costs me my cleanest scaling axis.
And if you wanted the distance rather than a path through real words, that is not this algorithm at all — that is the edit-distance DP.

Q10

Remove leaf levels

366. Find Leaves of Binary TreeMedium · premiumClosest public twin — not in the supplied list
1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Given an imbalanced binary tree: Print the leaf nodes. Remove those leaves. Print the new leaf nodes. Continue until only the root remains. Follow-up: Solve for an N-ary tree.
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
The wording is procedural — print, remove, repeat — which is a hint about the expected answer rather than about the problem. Taking it literally leads to an O(n·h) solution; noticing that it describes a property leads to O(n).
Reported elsewhere
Find Leaves of Binary Tree (LeetCode 366) — on your own list, first-party, and already worked in the coding page.
Reported elsewhere
Nested List Weight Sum II (LeetCode 364) — on the supplied list twice, and independently reader-reported. Weighting by depth from the bottom is exactly this problem’s recurrence in different clothing.
LeetCode
366 is the exact match. 103 Binary Tree Zigzag is on the supplied list and is the instructive contrast — levels from the top, which BFS gives you and this problem cannot use.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported and independently first-party reported. High confidence, and the cheapest high-value question on the page after Q6.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickThe statement says "continue until only the root remains", which implies the root is never removed — but a root with no children is a leaf, so the first round would print it. Both readings produce the same grouping; they differ only in whether the last round is emitted. I include the root as its own final level, because the sequence of levels is the natural output and stopping one short makes the single-node tree return nothing. Say which you chose. The second, larger question: does the tree actually have to be mutated? "Remove those leaves" sounds like it must. It does not, and that is the point of the question.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

Peel the tree like an onion. Take all the leaves, then all the nodes that became leaves, and so on, until nothing is left.

What we are being asked to compute

A list of lists: the nodes removed in each round, in order. Round 0 is the original leaves.

Constraints that matter
  • The tree is explicitly imbalanced, so the number of rounds is the height, which can be n. That makes a per-round pass O(n²) in the worst case.
  • Rounds are determined by the tree’s shape, not by the order you process nodes.
  • Mutation is optional — and deciding that is the question.
  • Recursion depth equals the height, which for a degenerate tree is n.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
The root is emitted as its own final round.The alternative stops one round early. Same grouping; only the last line differs. A single-node tree is the test that distinguishes them.
Order within a round does not matter.The recursion produces one particular order. If a specific order were required — left-to-right, say — the traversal order would have to be pinned, which the post-order walk happens to give anyway.
Values are not unique.Nothing here depends on uniqueness. If nodes had to be identified rather than valued, the lists would hold nodes instead of ints.
The caller does not need the tree afterwards.If it does, the non-mutating version is not merely faster, it is the only correct one. Ask — this is a cheap clarification with a real consequence.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Tree1 with children 2 and 3; 2 with children 4 and 5
Round 0the leaves[3, 4, 5]
Round 12 is now a leaf[2]
Round 2only the root is left[1]
Mineright-leaning chain 1 → 2 → 3[[3], [2], [1]] — one node per round, h = n
Minea single node[[9]] — the root counts as a leaf
N-ary1 with children 2 and 3; 2 with child 4[[3, 4], [2], [1]]
Where these came fromNo example came with the statement; the tree above is the standard shape for this problem and the rest are mine. The mutating and non-mutating implementations are cross-checked against each other in the test suite, because "I refactored it to avoid mutation and it still agrees" is the only claim worth making here.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
The statement describes a procedure, and the procedure is not the answer. Ask instead: which round does a given node belong to? A node leaves in round 0 if it is a leaf. Otherwise it cannot go until all its children have gone, so it leaves one round after its slowest child. That is round(node) = 1 + max(round(left), round(right)), with round(null) = -1 and that expression is exactly the height of the node measured from the bottom. So the rounds are not something you perform — they are a property every node already has, and one post-order pass computes all of them. Bucket each node by its height as the recursion returns and you have the whole answer in O(n), with the tree untouched. The distinction that makes this click: depth is passed down, height is returned up. A BFS level counter gives you depth from the root, which is why 103 Binary Tree Zigzag is BFS and this is not. Anything asking about the last level, peeling leaves, or weighting by distance from the bottom needs the post-order return value. That is also precisely why 364 Nested List Weight Sum II is harder than 339, its top-down sibling — same problem, same fix. So the answer to "remove those leaves" is: I do not have to. And saying that, rather than doing it, is the whole question.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Follow the statement literally. Walk the tree collecting leaves, then walk it again cutting the links to them, and repeat until empty.

What it costs

O(n) per round × h rounds = O(n · h), which on the imbalanced tree the statement specifies is O(n²). O(h) space.

Why it stops workingIt is correct, and on a balanced tree it is O(n log n), which is fine. The statement says imbalanced, and a degenerate chain of 10,000 nodes makes it 100 million operations for something achievable in 10,000. The deeper objection is that it destroys the input, and nothing in the problem requires that — the caller may well want the tree afterwards.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTThe root of a binary tree
↓
TRANSFORMATIONEach node’s peel round is recognised as its height from the bottom
↓
DATA STRUCTUREList<List<int>>, indexed by height. No queue, no visited set, no mutation.
↓
ALGORITHMOne post-order recursion returning height; append each node to levels[height]
↓
OUTPUTThe list of rounds, and an untouched tree
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
List<List<int>> indexed by heightHeight values arrive in no particular order, but they are dense from 0 upwards, so a list indexed by height is the natural bucket. A dictionary would work and would need sorting at the end.
Post-order, specificallyA node’s height depends on its children, so the children must be resolved first. That is the definition of post-order — the traversal is not a choice here, it is forced by the recurrence.
return -1 for nullSo a leaf returns 0 and becomes round 0. Returning 0 for null would make every leaf round 1 and every list index off by one.
No mutationThe tree is only read. That makes the function safe to call twice, safe to call on a shared tree, and trivially thread-safe for concurrent readers — none of which the literal reading gives you.
The algorithm
  1. Height(null) returns -1.
  2. Recurse into both children first.
  3. h = 1 + max(leftHeight, rightHeight).
  4. If h == levels.Count, this is the first node seen at that height — append a new list.
  5. Add the node’s value to levels[h].
  6. Return h.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

The example tree: root 1, children 2 and 3, and 2 has children 4 and 5. Post-order, so the deepest nodes resolve first.

VisitChildren heightshlevelsNote
4-1, -10[[4]]leaf → round 0; list created
5-1, -10[[4, 5]]also round 0
20, 01[[4, 5], [2]]one round after its slowest child
3-1, -10[[4, 5, 3], [2]]round 0, although it is shallower than 4 and 5
11, 02[[4, 5, 3], [2], [1]]root → final round
What the state tells youNode 3 is the case worth pointing at. It sits at depth 1 while nodes 4 and 5 sit at depth 2, yet all three leave in round 0 — because round is height from the bottom, not depth from the top. Any solution driven by BFS levels gets this wrong, and it is the single fastest way to check whether someone has the right mental model.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
Binary, non-mutating, plus the mutating version for comparison 45 lines
Q10_Leaves.cs
public static class LeafPeeling { // No mutation at all. A node's peel round is 1 + max(round of its children), which is // exactly its height measured from the bottom. One post-order pass buckets every node. public static List<List<int>> PeelLevels(TreeNode root) { var levels = new List<List<int>>(); Height(root, levels); return levels; } private static int Height(TreeNode node, List<List<int>> levels) { if (node == null) return -1; // so a leaf comes out as 0 int h = 1 + Math.Max(Height(node.Left, levels), Height(node.Right, levels)); if (h == levels.Count) levels.Add(new List<int>()); // first node seen at this depth levels[h].Add(node.Value); return h; } // If the interviewer genuinely wants the tree destroyed, do it explicitly: one pass per // round, cutting the child links whose subtree was a leaf. O(n) per round, O(n·h) overall. public static List<List<int>> PeelAndMutate(ref TreeNode root) { var rounds = new List<List<int>>(); while (root != null) { var picked = new List<int>(); root = Strip(root, picked); rounds.Add(picked); } return rounds; } private static TreeNode Strip(TreeNode node, List<int> picked) { if (node == null) return null; if (node.Left == null && node.Right == null) { picked.Add(node.Value); return null; // the parent's link is cut here } node.Left = Strip(node.Left, picked); node.Right = Strip(node.Right, picked); return node; } }
The N-ary follow-up — the recurrence does not change 20 lines
Q10_Leaves.cs
public static class NaryLeafPeeling { public static List<List<int>> PeelLevels(NaryNode root) { var levels = new List<List<int>>(); Height(root, levels); return levels; } private static int Height(NaryNode node, List<List<int>> levels) { if (node == null) return -1; int h = -1; foreach (NaryNode child in node.Children) h = Math.Max(h, Height(child, levels)); h += 1; if (h == levels.Count) levels.Add(new List<int>()); levels[h].Add(node.Value); return h; } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
if (node == null) return -1;

The base case that makes the indices line up. A leaf has two null children, so 1 + max(-1, -1) = 0, and round 0 is index 0. Return 0 for null and every level shifts by one — the classic off-by-one in this problem, and it produces an answer that is wrong in a way that looks structured.

if (h == levels.Count) levels.Add(new List<int>());

Heights arrive in whatever order the recursion reaches them, but a node of height h can only be reached after a node of height h-1 has been (its own child), so levels.Count is never more than one short. That is why a single if suffices rather than a while loop or a pre-sized list — and it is worth stating, because it looks like a bug that happens to work.

Why PeelLevels takes the list as a parameter

The recursion needs to write into shared state and return the height. Passing the accumulator down is the cheapest way; the alternative is returning a tuple of (height, levels) and merging, which allocates per node for no benefit.

PeelAndMutate uses ref TreeNode root

The last round removes the root itself, so the caller’s reference has to be nulled. Without ref the caller is left holding a node that the function believes it deleted — the kind of API detail that is invisible in a test and obvious in a code review.

Strip returns the replacement node

Returning null for a leaf is what lets the parent do node.Left = Strip(node.Left, picked) and cut the link without needing a parent pointer or a special case for "my child was a leaf". It is the standard shape for tree mutation in a functional style and it removes every null check.

The N-ary version is the same function

h = -1; foreach (child) h = max(h, Height(child)); h += 1; is 1 + max(children) with an empty-children base case folded in. Nothing else changes — not the bucketing, not the base case, not the complexity. That is the answer to the follow-up, and the fact that it is this small is the point.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
PeelLevelsO(n)O(n + h)Each node visited once; output is n values, recursion is h deep
PeelAndMutateO(n · h)O(h)One full pass per round, h rounds
NaryLeafPeeling.PeelLevelsO(n)O(n + h)Each node and each child link visited once
The part worth saying out loudThe O(h) recursion depth is the real risk here, not the time. The statement says imbalanced, and a degenerate chain of 100,000 nodes will overflow the default 1 MB stack. The fix is an explicit stack, or a Thread created with a larger stack size. Worth raising — the question hands you the word "imbalanced" and it is the only reason it is there.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Null rootReturns an empty list, no exception.
Single nodeReturns [[value]] — the root is its own round. This is the case that distinguishes the two readings of "until only the root remains".
A degenerate chainh = n, so n rounds of one node each. Correct, and the stack-depth risk.
A perfect treeh = log n rounds, with half the nodes in round 0.
Duplicate valuesFine; nothing is keyed on value.
One-sided nodesA node with only a left child has height 1 + max(hL, -1) = 1 + hL. The null side contributes nothing, which is what the -1 base case buys.
N-ary node with no childrenThe loop does not execute, h stays -1 and becomes 0 — a leaf. The base case works without a special case for empty children.
100,000-node chainRecursion overflows. Needs an iterative post-order walk or a bigger stack.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 5 follow-ups.
“Solve it for an N-ary tree.” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The instinct is that fan-out changes something. It does not — the recurrence is "one more than my slowest child", and the number of children is irrelevant to that.

Approach

h = 1 + max over all children, with -1 when there are none. The bucketing, the base case and the complexity are all unchanged.

Trade-off

None. The whole point is that the generalisation is free, and it is free because the original solution was written against the recurrence rather than against the binary shape. If the original had been the literal remove-and-repeat version, this follow-up would have needed the removal logic rewritten for a child list.

N-ary
Q10_Leaves.cs
public static class NaryLeafPeeling { public static List<List<int>> PeelLevels(NaryNode root) { var levels = new List<List<int>>(); Height(root, levels); return levels; } private static int Height(NaryNode node, List<List<int>> levels) { if (node == null) return -1; int h = -1; foreach (NaryNode child in node.Children) h = Math.Max(h, Height(child, levels)); h += 1; if (h == levels.Count) levels.Add(new List<int>()); levels[h].Add(node.Value); return h; } }
TimeSpace
O(n)O(n + h)
“Does the tree actually have to be mutated?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

This is the question behind the question, and it should be raised before writing anything rather than waiting to be asked.

Approach

No. The rounds are a property of the shape, so one read-only pass computes them. If the caller genuinely wants the tree destroyed, PeelAndMutate does it explicitly — but as a separate, clearly-named operation, not as a side effect of a query.

Trade-off

O(n) versus O(n·h), and a preserved input versus a destroyed one. There is no case where the mutating version is better for computing the answer; it is only better if destruction is itself the goal. Separating the two is the design point.

The mutating version, as a separate operation
Q10_Leaves.cs
// If the interviewer genuinely wants the tree destroyed, do it explicitly: one pass per // round, cutting the child links whose subtree was a leaf. O(n) per round, O(n·h) overall. public static List<List<int>> PeelAndMutate(ref TreeNode root) { var rounds = new List<List<int>>(); while (root != null) { var picked = new List<int>(); root = Strip(root, picked); rounds.Add(picked); } return rounds; }
TimeSpace
O(n) non-mutating; O(n·h) mutatingO(n + h) / O(h)
“The tree has 100,000 nodes in a chain. Your recursion overflows.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The statement said imbalanced, so this is not hypothetical. The recurrence is fine; the implementation of it is not.

Approach

Convert to an iterative post-order traversal with an explicit Stack, carrying computed heights in a Dictionary<TreeNode, int> or by pushing each node twice with a visited marker. Alternatively, run the recursion on a Thread constructed with a larger stack — which is a legitimate and much shorter answer in C#, and worth offering as the pragmatic option.

Trade-off

The explicit stack is robust and roughly doubles the code, and it moves the memory from the stack to the heap where it is bounded by available memory rather than by a 1 MB default. The bigger-thread trick is three lines and only moves the cliff. For a library I would write the iterative version; for a one-off I would raise the stack and document why.

TimeSpace
O(n)O(n) heap instead of O(n) stack
“Return the nodes, not the values. And what if you need the parent of each?” · likely pushback
Thinking

A small change that reveals whether the traversal was written for the answer or for the output format.

Approach

Bucket TreeNode references instead of ints — a type change and nothing else. For parents, pass the parent down as a second argument; it costs one parameter and no extra pass, because the recursion already visits every node with its parent on the stack.

Trade-off

Holding node references keeps the whole tree alive from the result, which matters if the caller expected the values to be a lightweight snapshot. Worth a sentence about lifetime, not more.

TimeSpace
O(n)O(n)
“Why is this not just BFS with levels?” · likely pushback
Thinking

Being able to answer this precisely is the clearest evidence of understanding, and it is worth volunteering.

Approach

BFS gives depth from the root; this needs height from the leaves. In the worked example, node 3 is at depth 1 and nodes 4 and 5 at depth 2, yet all three leave in round 0. No function of depth produces that grouping, because two nodes at different depths can share a round and two nodes at the same depth can be in different ones.

Trade-off

It is not a trade, it is a correctness distinction. 103 Binary Tree Zigzag is on the supplied list and is the BFS version of a level question; doing the two back to back is the fastest way to internalise which is which.

TimeSpace
——
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Not implementing the statement literally — recognising that a procedural description describes a property.
  • Bottom-up versus top-down recursion — height returned up, depth passed down. The core skill, and the link to 364.
  • Base-case discipline — -1 for null, and why.
  • Knowing when not to mutate, and raising it as a question rather than assuming.
  • Stack-depth awareness — the word "imbalanced" is in the statement for a reason.
A senior engineer might say
Writes the post-order height solution, buckets by height, gets the -1 base case right, and generalises to N-ary in two lines.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and: says up front that the tree does not need mutating and asks whether the caller needs it afterwards; explains why BFS cannot produce this grouping, with node 3 as the counterexample; notices that "imbalanced" implies a stack-depth risk and offers the iterative version; and connects it to 364, which is on the supplied list twice. The "I do not need to mutate it" observation, made before being asked, is the signal.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Let me ask about the round the statement describes rather than perform it. Which round does a given node leave in?
A leaf leaves in round 0. Any other node cannot leave until all its children have, so it leaves one round after its slowest child. That is one plus the max over children — which is just the node's height measured from the bottom.
So the rounds are not something I do, they are something every node already has. One post-order pass computes every height, I bucket nodes by height, and I am done in O(n) with the tree untouched.
Which raises the question — do you want the tree mutated? I do not need to, and if the caller wants it afterwards then not mutating is the only correct answer. If you do want it destroyed I would do that as a separate, explicitly named operation rather than as a side effect of this one.
Base case is minus one for null, so a leaf comes out as zero and the list indices line up.
Worth saying why this is not BFS. BFS gives me depth from the root, and I need height from the leaves. In this tree node 3 is at depth 1 while 4 and 5 are at depth 2, and all three leave in round 0. No function of depth gives me that grouping.
For N-ary the recurrence does not change at all — one plus the max over the children, however many there are. Same bucketing, same base case, same complexity.
One thing I would flag: you said imbalanced, so the height can be n. That means my recursion is n deep, and at a hundred thousand nodes it overflows the default stack. I would either write it iteratively with an explicit stack or run it on a thread with a bigger one.
