1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Implement: insert(val) → expected O(1) remove(val) → expected O(1) removeRandomElement() → expected O(1)
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Note the word expected in all three requirements. That is doing real work: it concedes that the structure is hash-based and therefore has no worst-case guarantee, which is exactly the distinction to make out loud.
Reported elsewhere
Insert Delete GetRandom O(1), duplicates allowed (LeetCode 381) — on your own list, first-party, and already worked in the coding page.
LeetCode
380 is the standard version and is on the supplied list. The difference here is removeRandomElement instead of getRandom, which needs no new idea — it is Remove(PeekRandom()).
ClassificationInterviewDB reported and independently first-party reported. High confidence. The variation — remove-random rather than get-random — is trivial once the base structure is right.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickAre duplicates allowed? The statement says insert(val) with no return type and does not say. I take no duplicates in the base design, because that is LeetCode 380 and it is the simpler structure, and then give the duplicates version as a follow-up. The distinction matters more than it looks: with duplicates, remove(val) has to choose which copy to remove, the value-to-index map becomes a value-to-set-of-indices map, and uniform random selection becomes uniform over occurrences rather than over distinct values. Ask. Second: what should removeRandomElement do on an empty structure? Throwing is the honest answer for a method that must return a value; returning a sentinel means a caller cannot distinguish "empty" from "the element was the sentinel".
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A bag you can add to, delete a specific item from, and pull a random item out of — all in constant time.

What we are being asked to compute

Not a value so much as a set of guarantees. The interesting one is uniform randomness together with O(1) deletion, because the obvious structures give you one or the other.

Constraints that matter
  • All three operations O(1) expected, which the statement is careful to say.
  • The random choice must be uniform over the current contents.
  • A specific value must be deletable, not just an arbitrary one.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
No duplicates in the base version.With duplicates the index map becomes a map to a set of positions, and "remove this value" needs a policy for which copy. Both are implemented here; the base one is 380.
insert returns whether it changed the structure.LeetCode 380 does this and it is genuinely useful — the caller can tell an insert from a no-op without a second lookup.
removeRandomElement throws when empty.A sentinel return conflates "nothing here" with "the element was the sentinel". If the signature must not throw, it should return bool TryRemoveRandom(out int).
Random is seedable.Otherwise the uniformity cannot be tested. The test suite runs 40,000 trials and a chi-square check, which needs determinism per trial.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
SequenceInsert(5)true
Insert(5) againfalse — already present
Insert(6); Insert(7); Remove(6)true; count is 2
Remove(6) againfalse
CriticalInsert(1); Insert(2); Remove(2)removing the last slot — the case that breaks a careless swap
Drain500 values inserted, then drained by RemoveRandomevery value comes out exactly once
Uniformity40,000 trials of "insert 0–9, remove one at random"chi-square 9 dof, passes
Where these came fromThe "Critical" row is the one to write a test for before anything else: when the slot being removed is the last slot, the swap-with-last becomes a self-assignment and the index bookkeeping has to still be right. The uniformity check is a chi-square test over 40,000 seeded trials, because "it looks random" is not a claim worth making.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
Take the three requirements one at a time and notice that each rules out the structure that serves the others. A dictionary gives O(1) insert and O(1) delete by value. It cannot give you a uniform random element — there is no way to pick the i-th entry of a hash table in constant time, and iterating to the i-th is O(n). An array gives O(1) random selection, because a uniform index is one call to the random generator. But deleting a specific value means finding it, O(n), and then closing the hole, also O(n). So hold both: the array holds the values, and the dictionary maps each value to its index in the array. Random selection uses the array. Finding a value uses the dictionary. That leaves the one real problem: deleting from the middle of an array without shifting. And the insight is that nothing requires the array to be ordered. Its only job is to be a gap-free list of the current contents, so that a uniform index is a uniform element. So instead of shifting, move the last element into the hole and shorten the array by one. O(1), and the array is still gap-free. The gap-free property is the load-bearing one and is worth naming explicitly: it is what makes _rng.Next(_items.Count) uniform. Tombstones or a free-list would break it, because a random index could land on a hole. Then removeRandomElement is just the composition: pick a uniform index, read the value, and run the same removal. No new machinery at all.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

A List<int> alone. Insert appends. Remove scans for the value and calls RemoveAt. Random picks an index.

What it costs

Insert O(1), remove O(n) — O(n) to find plus O(n) to shift — random O(1). Space O(n).

Why it stops workingOnly remove is wrong, and it is wrong twice, for two independent reasons: finding the value is linear, and closing the hole is linear. The dictionary fixes the first and the swap-with-last fixes the second, and separating them is the cleanest way to explain the design — each structure exists to kill one of the two linear costs.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTInsert(val), Remove(val), RemoveRandomElement()
↓
TRANSFORMATIONValues live in an unordered, gap-free array; the dictionary remembers where each one is
↓
DATA STRUCTUREList<int> _items and Dictionary<int, int> _indexOf
↓
ALGORITHMInsert appends and records the index. Remove looks up the index, overwrites it with the last element, fixes that element’s index, and truncates. Random picks a uniform index and removes that value.
↓
OUTPUTO(1) expected for all three
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
List<int> for the valuesContiguous, so a uniform index is a uniform element, and Count is O(1). The order is meaningless and that is exactly what permits the swap.
Dictionary<int, int> value → indexKills the linear search. Storing the index rather than anything else is what makes the removal O(1) — it is the same trick as Q1 storing the node rather than the value.
Swap-with-last rather than shiftingO(1) instead of O(n), available only because the array’s order carries no meaning.
No tombstones, no free listThe array must stay gap-free or Next(Count) stops being uniform. This is the reason to reject the tombstone approach, and it is worth saying rather than just not doing it.
The algorithm
  1. Insert: if present, return false. Otherwise record _indexOf[val] = _items.Count and append.
  2. Remove: look up slot; absent → false.
  3. Read last = _items[^1]. Write _items[slot] = last.
  4. Set _indexOf[last] = slot before truncating — the ordering that makes the last-slot case work.
  5. Truncate the list and remove val from the dictionary.
  6. RemoveRandomElement: slot = rng.Next(Count), read the value, call Remove.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

The case that matters. Contents [1, 2, 3] with _indexOf = {1:0, 2:1, 3:2}. First remove a middle element, then remove the last one.

Step_items_indexOfNote
start[1, 2, 3]{1:0, 2:1, 3:2}
Remove(2): slot = 1[1, 2, 3]last = 3
write last into the hole[1, 3, 3]slot 1 now holds 3
fix the moved index{1:0, 2:1, 3:1}3 is now at slot 1
truncate, drop 2[1, 3]{1:0, 3:1}gap-free, and correct
Remove(3): slot = 1[1, 3]last is also 3 — slot == last index
write, fix, truncate[1]{1:0}_indexOf[3] = 1 then Remove(3) deletes it anyway
What the state tells youThe last two rows are the whole test. When slot is the last index, the write is a self-assignment and _indexOf[last] = slot sets the index of the very value being deleted — which is harmless only because the dictionary removal comes after. Reverse those two lines and the entry is re-inserted after being removed, leaving a dictionary entry pointing past the end of the array. It passes every test where the removed element is not last.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The structure 45 lines
Q12_RandomBag.cs
// Q12 — Insert, Remove and RemoveRandom, all expected O(1). public class RandomBag { private readonly List<int> _items = new(); // the values, in no meaningful order private readonly Dictionary<int, int> _indexOf = new(); // value -> its slot in _items private readonly Random _rng; public RandomBag(int? seed = null) { _rng = seed.HasValue ? new Random(seed.Value) : new Random(); } public int Count => _items.Count; public bool Insert(int value) { if (_indexOf.ContainsKey(value)) return false; _indexOf[value] = _items.Count; _items.Add(value); return true; } // The whole technique: never shift the list. Move the LAST element into the hole. public bool Remove(int value) { if (!_indexOf.TryGetValue(value, out int slot)) return false; int last = _items[^1]; _items[slot] = last; _indexOf[last] = slot; // must run BEFORE the removal below, for slot == last index _items.RemoveAt(_items.Count - 1); _indexOf.Remove(value); return true; } public int RemoveRandomElement() { if (_items.Count == 0) throw new InvalidOperationException("Empty."); int slot = _rng.Next(_items.Count); // uniform only because the list is gap-free int value = _items[slot]; Remove(value); return value; } public int PeekRandom() { if (_items.Count == 0) throw new InvalidOperationException("Empty."); return _items[_rng.Next(_items.Count)]; } }
The duplicates variant 46 lines
Q12_RandomBag.cs
// Follow-up — duplicates allowed. The value now maps to a SET of slots, and any one of them // may be evicted. Random selection stays uniform over occurrences, not over distinct values. public class RandomMultiBag { private readonly List<int> _items = new(); private readonly Dictionary<int, HashSet<int>> _slots = new(); private readonly Random _rng; public RandomMultiBag(int? seed = null) { _rng = seed.HasValue ? new Random(seed.Value) : new Random(); } public int Count => _items.Count; public bool Insert(int value) { if (!_slots.TryGetValue(value, out var set)) _slots[value] = set = new HashSet<int>(); bool firstCopy = set.Count == 0; set.Add(_items.Count); _items.Add(value); return firstCopy; } public bool Remove(int value) { if (!_slots.TryGetValue(value, out var set) || set.Count == 0) return false; int slot = set.First(); int lastSlot = _items.Count - 1; int last = _items[lastSlot]; set.Remove(slot); if (slot != lastSlot) { _items[slot] = last; _slots[last].Remove(lastSlot); _slots[last].Add(slot); } _items.RemoveAt(lastSlot); if (set.Count == 0) _slots.Remove(value); return true; } public int RemoveRandomElement() { if (_items.Count == 0) throw new InvalidOperationException("Empty."); int value = _items[_rng.Next(_items.Count)]; Remove(value); return value; } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
The ordering of the four lines in Remove

_items[slot] = last; then _indexOf[last] = slot; then RemoveAt, then _indexOf.Remove(value). When slot is the last index, last == value, so line 2 writes an index for the value being deleted and line 4 removes it. Swap lines 2 and 4 and you are left with a dictionary entry pointing at a slot that no longer exists — a bug that only fires when the removed element happens to be last.

_items[^1] and then RemoveAt(Count - 1)

The C# index-from-end operator for the read, and an explicit index for the removal. List<T>.RemoveAt on the final element is O(1) — there is nothing after it to shift — which is what keeps the whole operation constant time.

Why PeekRandom and RemoveRandomElement are separate

RemoveRandomElement is Remove(PeekRandom()) in effect, and keeping the peek available is what makes the structure also answer LeetCode 380. The requirement asked for remove-random; having both costs three lines and makes the relationship obvious.

Uniformity comes from the gap-free invariant

_rng.Next(_items.Count) is uniform over indices, and uniform over indices is uniform over elements only if every index holds a live element. That is the invariant the swap preserves and that a tombstone scheme would destroy. It is the one-sentence justification for the whole design.

The duplicates version: Dictionary<int, HashSet<int>>

One value now owns several slots. Remove takes set.First() — an arbitrary copy, which is fine because the copies are indistinguishable. The delicate part is when the moved element has the same value as the one being removed: then set and _slots[last] are the same object, and the remove-then-add sequence has to leave exactly the right slot behind. The test suite covers that case specifically.

if (slot != lastSlot) in the duplicates version

Guarding the swap. Without it, removing the last slot would do _slots[last].Remove(lastSlot) followed by .Add(slot) with slot == lastSlot, re-adding the slot that was just removed. The base version does not need the guard because the dictionary removal comes last; the duplicates version does, because the set is shared.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
InsertO(1) expectedO(1)One dictionary write plus an amortised list append
RemoveO(1) expectedO(1)One lookup, one write, one truncate
RemoveRandomElementO(1) expectedO(1)One random index plus a Remove
space—O(n)One list slot and one dictionary entry per value
The part worth saying out loud"Expected" is doing real work in all three, and the statement itself says so. The bound rests on hashing: adversarial or pathological keys degrade a dictionary to O(n). The list operations are genuinely worst-case O(1) — appending is amortised because of resizing, and RemoveAt on the last element shifts nothing. So the honest summary is: O(1) worst case on the array side, O(1) expected on the dictionary side, and the composition is therefore expected. Saying that unprompted reads very differently from being corrected on it.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Insert a duplicateReturns false, changes nothing, in the base version.
Remove an absent valueReturns false.
Remove the last slotThe critical case. Works because the dictionary removal comes after the index fix.
Remove the only elementLeaves both structures empty and consistent.
RemoveRandomElement on emptyThrows. State the choice, or expose a Try variant.
Drain the whole structureEvery value comes out exactly once — tested with 500 values.
Re-insert a removed valueFine; it gets a fresh slot at the end.
Duplicates version, remove when the moved element shares the valueThe subtle case; the slot != lastSlot guard handles it.
Very large nBounded by memory. The list resizing is amortised O(1) and doubles capacity, so peak memory is up to 2× the live set — worth knowing if memory is tight.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 4 follow-ups.
“Allow duplicates.” · likely pushback
Thinking

Which part of the design stops working? The value → index map, because a value no longer has an index. Everything else survives.

Approach

Dictionary<int, HashSet<int>> from value to the set of slots holding it. Remove picks any slot from the set — copies are indistinguishable — and performs the same swap, updating the moved element’s slot in its own set. The array stays gap-free, so randomness is unchanged and is now uniform over occurrences: a value present three times is three times as likely, which is usually what is wanted and should be stated either way.

Trade-off

A HashSet per distinct value instead of an int, so materially more memory and an extra indirection on every operation. This is LeetCode 381 and it is rated Hard, entirely because of the bookkeeping when the moved element shares the value being removed. Everything stays O(1) expected.

Duplicates allowed
Q12_RandomBag.cs
// Follow-up — duplicates allowed. The value now maps to a SET of slots, and any one of them // may be evicted. Random selection stays uniform over occurrences, not over distinct values. public class RandomMultiBag { private readonly List<int> _items = new(); private readonly Dictionary<int, HashSet<int>> _slots = new(); private readonly Random _rng; public RandomMultiBag(int? seed = null) { _rng = seed.HasValue ? new Random(seed.Value) : new Random(); } public int Count => _items.Count; public bool Insert(int value) { if (!_slots.TryGetValue(value, out var set)) _slots[value] = set = new HashSet<int>(); bool firstCopy = set.Count == 0; set.Add(_items.Count); _items.Add(value); return firstCopy; } public bool Remove(int value) { if (!_slots.TryGetValue(value, out var set) || set.Count == 0) return false; int slot = set.First(); int lastSlot = _items.Count - 1; int last = _items[lastSlot]; set.Remove(slot); if (slot != lastSlot) { _items[slot] = last; _slots[last].Remove(lastSlot); _slots[last].Add(slot); } _items.RemoveAt(lastSlot); if (set.Count == 0) _slots.Remove(value); return true; } public int RemoveRandomElement() { if (_items.Count == 0) throw new InvalidOperationException("Empty."); int value = _items[_rng.Next(_items.Count)]; Remove(value); return value; } }
TimeSpace
O(1) expectedO(n) with a larger constant
“Make it thread-safe.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The two structures have to move together. Any window in which the array has been truncated but the dictionary has not is a window in which a random pick can read past the end.

Approach

One lock around each operation. The critical section is a handful of writes, so it is short even under contention, and ConcurrentDictionary alone would be wrong — it makes each dictionary operation atomic without making the pair atomic, which is the actual requirement.

Trade-off

Simple and correct, and it serialises everything including the random reads. Sharding is much harder here than for a cache: a uniform random pick across shards has to be weighted by each shard’s size, and reading those sizes without a lock reintroduces the race. The pragmatic answer is one lock and, if reads dominate and slight staleness is acceptable, a snapshot for the random path.

TimeSpace
O(1) plus lock acquisitionunchanged
“Weight the random selection.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The gap-free array gives uniformity for free, and weighting is exactly the thing it cannot do.

Approach

Two options. If weights are small integers, insert each value w times — the duplicates version already handles it and uniformity over occurrences becomes weighting for free. For arbitrary weights, prefix sums plus binary search, which is 528 Random Pick with Weight and is on the supplied list.

Trade-off

Repeated insertion keeps every operation O(1) and costs memory proportional to the total weight. Prefix sums cost O(log n) per pick and O(n) to rebuild after any change, which makes them poor for a mutable structure. Since this structure’s whole point is mutation, repeated insertion is the better fit here — and that is the reasoning, not the answer, that matters.

TimeSpace
O(1) for repeated insertion; O(log n) for prefix sumsO(total weight) or O(n)
“It must be a snapshot — callers iterate while others mutate.” · likely pushback
Thinking

Iteration was never in the requirements, and adding it changes the structure’s character from a bag to a collection.

Approach

Copy-on-write: mutations build a new array and dictionary and publish the pair atomically behind a single reference, so an iterator holds a consistent immutable snapshot. Alternatively hand out an explicit ToArray() copy and keep mutation cheap.

Trade-off

Copy-on-write makes every mutation O(n), which destroys the entire point of the structure. The explicit-copy option keeps mutations O(1) and makes the snapshot O(n) at the moment it is taken, which is almost certainly the right trade — iteration is rare, mutation is the hot path. Worth saying plainly that copy-on-write would be the wrong call here.

TimeSpace
O(n) per mutation for CoW; O(n) per snapshot otherwiseO(n) per live snapshot
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Structure composition — two structures each covering the other’s weakness.
  • Recognising which properties are not required — the array need not be ordered, and that is what unlocks O(1) deletion.
  • Invariant identification — gap-free is what makes the randomness uniform.
  • Index bookkeeping — the last-slot case, which is where this is actually implemented wrongly.
  • Expected versus worst case — the statement raises it, so it should be addressed.
A senior engineer might say
Builds list plus dictionary, gets the swap-with-last right including the last-slot case, and composes remove-random from the pieces.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and: says explicitly that the array’s order is meaningless and that this is what permits the swap; names the gap-free invariant as the justification for uniformity and uses it to reject tombstones; asks about duplicates before choosing the map type; addresses "expected" by separating the worst-case array operations from the expected-case hashing; and on the weighting follow-up, argues from this structure being mutable rather than reciting both options. Naming the invariant is the discriminating move — it is what turns a memorised trick into a design.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Let me take the three requirements one at a time, because each one rules out the structure that serves the others.
A dictionary gives me O(1) insert and O(1) delete by value, but there is no way to pick a uniform random entry of a hash table in constant time.
An array gives me the uniform pick for free — one call to the random generator — but deleting a specific value means finding it, which is linear, and then closing the hole, which is also linear.
So I hold both: the array holds the values, and the dictionary maps each value to its index in the array. That kills the linear search.
The remaining problem is the hole. And the key realisation is that nothing requires the array to be ordered — its only job is to be a gap-free list of the current contents. So instead of shifting, I move the last element into the hole and shorten the array.
That gap-free property is the invariant the whole thing rests on, because a uniform index is only a uniform element if every index holds something live. It is also why I would not use tombstones — a random index could land on a hole.
One detail I would write a test for first: when the slot I am removing is the last slot. The swap becomes a self-assignment, and I have to fix the moved element's index before removing the value from the dictionary — otherwise I re-insert the entry I just deleted and leave it pointing past the end of the array. It passes every test where the element is not last.
Then remove-random is just the composition: uniform index, read the value, same removal path. No new machinery.
One thing on the complexity — you said expected, and that is right. The array operations are genuinely worst-case constant; it is the hashing that is only expected. Worth separating.
And I would ask about duplicates, because that changes the map from value-to-index to value-to-set-of-indices, and it changes what uniform means — uniform over occurrences rather than over distinct values.
Video lesson plan · LinkedIn Coding — Insert, Remove and Remove-Random in O(1) · ~13 min

Derive the pairing from the three requirements, name the gap-free invariant, and spend real time on the last-slot bug.

Q13

Rank-based cache

MediumHigh priorityDESIGNDATA STRUCTURE DESIGNCACHEHEAPHASHING

High priority: the ranked-eviction cache is one of the best-attested shapes at LinkedIn, and it composes with Q1.

1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Create a cache with: • fixed capacity • static distinct integer ranks • key/value • slow DataSource If cache is full: evict the item with the lowest rank. If the requested item is not cached: retrieve from DataSource.
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
The two words that matter are static and distinct. Static means ranks never change, which removes the hardest part of LFU. Distinct means a rank-ordered structure can compare on rank alone and lose nothing. Both are gifts, and both should be acknowledged out loud — they are what make this Medium rather than Hard.
Reported elsewhere
"LFU cache with a GetRank() function, LRU as the tiebreaker" — LeetCode Discuss, LinkedIn Staff SWE with offer, Jun 2025. First-hand. That question is harder than this one (frequency is dynamic) but the shape is the same.
Reported elsewhere
All O’one Data Structure (LeetCode 432) — on your own list. It is the answer to the follow-up where ranks stop being static.
LeetCode
460 LFU Cache and 215 Kth Largest are both on the supplied list. 460 is this with a dynamic rank; 215 is the selection half in isolation.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported, with a very close first-hand report from a successful Staff candidate. The strongest evidence on the page after Q5 and Q17.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickThe interesting gap: what happens when the new item’s rank is lower than everything in the cache? Taken literally, "if the cache is full, evict the lowest rank" would have you evict a better item and store a worse one — and if the next request is for that same low-ranked key, you would do it again, thrashing the cache with the least valuable data. That is clearly not intended, but the statement does not rule it out. I decline to cache in that case and return the value anyway, which is an admission policy rather than an eviction policy, and I would say so explicitly. It is the single best thing to raise on this question, because it is a real design decision hiding in an under-specified sentence. Also unstated: is rank available without a fetch? GetRank(key) as a separate method implies yes, which matters — if rank were only known after the expensive fetch, you could not decide admission before paying for the data.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A cache in front of something slow, where importance is given rather than inferred. Unlike LRU or LFU, the cache is told what each item is worth and never has to guess.

What we are being asked to compute

Get(key) returns the value, from the cache if present and from the source otherwise. The design content is the eviction and admission policy around it.

Constraints that matter
  • Ranks are static — they never change for a key. This is what makes a heap or sorted set viable, because nothing ever needs re-prioritising.
  • Ranks are distinct, so comparing on rank alone is a total order.
  • Fixed capacity; eviction is by lowest rank.
  • The data source is slow, so a miss is expensive and the hit rate is the thing being optimised.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
GetRank(key) is cheap and available without fetching the value.If rank came only with the data, admission could not be decided before paying the fetch cost — the policy would have to become "fetch, then decide whether to keep", which is still correct but much less useful.
A low-ranked newcomer is not cached when the cache is full.The literal reading would evict something better to store something worse. This is the clarification to raise.
Ranks really are distinct.The comparer depends on it. Comparing on rank alone means two items with equal rank look identical to the sorted set, and one would be silently dropped. If ranks could collide, the comparer must fall back to the key.
No TTL, no invalidation.With static ranks and immutable values there is nothing to expire. If values could change behind the cache, this becomes Q1 and needs a TTL or an invalidation hook.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Setupcapacity 3, ranks a=10 b=20 c=30 d=5 e=40
Get(a), Get(b), Get(c)3 source calls; cache full
Get(a) againhit — no source call
Get(e), rank 40a (rank 10, the lowest) evicted; e admitted
Key caseGet(d), rank 5not cached — 5 is below every rank held; value still returned
cache still holds b, c, enothing was evicted for d
Where these came fromNo examples were supplied. The "Key case" row is the ambiguity above, and the test suite asserts both halves of it: that d is not admitted, and that Get(d) still returns the correct value. A cache that declines to store something must still answer the question.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
Two observations, and the first one is a gift you should notice out loud. Static ranks remove the hard part of LFU. In an LFU cache, every access changes a key’s frequency, so the priority structure must support "increase the priority of this arbitrary element", which a plain heap cannot do in better than O(n). That is why LFU needs the bucket-of-equal-counts construction from 432 All O’one. Here, rank never changes. So nothing is ever re-prioritised, and a simple ordered structure is enough. Saying "this is much easier than LFU, and here is exactly why" is a strong opening. Distinct ranks let you compare on rank alone. The eviction victim is the minimum rank, so you need a structure that yields its minimum cheaply, plus a dictionary for O(1) lookup by key. Same pairing as Q1 and Q12: one structure for the ordering, one for the lookup. A min-heap gives O(1) peek and O(log n) pop. A SortedSet gives O(log n) minimum and, unlike a heap, supports removing an arbitrary element — which a heap cannot do without an auxiliary index. Since nothing here needs arbitrary removal yet, either works; the sorted set costs nothing extra and keeps the door open. And then the third thing, which the statement does not ask and which is the actual design judgement: eviction policy and admission policy are different questions. "Evict the lowest rank" says what leaves. It does not say what is allowed in. If a newcomer ranks below everything held, admitting it means evicting something better — so the right policy is to serve the value and not cache it. Recognising that an under-specified sentence conceals a second policy is the staff-level move here.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

A Dictionary<TKey, (value, rank)>. On a miss when full, scan every entry to find the lowest rank and evict it.

What it costs

Get O(1) on a hit, O(capacity) on an eviction. Space O(capacity).

Why it stops workingAt capacity 100 the scan is irrelevant and this is a perfectly good answer to give first. It stops being fine at capacity 100,000 — and more importantly, it is the same self-defeating shape as Q1’s brute force: the larger you make the cache to improve the hit rate, the more each miss costs. A structure that knows its own minimum removes that coupling.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTGet(key), a capacity, and an IRankedDataSource
↓
TRANSFORMATIONEach entry is stored twice: by key for lookup, and by rank for eviction
↓
DATA STRUCTUREDictionary<TKey, (TValue, int)> plus SortedSet<(int Rank, TKey Key)> with a rank-only comparer
↓
ALGORITHMHit → return. Miss → fetch, then admit only if the rank beats the current minimum; admitting evicts the minimum.
↓
OUTPUTThe value, always; caching is a separate decision
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
Dictionary for lookupO(1) hit path, which is the whole reason a cache exists.
SortedSet<(int Rank, TKey Key)> for eviction orderO(log n) minimum and O(log n) arbitrary removal. A PriorityQueue would give a cheaper peek and cannot remove an arbitrary element, which forecloses the invalidation follow-up. The sorted set costs a log factor on peek and keeps the option.
A comparer on rank onlyAvoids requiring TKey : IComparable, which would be an unnecessary constraint on the cache’s users. Safe because ranks are distinct — and if that guarantee failed, the comparer would silently drop entries, which is the thing to flag.
Admission check before evictionThe policy decision. Comparing the newcomer’s rank against the current minimum is one line and it is what stops the cache being thrashed by low-value keys.
Counters for hits, misses, admissions and rejectionsA cache you cannot observe is a cache you cannot tune. Four increments, and they turn "is the policy working" from an argument into a measurement.
The algorithm
  1. Get: dictionary hit → count it and return.
  2. Miss → fetch the value and the rank from the source.
  3. If the cache is not full, store and index it.
  4. If full, compare the new rank with _byRank.Min.Rank.
  5. Lower → count a rejection and return the value without caching.
  6. Higher → evict the minimum from both structures, then store and index the newcomer.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

Capacity 3. Ranks: a=10, b=20, c=30, d=5, e=40. The two interesting steps are the admission of e and the rejection of d.

CallSource?EntriesbyRank (min first)Note
Get(a)yesa(10,a)
Get(b)yesa b(10,a) (20,b)
Get(c)yesa b c(10,a) (20,b) (30,c)full
Get(a)noa b cunchangedhit — the point of the cache
Get(e) rank 40yesb c e(20,b) (30,c) (40,e)40 > min 10 → evict a, admit e
Get(d) rank 5yesb c eunchanged5 < min 20 → not admitted; value returned
Get(d) againyesb c eunchangedstill a miss — the cost of the policy
What the state tells youThe last two rows are the trade. Rejecting d protects three better-ranked entries, and the price is that every request for d pays the source. That is correct under the assumption that rank reflects value — and if d were requested a thousand times a second, rank would be the wrong signal and the cache would need to combine rank with frequency. Worth naming as the limit of the policy rather than defending it absolutely.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The data source interface 5 lines
Q13_RankCache.cs
// Q13 — fixed-capacity cache that evicts the lowest static rank. public interface IRankedDataSource<TKey, TValue> { TValue Get(TKey key); // slow: this is what the cache exists to avoid int GetRank(TKey key); // static and distinct across keys }
The cache 54 lines
Q13_RankCache.cs
public class RankCache<TKey, TValue> { // Ranks are distinct, so comparing on rank alone is a total order and nothing is lost. // If ranks could repeat, this comparer would silently drop entries — worth saying out loud. private sealed class ByRank : IComparer<(int Rank, TKey Key)> { public int Compare((int Rank, TKey Key) a, (int Rank, TKey Key) b) => a.Rank.CompareTo(b.Rank); } private readonly int _capacity; private readonly IRankedDataSource<TKey, TValue> _source; private readonly Dictionary<TKey, (TValue Value, int Rank)> _entries = new(); private readonly SortedSet<(int Rank, TKey Key)> _byRank = new(new ByRank()); public long Hits { get; private set; } public long Misses { get; private set; } public long Admitted { get; private set; } public long Rejected { get; private set; } public RankCache(int capacity, IRankedDataSource<TKey, TValue> source) { if (capacity <= 0) throw new ArgumentOutOfRangeException(nameof(capacity)); _capacity = capacity; _source = source ?? throw new ArgumentNullException(nameof(source)); } public TValue Get(TKey key) { if (_entries.TryGetValue(key, out var hit)) { Hits++; return hit.Value; } Misses++; TValue value = _source.Get(key); int rank = _source.GetRank(key); Admit(key, value, rank); return value; // returned either way; caching is separate } private void Admit(TKey key, TValue value, int rank) { if (_entries.Count == _capacity) { var lowest = _byRank.Min; // The newcomer is worth less than everything already held. Evicting something better // to store something worse is strictly wrong, so decline to cache it. if (rank < lowest.Rank) { Rejected++; return; } _byRank.Remove(lowest); _entries.Remove(lowest.Key); } _entries[key] = (value, rank); _byRank.Add((rank, key)); Admitted++; } public bool Contains(TKey key) => _entries.ContainsKey(key); public int Count => _entries.Count; public int LowestRank => _byRank.Count == 0 ? int.MinValue : _byRank.Min.Rank; }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
The ByRank comparer, and the guarantee it leans on

It compares a.Rank.CompareTo(b.Rank) and ignores the key. A SortedSet treats "compares equal" as "is the same element", so two entries with the same rank would collapse and one would be silently lost. Distinct ranks are what make this safe, and the comment says so in the code. If the guarantee were dropped, the comparer would need ?: a.Key.CompareTo(b.Key) and TKey would have to be comparable.

Why the value is returned even when not cached

Get fetches, calls Admit, and returns the value regardless of what Admit decided. Caching is an optimisation, not part of the contract — conflating the two is how a cache ends up unable to serve keys it has chosen not to store.

if (rank < lowest.Rank) { Rejected++; return; }

The admission policy, in one line. Everything before it is mechanism; this is the judgement. Note < rather than <=: an equal rank cannot occur given the distinctness guarantee, so the choice is moot — but writing < rather than <= means the code would prefer the incumbent on a tie, which is the conservative behaviour.

Eviction touches both structures

_byRank.Remove(lowest) and _entries.Remove(lowest.Key). The invariant is that the two hold the same key set at all times, and the tuple in the sorted set carries the key precisely so the dictionary entry can be found from it — the same reason Q1’s node carries its key.

The four counters

Hits, Misses, Admitted, Rejected. The last two are the ones that matter for this design: a high rejection count means capacity is too small for the rank distribution, and that is not visible from the hit rate alone. It is two lines of code and it is the difference between a policy you can tune and one you can only argue about.

LowestRank as a public property

Exposed for tests, and useful in production for the same reason — it is the admission threshold, so it answers "what rank do I need to get cached right now".

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
Get (hit)O(1) expectedO(1)One dictionary probe
Get (miss, not full)O(log n)O(1)Source fetch plus one sorted-set insert
Get (miss, admitted)O(log n)O(1)One Min, one remove, one insert
Get (miss, rejected)O(log n)O(1)One Min, then nothing
space—O(capacity)One dictionary entry and one set entry per cached key
The part worth saying out loudThe source fetch dominates everything on this table and it is worth saying so. A slow data source is by definition orders of magnitude above a log-n in-memory operation, so optimising O(log n) to O(1) here would be pointless. What matters is the hit rate, which is a function of the policy rather than of the data structures — which is why the admission decision deserves more of the discussion than the choice between a heap and a sorted set.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Capacity 0 or negativeThrows in the constructor.
First Get on an empty cacheMiss, fetch, store. No eviction path taken.
Cache full, newcomer ranks highestMinimum evicted, newcomer admitted.
Cache full, newcomer ranks lowestNot admitted; value still returned. The design decision.
Repeated request for a rejected keyEvery one is a miss. The cost of the policy, and a counter makes it visible.
Same key requested twiceSecond is a hit; the source is called once.
Equal ranks, despite the guaranteeThe comparer would treat them as one entry and silently lose one. Flag it — the code depends on the guarantee the statement gave.
Source throwsPropagates. The cache is unchanged, because nothing is stored until the fetch succeeds.
Capacity 1Works: every miss with a higher rank replaces the single entry.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 4 follow-ups.
“Ranks are no longer static — they change as items are accessed. Now what?” · likely pushback
Thinking

This is the question that turns it into LFU, and the structure that made it easy stops working. Say what breaks before saying what to do.

Approach

A heap cannot increase the priority of an arbitrary element without an index into it, and a SortedSet needs remove-then-reinsert, O(log n) per access — on the hit path, which was O(1). That is the real cost: the hot path gets slower. The proper answer is the 432 All O’one construction: a doubly linked list of count buckets, each bucket holding the keys with that count, plus a map from key to its bucket. Incrementing a count is unlinking from one bucket and linking into the adjacent one — O(1). The minimum is the head bucket. That is also how LFU caches are built, and the reported LinkedIn question adds LRU ordering within a bucket, which the bucket’s own list gives you for free.

Trade-off

O(1) everything, for considerably more code and a structure that is genuinely fiddly to get right. With static ranks none of it is needed, which is exactly why the word "static" in the statement is worth acknowledging — it is the difference between this question and a Hard one.

TimeSpace
O(1) with buckets; O(log n) per access with a sorted setO(capacity)
“Combine rank with recency, so a low-ranked but very hot key can still be cached.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The walkthrough exposed the weakness: a rejected key requested constantly pays the source every time. Rank alone cannot see that.

Approach

Score each entry as a function of both, for example rank + w · recencyBonus, and evict the lowest score. Or segment the cache: reserve most of the capacity for rank-ordered entries and a small window for recently-requested ones, promoting a key out of the window if it is requested again. The segmented approach is what production caches actually do — it is the shape of both ARC and W-TinyLFU.

Trade-off

A combined score makes the priority dynamic, so the previous follow-up’s problem arrives with it. Segmentation keeps each structure simple and static, at the cost of a tuning parameter — how big the window is — which nobody can set correctly without measurement. I would segment, and instrument it, and let the rejection counter tell me the window size.

TimeSpace
O(log n) per evictionO(capacity)
“It is distributed across many nodes.” · likely pushback
Thinking

"Evict the globally lowest rank" needs global knowledge, and no cache pays for that on the eviction path.

Approach

Partition by key with consistent hashing; each node runs this cache over its own shard. Eviction becomes per-shard, so the item evicted is the lowest rank on that node, not globally. Ranks being static helps enormously here — they need no coordination or replication, so every node can decide independently and no node ever disagrees with another about an item’s worth.

Trade-off

Per-shard eviction is approximate: a globally high-ranked item can be evicted because its shard happens to be full of even better items. That is the same approximation as sharded LRU and it is universally accepted. Static ranks make this the easiest distributed cache of the three on this page, and that is worth saying.

TimeSpace
O(log n) local plus one network hopO(capacity) per node
“Values can change in the source. Keep the cache correct.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The statement gave no invalidation requirement, so this changes the contract rather than the implementation — worth saying before answering.

Approach

Either a TTL per entry, which is Q1 and composes directly, or an explicit Invalidate(key) that removes from both structures. This is where the choice of SortedSet over PriorityQueue pays off: arbitrary removal is O(log n) on a sorted set and impossible on a heap without an auxiliary index.

Trade-off

TTL is self-healing and serves stale data for up to the TTL. Explicit invalidation is exact and needs the source to notify the cache, which is a coupling that does not always exist. Most systems use both — invalidation where a signal exists, TTL as the backstop — and saying that is better than picking one.

TimeSpace
O(log n) for invalidationunchanged
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Reading the gift in the statement — "static" and "distinct" are load-bearing, and noticing them is the difference between this and LFU.
  • Structure pairing — dictionary for lookup, ordered structure for eviction. The third question on this page with that shape.
  • Separating eviction from admission — the under-specified case, and the best thing to raise.
  • Library knowledge — why SortedSet rather than PriorityQueue, and what that choice buys later.
  • Knowing where the time actually goes — the source fetch dominates, so hit rate beats asymptotics.
  • Observability — a policy without counters cannot be tuned.
A senior engineer might say
Builds dictionary plus heap or sorted set, evicts the minimum rank, and handles the miss path correctly.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and: says up front that static ranks make this much easier than LFU and why; raises the low-ranked-newcomer case as an admission policy distinct from eviction, and chooses not to cache; notes that the rank-only comparer depends on the distinctness guarantee and would silently lose entries without it; picks SortedSet over PriorityQueue for the arbitrary-removal capability and says which follow-up that enables; observes that the source fetch dominates so the hit rate matters more than the log factor; and adds rejection counters so the policy is measurable. The admission-versus-eviction distinction is the discriminating one — it is a real design decision that the statement leaves out.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Two words in the statement are doing a lot of work, and I want to acknowledge them. Static and distinct.
Static ranks make this much easier than LFU. In an LFU cache every access changes a key's frequency, so the priority structure has to support increasing the priority of an arbitrary element, which a heap cannot do cheaply — that is why LFU needs the bucket construction from All O'one. Here rank never changes, so nothing is ever re-prioritised.
Distinct ranks mean I can compare on rank alone, which saves me from requiring the key type to be comparable. Worth flagging though: a sorted set treats compares-equal as same-element, so if ranks could collide I would silently lose entries. My comparer depends on your guarantee.
So: a dictionary from key to value and rank for the O(1) hit path, and a sorted set ordered by rank so the eviction victim is the minimum.
Now the part your statement does not specify, and I think it is the interesting one. What happens when the new item's rank is lower than everything already cached? Taken literally I would evict something better to store something worse, and if that key were requested again I would do it again.
So I would separate two policies. Eviction says what leaves — lowest rank. Admission says what is allowed in, and I would decline to cache a newcomer that ranks below the current minimum. I still return the value; caching is an optimisation, not part of the contract.
The cost of that is real: a rejected key pays the source on every request. So I would count rejections, because a high rejection rate tells me the capacity is too small for the rank distribution, and the hit rate alone would not show that.
One more thing on complexity — the source is slow, so it dominates everything here. Optimising the log n to constant would be pointless. What matters is the hit rate, which comes from the policy rather than the structures.
I chose SortedSet over PriorityQueue deliberately. A heap has a cheaper peek and cannot remove an arbitrary element, and I would want that if you later asked for invalidation.

Q15

Task manager — dependency graph

LeetCodeDifficultyRelation
261. Graph Valid TreeMedium · premiumSimilar pattern
1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Given: interface Task { String getId(); List<Task> getSubTasks(); } A task depends on completion of all subtasks. Implement: addTask(Task t) Throws if adding the task creates a cycle. pollNextTask() Returns the next executable task whose dependencies are completed.
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Supplied with the interface and both method contracts. The contract has a hole in it — there is no way to signal that a task has finished, so nothing can ever become executable after the first batch. That is the most important thing to notice.
Reported elsewhere
"Implement getBuildOrder() given getDependencies()" — independently reported in the existing research. Same dependency graph, asked as a package build order.
Reported elsewhere
210 Course Schedule II is already a worked problem in the coding page and is the batch version of this.
LeetCode
261 Graph Valid Tree is on the supplied list — undirected cycle detection. This needs the directed version, where a cycle is a back edge to a node still on the current path. Different algorithm, same word.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported and independently reported under a different name. High confidence in the problem; the incremental addTask/pollNextTask framing is what InterviewDB adds over the batch topological sort.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickThe interface cannot work as given, and saying so is the question. pollNextTask() returns a task whose dependencies are complete. Nothing in the interface ever says a task became complete. So the first call returns a leaf, and every subsequent call returns either the same leaf forever or nothing — the system cannot make progress. A CompleteTask(id) (or a callback, or having pollNextTask mark the task complete on return) is required, and which one you pick changes the semantics. I add CompleteTask(string id) and say why. Two smaller gaps follow from it: does pollNextTask hand the same task out twice if it is called twice before completion (I say no, tracked by an issued set), and what happens if a worker takes a task and dies (I add RequeueTask, because without it the task is lost forever). Also: what does addTask do if the task is already registered with different subtasks? I union the edges, and refuse if the task has already been issued — see the follow-up, because this is where the real bug was.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A work queue that understands dependencies. Tasks arrive with their subtasks, a task cannot run until all its subtasks have, and a dependency that would make progress impossible must be rejected at the moment it is introduced.

What we are being asked to compute

Not a value — a component with three or four operations. The interesting parts are cycle detection on insert, and the atomicity of a rejected insert.

Constraints that matter
  • Cycle detection happens on addTask, not at execution time. That is the design difference from a batch topological sort and it is what the question is about.
  • A rejected addTask must leave the manager exactly as it was. Half-applying a change and then throwing is worse than not validating.
  • Subtasks arrive as objects, so the whole reachable closure gets registered, not just the top task.
  • A task reachable by two paths is one task, registered once.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
CompleteTask(id) exists, because the interface as given cannot make progress.Without it, pollNextTask returns the first leaf and nothing ever unblocks. This is the clarification to raise before writing any code.
pollNextTask issues each task at most once.Otherwise two workers polling concurrently both run the same task. Tracked with an issued set; a dropped task needs an explicit RequeueTask.
Ids are unique and identify a task; the object is incidental.Two Task objects with the same id are the same task. That is what lets a cycle be closed across two addTask calls with different objects.
Re-adding a registered task unions its dependencies.The alternative — first registration wins — silently ignores what the caller asked for. Unioning is the defensible choice, and it is what exposed a real bug; see the follow-up.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Chaindeploy → build → compilepoll gives compile; after CompleteTask(compile), build; then deploy
Diamondtop → {a, b}a and b both runnable; top only after both complete
Cyclex → y and y → xaddTask throws; manager still holds 0 tasks
Cross-batchadd p→q, then add q→pthrows — the cycle is only closed by combining the two calls
Sharedroot → {l, r}, both l and r → sharedshared registered once; completing it unblocks both
Selfs → sthrows
Where these came fromThe cross-batch row is the one that found a real bug in my first implementation — see the code walkthrough. Every row above is a test, including the assertions that the manager is unchanged after each throw, which is the part that is easy to get wrong and impossible to notice without testing for it.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
Three observations, and only the first is the algorithm. One: it is a dependency graph, so readiness is an in-degree count. A task is runnable when the number of its subtasks that are not yet complete reaches zero. Keep that count per task, keep a reverse map from each subtask to the tasks waiting on it, and completing a task is a decrement of each waiter — O(1) per edge, and any waiter that hits zero goes on the ready queue. That is Kahn’s algorithm, run incrementally instead of in a batch. Two: cycle detection is on insert, which is a different problem from topological sorting. A batch topological sort detects a cycle by failing to sort — if fewer than n tasks come out, there was a cycle. That does not work here, because the graph is built incrementally and you must reject at the moment the bad edge is added. So it needs an explicit directed cycle check: three-colour DFS, where grey means "on the current path" and meeting grey is a back edge. Three, and this is the staff-level part: the insert must be atomic. Check the whole merged graph first, commit second. It is tempting to register as you walk and unwind on failure, and unwinding is exactly where the bug lives — a partially-registered rejected task leaves the manager in a state no caller can reason about. Collect into locals, validate, and only then touch the fields. Then nothing after the validation can fail, so there is nothing to unwind. And the reason the cycle check must run over the union of the existing and new edges rather than the new ones alone: a cycle can be closed by an edge whose other half was added in an earlier call. That is not hypothetical — my first implementation overwrote the existing edges instead of unioning them, and that exact case passed silently.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Store the tasks. On pollNextTask, run a full topological sort over the whole graph and return the first task whose dependencies are all complete. Detect cycles then, by the sort failing.

What it costs

addTask O(1), pollNextTask O(V + E). Space O(V + E).

Why it stops workingTwo problems, and the second is the real one. The performance objection is that pollNextTask is the hot operation — called once per task — so an O(V+E) poll makes the whole run O(V·(V+E)). The correctness objection is that the specification says addTask throws on a cycle, and deferring detection to poll time means the bad edge has already been accepted: the caller gets no error at the point where they could still do something about it, and by the time poll fails you cannot tell which of the accepted tasks caused it.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTAddTask(ITask), PollNextTask(), CompleteTask(id)
↓
TRANSFORMATIONThe task closure is walked into id-keyed nodes and edges; readiness becomes an outstanding-dependency count
↓
DATA STRUCTUREFive dictionaries and a queue: tasks, dependsOn, blocks (reverse), outstanding, plus _ready, _queued, _issued, _completed
↓
ALGORITHMCollect → cycle-check the merged graph → commit. Completion decrements waiters and enqueues those reaching zero.
↓
OUTPUTA runnable task, or null; CycleException on a cycle
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
_dependsOn and _blocks, both directions_dependsOn is needed for the cycle check; _blocks is needed so completion is O(waiters) rather than a scan of every task. Keeping both is the standard cost of incremental readiness.
_outstanding count per taskThe in-degree, restricted to incomplete dependencies. A set of incomplete dependencies would also work and costs more memory for no extra capability.
Queue plus a _queued setThe queue gives FIFO fairness among ready tasks. The set stops a task being queued twice, which becomes possible once edges can be added to an already-ready task.
_issued separate from _completedA task handed to a worker is neither ready nor done. Without the distinction, two polls return the same task and two workers run it.
Three-colour DFS for the cycle checkGrey = on the current path, black = finished and clean. Re-entering a black node must not be a failure — that is a diamond, not a cycle — and conflating the two is the classic error here.
The algorithm
  1. Phase 1 — walk the new task’s closure into local newTasks and newEdges. Touch no fields.
  2. Phase 2 — build merged as the union of existing and new edges; run the three-colour DFS from the new task’s id; throw CycleException if a back edge is found.
  3. Also refuse if a new edge would be added to a task already issued or completed — ambiguous, so reject rather than guess.
  4. Phase 3 — commit. Register unseen tasks, then add each new edge, updating _blocks and _outstanding.
  5. Mark ready any task whose outstanding count is zero.
  6. PollNextTask: pop from _ready, skipping completed, still-blocked, or already-issued entries.
  7. CompleteTask: idempotent; decrement each waiter and mark ready at zero.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

The cross-batch cycle, which is the case that matters. p depends on q is added first; then a second call says q depends on p.

StepnewEdgesmerged (the union)DFS fromResult
AddTask(p→q)p:[q], q:[]p:[q], q:[]pno cycle → commit; 2 tasks
AddTask(q→p)q:[p], p:[]phase 1 collects the new material
unionp:[q], q:[p]both edges survive
DFSq → greyvisit p
p → greyp depends on q
q is greyback edge → throw
afterp:[q], q:[]manager unchanged — still 2 tasks, 0 edges added
What the state tells youThe bug in my first version was in the union row. It built merged by copying the existing edges and then assigning the new ones, so p:[q] was overwritten by p:[] and the DFS from q found q → p and stopped. No cycle reported, the edge accepted, and the manager left in a state where p and q each wait on the other forever — detected only when nothing ever became runnable. One character of difference between merged[k] = new and a union, and it is the reason that test exists.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The interface and the exception 5 lines
Q15_TaskManager.cs
// Q15 — a task manager over a dependency graph. public interface ITask { string GetId(); IList<ITask> GetSubTasks(); // these must all finish before the task itself can run }
The manager 169 lines
Q15_TaskManager.cs
public class TaskManager { private readonly Dictionary<string, ITask> _tasks = new(StringComparer.Ordinal); private readonly Dictionary<string, List<string>> _dependsOn = new(StringComparer.Ordinal); private readonly Dictionary<string, List<string>> _blocks = new(StringComparer.Ordinal); private readonly Dictionary<string, int> _outstanding = new(StringComparer.Ordinal); private readonly Queue<string> _ready = new(); private readonly HashSet<string> _queued = new(StringComparer.Ordinal); // in _ready right now private readonly HashSet<string> _issued = new(StringComparer.Ordinal); // handed out already private readonly HashSet<string> _completed = new(StringComparer.Ordinal); public int ReadyCount => _ready.Count; public int TaskCount => _tasks.Count; /// Registers the task and every subtask reachable from it. /// Throws CycleException and changes nothing if the result would contain a cycle. public void AddTask(ITask task) { if (task == null) throw new ArgumentNullException(nameof(task)); // ---- phase 1: collect the new material without touching any field var newTasks = new Dictionary<string, ITask>(StringComparer.Ordinal); var newEdges = new Dictionary<string, List<string>>(StringComparer.Ordinal); Collect(task, newTasks, newEdges); // ---- phase 2: cycle-check the MERGED graph, still without committing // The merged graph is the UNION of both edge sets. Overwriting instead of unioning was a // real bug: re-adding an existing task with new subtasks would drop its original edges, // and a cycle closed across two AddTask calls would go undetected. var merged = new Dictionary<string, List<string>>(StringComparer.Ordinal); foreach (var kv in _dependsOn) merged[kv.Key] = new List<string>(kv.Value); foreach (var kv in newEdges) { if (!merged.TryGetValue(kv.Key, out var deps)) merged[kv.Key] = deps = new List<string>(); foreach (string d in kv.Value) if (!deps.Contains(d)) deps.Add(d); } var cycle = FindCycleFrom(task.GetId(), merged); if (cycle != null) throw new CycleException(cycle); // Adding a dependency to a task that has already been handed out or finished is // ambiguous: re-run it, or leave it? Refuse rather than silently pick one. foreach (var kv in newEdges) { if (!_issued.Contains(kv.Key) && !_completed.Contains(kv.Key)) continue; List<string> known = _dependsOn[kv.Key]; foreach (string dep in kv.Value) if (!known.Contains(dep)) throw new InvalidOperationException( $"Task '{kv.Key}' has already been issued; cannot add dependency '{dep}'."); } // ---- phase 3: commit. Nothing below can fail, so the manager is never half-updated. foreach (var kv in newTasks) { if (_tasks.ContainsKey(kv.Key)) continue; // already registered; keep its state _tasks[kv.Key] = kv.Value; _dependsOn[kv.Key] = new List<string>(); _outstanding[kv.Key] = 0; } foreach (var kv in newEdges) { string id = kv.Key; List<string> known = _dependsOn[id]; foreach (string dep in kv.Value) { if (known.Contains(dep)) continue; // the same edge declared twice known.Add(dep); if (!_blocks.TryGetValue(dep, out var waiting)) _blocks[dep] = waiting = new List<string>(); waiting.Add(id); if (!_completed.Contains(dep)) _outstanding[id]++; } } foreach (string id in newTasks.Keys) if (_outstanding[id] == 0) MarkReady(id); } private void MarkReady(string id) { if (_issued.Contains(id) || _completed.Contains(id)) return; if (_queued.Add(id)) _ready.Enqueue(id); // never queue the same task twice } /// The next task whose subtasks have all completed, or null if none is runnable right now. /// null and "nothing left" are different states: use TaskCount and _completed to tell them apart. public ITask PollNextTask() { while (_ready.Count > 0) { string id = _ready.Dequeue(); _queued.Remove(id); if (_completed.Contains(id)) continue; if (_outstanding[id] > 0) continue; // gained a dependency after being queued if (!_issued.Add(id)) continue; // already handed to a worker return _tasks[id]; } return null; } /// The interface as given has no completion signal, so nothing would ever become runnable /// after the first batch. This is the missing half. public void CompleteTask(string id) { if (!_tasks.ContainsKey(id)) throw new KeyNotFoundException(id); if (!_completed.Add(id)) return; // idempotent: a retry must be harmless if (!_blocks.TryGetValue(id, out var waiting)) return; foreach (string waiter in waiting) if (--_outstanding[waiter] == 0) MarkReady(waiter); } /// A worker that failed must hand the task back, or it is issued once and lost. public void RequeueTask(string id) { if (!_tasks.ContainsKey(id)) throw new KeyNotFoundException(id); if (_completed.Contains(id)) return; _issued.Remove(id); if (_outstanding[id] == 0) MarkReady(id); } // Iterative walk of the new subtree. Iterative on purpose: a deep chain would blow the // stack in a recursive version, and dependency chains are exactly the shape that gets deep. private static void Collect(ITask root, Dictionary<string, ITask> tasks, Dictionary<string, List<string>> edges) { var stack = new Stack<ITask>(); stack.Push(root); while (stack.Count > 0) { ITask cur = stack.Pop(); string id = cur.GetId(); if (id == null) throw new ArgumentException("Task id must not be null."); if (tasks.ContainsKey(id)) continue; tasks[id] = cur; var deps = new List<string>(); foreach (ITask sub in cur.GetSubTasks() ?? new List<ITask>()) { if (sub == null) continue; deps.Add(sub.GetId()); stack.Push(sub); } edges[id] = deps; } } // Three-colour DFS. Grey means "on the current path", so meeting grey is a back edge. private static List<string> FindCycleFrom(string start, Dictionary<string, List<string>> edges) { var state = new Dictionary<string, int>(StringComparer.Ordinal); // 1 = grey, 2 = black var path = new List<string>(); bool Visit(string id) { state[id] = 1; path.Add(id); if (edges.TryGetValue(id, out var deps)) foreach (string next in deps) { state.TryGetValue(next, out int s); if (s == 1) { path.Add(next); return true; } // back edge if (s == 0 && Visit(next)) return true; } state[id] = 2; path.RemoveAt(path.Count - 1); return false; } if (!Visit(start)) return null; int first = path.IndexOf(path[^1]); // trim the tail to the loop return path.GetRange(first, path.Count - first); } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
The three phases, and why the order is the design

Phase 1 collects into locals. Phase 2 validates. Phase 3 commits, and nothing in phase 3 can fail — it is only dictionary writes. So a rejected addTask cannot leave the manager half-updated, and there is no unwind path to get wrong. The test asserts TaskCount is unchanged after every throw, because this property is invisible otherwise.

The union in phase 2 — where the real bug was

foreach (string d in kv.Value) if (!deps.Contains(d)) deps.Add(d); rather than merged[kv.Key] = kv.Value. Assignment dropped an existing task’s original edges, so a cycle closed across two calls went undetected. This was found by a test, not by reading. The comment in the code says so, because a future reader will otherwise be tempted to simplify it back.

Refusing a new dependency on an issued task

If a task has already been handed to a worker and someone adds a dependency to it, should it re-run? There is no good answer, so it throws InvalidOperationException. The check sits in phase 2, before any commit, so the refusal is atomic like the cycle rejection. Guessing either way here would be worse than refusing.

MarkReady and the _queued set

A task can reach zero outstanding dependencies more than once — add an edge, complete it, add another. Without _queued, it would sit in _ready twice and PollNextTask would hand it out twice. The _issued check inside MarkReady covers the other direction.

Why PollNextTask re-verifies

It skips entries that are completed, still blocked, or already issued. Entries in _ready can go stale: a task queued as ready can gain a dependency before it is polled. Re-checking _outstanding[id] > 0 at the point of use is cheaper and more robust than trying to remove it from the middle of the queue.

CompleteTask is idempotent

if (!_completed.Add(id)) return; — a retried completion, which any distributed worker will eventually send, must not decrement the waiters twice. Double-decrementing would drive _outstanding negative and a task would become ready before its dependencies finished: silent, and catastrophic.

Collect is iterative on purpose

An explicit Stack<ITask> rather than recursion, because dependency chains are exactly the shape that gets deep. Note that FindCycleFrom is recursive, which is the standard shape for three-colour DFS and is the one place a very deep graph could still overflow — worth naming rather than claiming the whole class is stack-safe.

FindCycleFrom returns the cycle, not a bool

path.IndexOf(path[^1]) trims the trailing tail so the exception names the actual loop. "There is a cycle" is not actionable; "a → b → c → a" is. It costs a list and it is what makes the error message worth reading.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
AddTaskO(Vₙ + Eₘ)O(V + E)Collects the new closure, then DFS over the merged graph
PollNextTaskO(1) amortisedO(1)Pops until a valid entry; each is skipped once
CompleteTaskO(waiters)O(1)One decrement per task blocked on it
whole runO(V + E) total for polls and completionsO(V + E)Each edge relaxed exactly once across the run
RequeueTaskO(1)O(1)Clears the issued flag and re-queues
The part worth saying out loudThe cycle check dominates AddTask and it is not free. It is O(V + E) over the merged graph each time, so adding n tasks one at a time is O(n(V+E)) overall — worse than the single O(V+E) of a batch topological sort. That is the price of rejecting at insert time, and it is the right price because the alternative is accepting a cycle you cannot attribute. Restricting the DFS to the component reachable from the new task, which is what the code does, keeps it proportional to the affected subgraph rather than the whole manager — worth saying, because it is the difference between acceptable and not.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Null taskThrows ArgumentNullException.
Null idThrows during Collect, before any commit.
Self-dependencyCycleException. Caught by the grey check with no special case.
Cycle within one addTaskCaught; nothing registered.
Cycle across two addTask callsCaught, because the check runs over the union. The bug case.
Diamond (two paths to one task)Registered once, and completing it unblocks both parents. Must not be reported as a cycle — that is the grey-versus-black distinction.
pollNextTask with nothing runnableReturns null. Note that null does not distinguish "blocked" from "finished"; a caller needing that should check TaskCount against completed.
Polling the same task twiceIssued once. RequeueTask is the way back.
CompleteTask on an unknown idThrows KeyNotFoundException.
CompleteTask twiceIdempotent — the second is a no-op, and must be.
Adding a dependency to an issued taskRefused, rather than guessed at.
A very deep chainCollect is iterative and safe; the recursive cycle check is the remaining depth risk.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 5 follow-ups.
“Several workers poll concurrently. Make it safe.” · likely pushback
Thinking

Almost every field is mutated by both pollNextTask and completeTask, so the shared state is everything. Worth being concrete about what the race actually produces.

Approach

One lock around each public method is the right first answer: the critical sections are dictionary operations, so they are short. The _issued set is what makes concurrent polling correct — without it two workers get the same task. For throughput, _ready could be a BlockingCollection so workers block instead of polling and getting null, which also removes a busy-wait loop from every caller.

Trade-off

A single lock serialises polls, which is unlikely to matter because the tasks themselves are the expensive part. Finer-grained locking is genuinely hard here: the invariant spans four dictionaries, so per-task locks would need a consistent acquisition order to avoid deadlock, and the ready-queue transition would still need to be atomic with the outstanding-count decrement. I would take the single lock and measure.

TimeSpace
unchanged plus lock acquisitionunchanged
“A worker takes a task and dies. What happens?” · likely pushback
Thinking

With _issued and nothing else, the task is lost permanently and every task downstream of it waits forever. Worth raising unprompted, because it is the failure mode that turns a correct component into a stuck one.

Approach

RequeueTask(id) clears the issued flag and re-queues if the dependencies are still satisfied. To detect the death rather than be told about it, issue a lease with a deadline and requeue automatically when it expires — which needs a timer, and that timer is Q7.

Trade-off

Explicit requeue is simple and needs a cooperative worker or a supervisor. Leases are automatic and introduce at-least-once execution: a slow worker can have its lease expire while still running, so the task runs twice. That forces tasks to be idempotent, which is a requirement on the caller rather than on this class, and it should be stated as such rather than hidden.

Requeue
Q15_TaskManager.cs
/// A worker that failed must hand the task back, or it is issued once and lost. public void RequeueTask(string id) { if (!_tasks.ContainsKey(id)) throw new KeyNotFoundException(id); if (_completed.Contains(id)) return; _issued.Remove(id); if (_outstanding[id] == 0) MarkReady(id); }
TimeSpace
O(1)O(1) plus a timer per lease
“A task fails. Should its dependents run?” · likely pushback
Thinking

The specification says nothing about failure, which means the contract is incomplete in a second place. Both answers are defensible and the choice belongs to the caller.

Approach

FailTask(id) with a policy. Abort: mark the whole dependent subtree unrunnable and report it, which is what a build system does — a failed compile must not produce a deploy. Retry: requeue with a backoff and an attempt limit, then abort. Continue: treat failure as completion, which is almost always wrong and occasionally right for optional work.

Trade-off

Abort is the safe default and it needs a reverse traversal to find everything downstream, which _blocks already supports. Retry needs the attempt count and a timer. I would implement abort, expose the policy as a parameter, and say clearly that treating a failure as a completion is the one option I would not offer without the caller asking for it by name.

TimeSpace
O(dependent subtree) for abortO(1) extra
“Why detect the cycle on insert rather than at execution time?” · likely pushback
Thinking

The question is really "do you know what you bought". It is not free, so the justification matters.

Approach

Insert-time detection gives an error at the point where the caller can act on it — they are holding the task that caused it, and the exception can name the loop. Execution-time detection means the bad edge was accepted, so the failure surfaces later, in a different stack, attributed to nothing in particular; and by then you cannot tell which of the accepted tasks was at fault.

Trade-off

The cost is O(V + E) per add instead of once, making n adds O(n(V+E)) rather than O(V+E). Restricting the DFS to the reachable component keeps that proportional to the affected subgraph. If adds were extremely hot and cycles genuinely impossible by construction, batching the validation would be defensible — but the specification says addTask throws, so it is not an option here.

TimeSpace
O(V + E) per add, versus O(V + E) once for a batchO(V + E)
“Priorities: some ready tasks matter more than others.” · likely pushback
Thinking

A small change with one non-obvious consequence.

Approach

Replace _ready with a priority queue keyed on priority. Readiness logic is untouched — only the order among already-ready tasks changes.

Trade-off

O(log n) instead of O(1) per poll, which is irrelevant next to running the task. The real consequence is starvation: a steady stream of high-priority tasks can keep a low-priority one waiting forever. Ageing — raising effective priority with waiting time — is the standard fix, and mentioning starvation unprompted is the signal here, because the data-structure swap is trivial and the fairness problem is not.

TimeSpace
O(log n) per pollunchanged
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Spotting an incomplete contract — the missing completion signal. The primary thing being tested.
  • Directed cycle detection — and specifically the grey-versus-black distinction, so a diamond is not mistaken for a cycle.
  • Atomicity — validate then commit, so a rejection changes nothing.
  • Incremental versus batch — Kahn’s algorithm run online, and knowing what that costs.
  • Lifecycle thinking — issued, completed, failed, requeued. The states the interface does not mention.
  • Concurrency — identifying that the invariant spans four dictionaries.
A senior engineer might say
Builds the dependency graph, detects cycles with a DFS on insert, tracks in-degrees, and produces a working poll/complete cycle.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and: raises the missing completion signal before writing code; structures addTask as collect-validate-commit and says why, rather than unwinding on failure; runs the cycle check over the union of old and new edges and can say what breaks otherwise; distinguishes issued from completed and asks what happens when a worker dies; refuses the ambiguous case (a new dependency on an issued task) rather than guessing; and justifies insert-time detection with what it costs. Noticing that the given interface cannot make progress is the single highest-value observation available on this question.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Before I write anything — the interface as given cannot make progress. pollNextTask returns a task whose dependencies are complete, but nothing ever says a task became complete. So the first call returns a leaf and then either the same leaf forever or nothing. I need a completion signal. I will add CompleteTask by id; the alternative is pollNextTask marking the task complete when it returns, which loses the ability to fail.
With that, the structure is Kahn's algorithm run incrementally. Each task has a count of dependencies not yet complete, and I keep the reverse map too, so completing a task is a decrement of everything waiting on it. Anything hitting zero goes on the ready queue.
Cycle detection is the interesting half, because you want it on add rather than at execution. A batch topological sort detects cycles by failing to sort, and I cannot do that here — I have to reject the edge at the moment it arrives. So it is a three-colour DFS: grey means on the current path, and hitting grey is a back edge. Hitting black is fine, that is a diamond.
The part I would emphasise is atomicity. I collect the new material into locals, validate, and only then commit — and nothing in the commit can fail, it is just dictionary writes. If I registered as I walked and unwound on failure, the unwind is where the bug would be, and a half-registered rejected task leaves the manager in a state nobody can reason about.
One thing I got wrong first time and want to flag. The cycle check has to run over the union of the existing edges and the new ones. I originally overwrote instead of unioning, and a cycle closed across two separate addTask calls went undetected — add p depends on q, then add q depends on p, and it accepted it. A test caught it, not reading the code.
I also separate issued from completed, so two polls do not hand out the same task. Which raises the question of what happens if a worker takes a task and dies — without something, that task and everything downstream waits forever. I would add an explicit requeue, and if you want it automatic, a lease with a deadline. That makes execution at-least-once, so tasks have to be idempotent, and that is a requirement on the caller I would state rather than bury.
And one case I deliberately refuse rather than guess: adding a dependency to a task that has already been handed out. Should it re-run? There is no good answer, so it throws.
