Notice the hole in the contract first, then build the incremental Kahn plus insert-time cycle check, and use a real bug to show why the union matters.

1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Merge two trees containing: • key • value The value represents the sum of this node and its child node values. When merging: • absent branches are created • existing branch values are summed • child ordering is arbitrary • keys are unique among siblings Implement: merge(TreeNode s, TreeNode t) Follow-ups: 1. Best serialization format and trade-offs 2. Transform result into a side-heavy tree 3. Concurrent merges 4. Same tree used as source by one thread and target by another 5. Very large serialized trees (1GB+) 6. Performance optimization 7. Memory considerations
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Supplied with four precise rules and seven follow-ups, which is more than any other question here. Follow-ups 3 and 4 are concurrency, 5 and 7 are memory, 1 is encoding — so most of the question is not the recursion.
Reported elsewhere
"Merge two keyed trees, summing values (n-ary variant of Merge Two Binary Trees)" — on your own list of past LinkedIn questions. First-party, and already worked in the coding page.
Reported elsewhere
Tree Fusion was one of the visible interviewdb titles before you bought access, and was guessed in this workspace as "probably a tree merge, unconfirmed". The full statement now confirms that guess.
LeetCode
617 Merge Two Binary Trees (on the supplied list) is the binary case. 297 Serialize and Deserialize Binary Tree (also supplied) is follow-up 1. 100 Same Tree is the lockstep two-tree recursion.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported, independently first-party reported, and it resolves an earlier unknown title in this workspace. Highest confidence on the page.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pick"The value represents the sum of this node and its child node values" is an invariant, not just a description, and it has a consequence worth stating: the value is partly derived. A node’s own contribution is value − sum(children.value). The good news is that the invariant is preserved automatically by summing. If both inputs satisfy value = own + Σ children, then adding them gives (own₁+own₂) + Σ (children₁+children₂), which satisfies it too. So nothing has to be recomputed — and saying that is better than either silently relying on it or writing a fix-up pass. What the statement does not say: whether the merge may mutate its inputs, and what to do if the two roots have different keys. I build a new tree and mutate nothing — which is what makes follow-up 4 answerable — and I throw on mismatched roots rather than inventing a parent.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

Two trees describing the same hierarchy, each holding partial totals. Merge them into one tree whose values are the combined totals, creating branches that exist on only one side.

What we are being asked to compute

A merged tree. Same shape as the union of the two inputs; values summed where both have a node.

Constraints that matter
  • Children are identified by key, not by position. That is the whole difference from LeetCode 617 and it is what makes child lookup a dictionary probe.
  • Keys are unique among siblings, so a key identifies at most one child — which is what lets the children be a dictionary at all.
  • Child ordering is arbitrary, so the output order carries no meaning. That matters for serialisation, where you have to choose an order.
  • The value invariant is additive, so summing preserves it.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
Neither input is mutated; the merge returns a new tree.The statement does not say. It is what makes Merge(x, x) safe and concurrent readers safe, and it is the answer to follow-up 4 — so it is worth choosing deliberately and saying why.
Both roots have the same key.Otherwise there is no single tree to produce. I throw; the alternative is inventing a synthetic parent, which silently changes the data.
Values are non-negative and fit in a long.int would overflow on a merge of large totals, and the whole point is that values are sums. Negative values would break the IsConsistent check but not the merge.
Trees are deep enough to matter.Follow-up 5 says 1 GB, so recursion depth is a real risk and an iterative version has to exist. Assuming shallow trees would make follow-up 5 unanswerable.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Tree sroot 10 { a 6 { x 2 }, b 1 }own(root) = 10 − 6 − 1 = 3
Tree troot 12 { a 4, c 5 }own(root) = 12 − 4 − 5 = 3
Mergedroot 22 { a 10 { x 2 }, b 1, c 5 }
a exists in both6 + 4 = 10 — summed
b only in scarried across unchanged
c only in tbranch created
x under a, only in sgrandchild survives the merge
Where these came fromThe statement referred to a supplied example that did not come through, so this one is mine — built to exercise all four stated rules at once, plus a grandchild to confirm the recursion goes deeper than one level. The invariant is checked on the result by IsConsistent, and the iterative and recursive merges are asserted to agree.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
Two observations. The first is small and the second is what the follow-ups are built on. One: children keyed rather than positional turns the merge into three cases. At each node, look at every child key on both sides. In both → recurse. Only in s → copy the branch. Only in t → copy the branch. That is the same three-case shape as merging two sorted lists or comparing two trees, and with a dictionary of children each case is an O(1) probe. LeetCode 617 has the same three cases hidden in its null checks; making the children keyed just makes them explicit. Two: build a new tree rather than mutating either input. This looks like a style choice and it is the load-bearing decision of the whole question, because it answers three of the seven supplied follow-ups at once. Follow-up 4 asks what happens when one thread uses a tree as a source while another uses it as a target. If the merge mutates the target, the first thread is reading a tree that is changing underneath it — torn reads, double counting, and if the two are the same tree, potentially a dictionary being enumerated while it is written, which throws. If the merge only ever reads its inputs, all of that disappears: any number of threads may merge from the same trees simultaneously, and Merge(x, x) is well defined. So: choose the pure version first and let it answer the concurrency follow-ups, rather than writing the in-place version and then trying to lock it. The in-place variant is worth keeping to name the trap — which is why MergeInto is in the listing and refuses a self-merge explicitly. And the invariant needs no maintenance, because summation is additive. That is worth noticing rather than defending: if the invariant had been "value is the maximum of this node and its children", merging would have needed a fix-up pass, and this question would be materially harder.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Serialise both trees to flat lists of (path, value) pairs, merge the lists by path, then rebuild a tree from the merged paths.

What it costs

O(n log n) for the sort, plus O(n · depth) to build and compare the path strings. O(n) space, with the paths often larger than the trees.

Why it stops workingIt works, and it is genuinely how you would do this out of core for follow-up 5 — so it is worth stating rather than dismissing. In memory it is strictly worse: it materialises a path string per node, which for a deep tree costs more than the tree, and it pays a sort for an ordering the recursion gets for free. The recursion is O(n) with no sort and no path strings. But keep this approach in your pocket, because it is the external-merge answer.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTTwo MetaNode roots, each with Dictionary<string, MetaNode> children
↓
TRANSFORMATIONNone. The keyed children are already the index the merge needs.
↓
DATA STRUCTUREDictionary<string, MetaNode> per node for O(1) child lookup
↓
ALGORITHMRecurse in lockstep: sum the values, recurse on shared keys, clone branches present on one side only
↓
OUTPUTA new tree; both inputs untouched
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
Dictionary<string, MetaNode> for childrenKeys are unique among siblings, so a dictionary is the exact model, and "is this key on the other side" becomes O(1). A list of children would make the merge O(c²) per node.
StringComparer.OrdinalKeys are identifiers. Culture-aware comparison is both slower and, in the general case, capable of treating distinct keys as equal — which here would merge two different branches into one and silently corrupt the totals.
A new tree rather than in-placeThe decision that answers follow-ups 3 and 4. Inputs are read-only, so concurrent merges from shared trees need no locking at all.
long valuesThe values are sums of sums. int overflows, silently and negatively.
An iterative variant alongside the recursive oneFollow-up 5 specifies 1 GB trees, and the recursion depth is the height. The iterative version with an explicit stack is the one that survives a 60,000-deep chain — tested.
The algorithm
  1. Either side null → return a clone of the other.
  2. Root keys differ → throw; there is no single tree to produce.
  3. Create a new node with s.Value + t.Value.
  4. For each child of s: present in t → recurse; otherwise clone the branch.
  5. For each child of t not already handled → clone the branch.
  6. Return the new node.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

The example. s = root 10 { a 6 { x 2 }, b 1 } and t = root 12 { a 4, c 5 }.

AtstActionResult
root1012sumnew node root = 22
child a64in both → recursea = 10
child x under a2—only in s → clone the branchx = 2
child b1—only in s → cloneb = 1
child c—5only in t → clonec = 5
doneroot 22 { a 10 { x 2 }, b 1, c 5 }
What the state tells youCheck the invariant on the result: own(root) = 22 − 10 − 1 − 5 = 6, which is exactly own(s.root) + own(t.root) = 3 + 3. Nothing recomputed it — it fell out of the summation, which is the additivity point made concrete. Also note that s still reads 10 and t still has two children: nothing was mutated, and that is what makes the concurrency follow-ups easy.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The node 8 lines
Q17_MergeTrees.cs
// Q17 — merge two metadata trees keyed by child key, summing values. public class MetaNode { public string Key; public long Value; // this node plus the sum of its children public Dictionary<string, MetaNode> Children = new(StringComparer.Ordinal); public MetaNode(string key, long value) { Key = key; Value = value; } }
The merge, plus the in-place trap and the iterative version 96 lines
Q17_MergeTrees.cs
public static class MetaTreeMerge { /// Pure merge: builds a new tree and mutates neither input. /// That is what makes Merge(x, x) safe, and what makes concurrent readers safe. public static MetaNode Merge(MetaNode s, MetaNode t) { if (s == null) return Clone(t); if (t == null) return Clone(s); if (!string.Equals(s.Key, t.Key, StringComparison.Ordinal)) throw new ArgumentException($"Root keys differ: '{s.Key}' vs '{t.Key}'."); // Summing at every node preserves the invariant for free: if value = own + children on // both sides, then the sums also satisfy value = own + children. Nothing to recompute. var merged = new MetaNode(s.Key, s.Value + t.Value); foreach (var kv in s.Children) { merged.Children[kv.Key] = t.Children.TryGetValue(kv.Key, out var other) ? Merge(kv.Value, other) // present in both: recurse : Clone(kv.Value); // only in s: whole branch carried over } foreach (var kv in t.Children) if (!merged.Children.ContainsKey(kv.Key)) merged.Children[kv.Key] = Clone(kv.Value); // only in t: branch created return merged; } public static MetaNode Clone(MetaNode n) { if (n == null) return null; var copy = new MetaNode(n.Key, n.Value); foreach (var kv in n.Children) copy.Children[kv.Key] = Clone(kv.Value); return copy; } /// In-place variant: cheaper, and dangerous. Kept here to name the trap rather than hide it. /// MergeInto(x, x) double-counts and can enumerate a dictionary it is writing to. public static void MergeInto(MetaNode target, MetaNode source) { if (target == null || source == null) return; if (ReferenceEquals(target, source)) throw new InvalidOperationException("Self-merge in place would double-count."); target.Value += source.Value; foreach (var kv in source.Children) { if (target.Children.TryGetValue(kv.Key, out var child)) MergeInto(child, kv.Value); else target.Children[kv.Key] = Clone(kv.Value); } } /// Iterative merge for trees deep enough to overflow the stack — the 1 GB follow-up. public static MetaNode MergeIterative(MetaNode s, MetaNode t) { if (s == null) return Clone(t); if (t == null) return Clone(s); var root = new MetaNode(s.Key, s.Value + t.Value); var work = new Stack<(MetaNode S, MetaNode T, MetaNode Out)>(); work.Push((s, t, root)); while (work.Count > 0) { var (a, b, into) = work.Pop(); foreach (var kv in a.Children) { if (b.Children.TryGetValue(kv.Key, out var other)) { var child = new MetaNode(kv.Key, kv.Value.Value + other.Value); into.Children[kv.Key] = child; work.Push((kv.Value, other, child)); } else { into.Children[kv.Key] = Clone(kv.Value); } } foreach (var kv in b.Children) if (!into.Children.ContainsKey(kv.Key)) into.Children[kv.Key] = Clone(kv.Value); } return root; } /// The stated invariant, checkable. Worth writing: it is the assertion a reviewer asks for. public static bool IsConsistent(MetaNode n, out string offender) { offender = null; if (n == null) return true; long childSum = 0; foreach (var kv in n.Children) { if (!IsConsistent(kv.Value, out offender)) return false; childSum += kv.Value.Value; } if (n.Value < childSum) { offender = n.Key; return false; } // own contribution cannot be negative return true; } public static long OwnValue(MetaNode n) { long childSum = 0; foreach (var kv in n.Children) childSum += kv.Value.Value; return n.Value - childSum; } }
Follow-up 2 — the side-heavy form 30 lines
Q17_MergeTrees.cs
public static class SideHeavy { public static BinaryMetaNode Encode(MetaNode n) { if (n == null) return null; var node = new BinaryMetaNode(n.Key, n.Value); BinaryMetaNode prev = null; // Deterministic order: the source dictionary has none, and a serialised tree needs one. foreach (var kv in n.Children.OrderBy(k => k.Key, StringComparer.Ordinal)) { var child = Encode(kv.Value); if (prev == null) node.Left = child; else prev.Right = child; prev = child; } return node; } public static MetaNode Decode(BinaryMetaNode b) { if (b == null) return null; var node = new MetaNode(b.Key, b.Value); // Walk the sibling chain at this level; Decode(child) handles that child's own subtree. for (BinaryMetaNode child = b.Left; child != null; child = child.Right) node.Children[child.Key] = Decode(child); return node; } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
Why Merge allocates a new node before looking at any child

new MetaNode(s.Key, s.Value + t.Value) first, then the children are filled in. Nothing reads from the inputs after they have been written to, because they are never written to at all. That is the property the concurrency follow-ups need, and it comes from this line rather than from a lock.

The two loops, and the ContainsKey guard on the second

The first loop handles "in s" — recursing when t also has the key, cloning otherwise. The second handles "only in t", guarded by !merged.Children.ContainsKey(kv.Key) so shared keys are not processed twice. Without the guard, a shared child would be overwritten by a clone of t’s branch and s’s subtree would vanish — with a plausible-looking result.

MergeInto exists to name the trap, not to be used

It throws on ReferenceEquals(target, source). Self-merging in place double-counts, and worse, it enumerates source.Children while writing to target.Children — the same dictionary — which throws InvalidOperationException at an unpredictable point, leaving the tree half-merged. This is follow-up 4 in miniature and it is why the pure version is the answer.

MergeIterative and the work stack of triples

Stack<(MetaNode S, MetaNode T, MetaNode Out)> — the output node is carried alongside the two inputs, so children can be attached without a parent pointer. Tested on a 60,000-deep chain, which the recursive version would not survive. Note that Clone is still recursive, so a deep branch present on only one side would still overflow — an honest gap, and the fix is an iterative clone.

IsConsistent checks the invariant, and only one direction

It verifies value >= sum(children), i.e. that a node’s own contribution is non-negative. It cannot verify more without knowing the intended own-values. Writing the invariant as a checkable function is worth doing precisely because the statement gave it as prose — it is the assertion a reviewer would ask for.

SideHeavy.Encode orders siblings before encoding

OrderBy(k => k.Key, StringComparer.Ordinal). The statement says child ordering is arbitrary, and a dictionary has no order at all — so any serialised or linked form must choose one, or two encodings of the same tree will differ. That determinism is what makes the encoding comparable, hashable and diffable, and it is the detail that connects follow-up 2 to follow-up 1.

Decode walks the sibling chain, and the recursion handles the subtree

for (child = b.Left; child != null; child = child.Right) iterates one level; Decode(child) handles that child’s own children via child.Left. Getting this wrong — recursing in a way that also follows Right — duplicates siblings into the wrong level, which is why the round trip is asserted in the tests rather than eyeballed.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
MergeO(nₛ + nₜ)O(nₛ + nₜ)Every node of both inputs visited once; the output is a full copy
MergeIntoO(nₛ + nₜ)O(nₜ) new nodes onlyReuses the target, so only cloned branches allocate
MergeIterativeO(nₛ + nₜ)O(n) heap stackSame work; the stack moves from the thread stack to the heap
CloneO(n)O(n)One new node per source node
IsConsistentO(n)O(h)One pass, recursive
SideHeavy.EncodeO(n log c)O(n)The log c is sorting each node’s c children
The part worth saying out loudThe pure merge allocates a node for every node in both inputs, and that is its real cost — not the time. For 1 GB trees the copy is the problem, not the traversal: you need roughly 2× the memory of the result live at once. MergeInto avoids it by reusing the target, which is exactly the trade the concurrency follow-ups make you choose about. Naming memory rather than time as the binding constraint is the right framing for follow-ups 5 and 7.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Either input nullReturns a clone of the other. A clone, not the same reference, so the caller cannot accidentally alias an input into the result.
Both nullReturns null.
Root keys differThrows. Inventing a synthetic parent would change the data silently.
Merge(x, x)Safe — values double, x untouched. The pure version handles it with no special case.
MergeInto(x, x)Refused explicitly. Double-counts and can throw mid-merge, leaving the tree half-updated.
Deep chain (60,000 nodes)Recursive merge overflows; MergeIterative handles it. Tested. Clone is still recursive — a stated gap.
One tree a single nodeThe other’s children are all cloned across.
Value overflowlong. int would wrap negative on large sums.
Duplicate sibling keysImpossible by the stated constraint, and the dictionary enforces it.
Empty children on both sidesA leaf whose value is the sum of the two.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 7 follow-ups.
“What is the best serialization format, and what are the trade-offs?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

This is LeetCode 297, which is on the supplied number list. The thing that makes it interesting here is that children are keyed and unordered, which 297 does not have to deal with.

Approach

Whatever the format, two problems have to be solved: key and value per node, and where a node’s children end. Positional null markers (297’s approach) do not work, because the arity is unbounded — so either a child count per node or an explicit terminator. A length-prefixed binary format is the right answer for size and speed: [keyLen][key][value][childCount] then the children, depth-first. JSON is the right answer for debuggability and for crossing a service boundary you do not own. Protobuf sits between and buys schema evolution, which matters if this format outlives the code. And sibling order must be canonicalised — sorted by key — or two serialisations of the same tree differ, which breaks hashing, diffing and any content-addressed cache.

Trade-off

Binary is compact and fast and unreadable, and versioning it is on you. JSON is roughly 3–5× larger and parses far slower, and any engineer can debug it with cat. For 1 GB trees the size difference is the deciding factor and I would go binary with a version byte; for an API I would take JSON and not think about it again. The canonical ordering is not a trade — it is required either way.

TimeSpace
O(n) to write and readO(depth) streaming, rather than O(n)
“Transform the result into a side-heavy tree.” · supplied with the question
Thinking

"Side-heavy" is not standard terminology, so the honest move is to state the interpretation. The one that makes sense for an n-ary tree is the left-child / right-sibling encoding, which is LeetCode 431 and turns an n-ary tree into a binary one that leans right.

Approach

Each node’s Left is its first child and its Right is its next sibling. Every n-ary tree maps to a binary tree uniquely and reversibly, and the tree leans right by exactly the width of the widest sibling list. Siblings are sorted first, so the encoding is deterministic.

Trade-off

The binary form has fixed arity, so it serialises with positional markers and fits any binary-tree algorithm — which is the point. The cost is depth: a node with 1,000 children becomes a right chain of 1,000, so a recursive walk over the encoded form is far deeper than over the original. If the interviewer meant something else by side-heavy — a deliberate imbalance, or all children on one side — I would ask, because the word is not standard and guessing would waste the answer.

Left-child / right-sibling
Q17_MergeTrees.cs
public static class SideHeavy { public static BinaryMetaNode Encode(MetaNode n) { if (n == null) return null; var node = new BinaryMetaNode(n.Key, n.Value); BinaryMetaNode prev = null; // Deterministic order: the source dictionary has none, and a serialised tree needs one. foreach (var kv in n.Children.OrderBy(k => k.Key, StringComparer.Ordinal)) { var child = Encode(kv.Value); if (prev == null) node.Left = child; else prev.Right = child; prev = child; } return node; } public static MetaNode Decode(BinaryMetaNode b) { if (b == null) return null; var node = new MetaNode(b.Key, b.Value); // Walk the sibling chain at this level; Decode(child) handles that child's own subtree. for (BinaryMetaNode child = b.Left; child != null; child = child.Right) node.Children[child.Key] = Decode(child); return node; } }
TimeSpace
O(n log c) to encode, O(n) to decodeO(n)
“Several threads merge concurrently.” · supplied with the question
Thinking

With the pure merge, this is nearly a non-question — and explaining why it is a non-question is the answer.

Approach

Merge only reads its inputs and only writes nodes it has just allocated, so any number of threads may merge from the same trees with no synchronisation at all. Nothing is shared and mutable. The work also parallelises naturally: each child subtree is independent, so Parallel.ForEach over the children of the root, with a depth cutoff so small subtrees are merged inline rather than scheduled.

Trade-off

Purity buys lock-free concurrency and costs a full copy. MergeInto avoids the copy and immediately needs locking — and locking a tree correctly means either one lock (no concurrency) or per-node locks with a consistent acquisition order (deadlock risk, and hard to review). Parallelising has a real cutoff: below a few thousand nodes the task overhead exceeds the merge, so measure rather than parallelise everything.

TimeSpace
O(n) work, O(depth) span with full parallelismO(n) for the result
“The same tree is used as a source by one thread and as a target by another.” · supplied with the question
Thinking

This is the sharpest of the seven, and with the pure version the answer is already in place — which is the reward for having chosen it.

Approach

With Merge, there is no target. Nothing is written to either input, so the scenario cannot arise: both threads read, both produce their own result. With MergeInto it is a genuine race, and it is worth being concrete about the damage. The reader sees some children already summed and others not — a torn read, producing a total that corresponds to no point in time. And if the source and target are the same object, a dictionary is enumerated while being written, which throws InvalidOperationException partway through and leaves the tree half-merged with no way to roll back.

Trade-off

If in-place is unavoidable for memory reasons, the answer is copy-on-write with an atomic publish: build the merged tree, then swap the root reference with Interlocked.Exchange. Readers hold whichever root they picked up and see a consistent snapshot; there is no locking and no torn read. The cost is that the copy is back, which is the honest circle: you can have immutability or you can have the memory saving, not both.

The in-place version, which refuses the self-merge rather than hiding it
Q17_MergeTrees.cs
/// In-place variant: cheaper, and dangerous. Kept here to name the trap rather than hide it. /// MergeInto(x, x) double-counts and can enumerate a dictionary it is writing to. public static void MergeInto(MetaNode target, MetaNode source) { if (target == null || source == null) return; if (ReferenceEquals(target, source)) throw new InvalidOperationException("Self-merge in place would double-count."); target.Value += source.Value; foreach (var kv in source.Children) { if (target.Children.TryGetValue(kv.Key, out var child)) MergeInto(child, kv.Value); else target.Children[kv.Key] = Clone(kv.Value); } }
TimeSpace
O(n)O(n) for the snapshot
“The serialized trees are over 1 GB.” · supplied with the question
Thinking

At this size the constraints invert: memory becomes binding and the recursion becomes a liability. Neither tree may be fully materialised.

Approach

Three things. Do not recurse. MergeIterative moves the stack to the heap; it is tested on a 60,000-deep chain. (Clone is still recursive — that would need fixing too.) Stream instead of materialising. If both files are written with siblings in canonical key order, the merge is a single synchronised pass over the two streams, writing the output as it goes — exactly a sorted-list merge, with O(depth) memory rather than O(n). That is the real answer, and it is why the canonical ordering from follow-up 1 matters here. If they are not ordered, sort externally first, or fall back to the path-keyed external merge from the brute-force section — which is exactly where that approach earns its place.

Trade-off

Streaming is O(depth) memory instead of O(n) and needs the inputs canonically ordered, so it pushes a requirement back onto whoever writes the files. It also gives up random access: you cannot answer "what is the value at this path" without a full pass. In .NET there is a further specific: any buffer over 85 KB lands on the Large Object Heap, which is not compacted by default, so a 1 GB merge that allocates large arrays fragments the heap. Use pooled buffers and Span<T> slices rather than fresh arrays per node.

Iterative merge, for depth
Q17_MergeTrees.cs
/// Iterative merge for trees deep enough to overflow the stack — the 1 GB follow-up. public static MetaNode MergeIterative(MetaNode s, MetaNode t) { if (s == null) return Clone(t); if (t == null) return Clone(s); var root = new MetaNode(s.Key, s.Value + t.Value); var work = new Stack<(MetaNode S, MetaNode T, MetaNode Out)>(); work.Push((s, t, root)); while (work.Count > 0) { var (a, b, into) = work.Pop(); foreach (var kv in a.Children) { if (b.Children.TryGetValue(kv.Key, out var other)) { var child = new MetaNode(kv.Key, kv.Value.Value + other.Value); into.Children[kv.Key] = child; work.Push((kv.Value, other, child)); } else { into.Children[kv.Key] = Clone(kv.Value); } } foreach (var kv in b.Children) if (!into.Children.ContainsKey(kv.Key)) into.Children[kv.Key] = Clone(kv.Value); } return root; }
TimeSpace
O(n) with one passO(depth) streaming versus O(n) in memory
“Optimise the performance.” · supplied with the question
Thinking

Say what the bottleneck is before optimising, because for this problem it is allocation rather than comparison.

Approach

In order of payoff. Iterate the smaller tree and probe the larger, so the number of dictionary lookups is bounded by the smaller side. Pre-size the child dictionaries to the combined child count, avoiding rehashing as they fill. Intern or pool the key strings, since the same keys recur across a large tree and each one is otherwise a separate allocation. Parallelise across the root’s children with a size cutoff. And StringComparer.Ordinal, which is both faster and the correct comparer.

Trade-off

The first three are close to free and I would do them without measuring. Parallelism has a real cutoff and can be slower on small trees. String interning trades a permanent table for reduced allocation, which is right for a long-lived service and wrong for a one-off tool. The honest headline is that the merge is allocation-bound, not compute-bound — so the biggest single win is not allocating, which means MergeInto where the semantics allow it.

TimeSpace
O(min(nₛ, nₜ)) lookupsunchanged
“What about memory?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The .NET specifics matter here and are what distinguish an answer from a generic one.

Approach

The pure merge needs both inputs plus the full result live simultaneously — roughly 2× the result. Per node the overhead is the object header, the key string and a Dictionary instance, and the dictionary is the expensive part: an empty Dictionary<string, MetaNode> is around 80 bytes, which on a tree of mostly-leaf nodes can exceed the data. Allocating it lazily, only when a node actually gains a child, is a large and easy saving. Beyond that: pool and reuse buffers rather than allocating per node, keep allocations under the 85 KB Large Object Heap threshold, and if the trees are genuinely huge, consider a flattened representation — arrays of nodes indexed by integer rather than object references — which removes the per-object overhead entirely and is much less pleasant to work with.

Trade-off

Lazy dictionaries save a lot and add a null check on every access. A flattened array representation saves the most and costs readability and every existing traversal. MergeInto halves peak memory and gives up the concurrency properties. These are the same trade seen from three directions, and the right answer depends on whether the trees are held long-term or merged once and discarded.

TimeSpace
unchanged~2× the result for the pure merge; ~1× for in-place
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Keyed versus positional recursion — the three-case merge, made explicit by the dictionary.
  • Immutability as a design tool — choosing the pure version and having it answer three later follow-ups.
  • Invariant reasoning — noticing that additivity preserves the stated property for free.
  • Concurrency — naming the concrete damage of the in-place race, including the enumerate-while-writing throw.
  • Encoding design — unbounded arity, canonical ordering, and why 297’s null markers do not transfer.
  • Memory at scale — recursion depth, streaming, and .NET specifics like the LOH and the per-node dictionary cost.
A senior engineer might say
Writes the keyed three-case recursion correctly, handles absent branches on both sides, and answers the follow-ups one at a time as they come.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and: chooses immutability up front and says that it is what will answer the concurrency follow-ups, rather than arriving there when asked; notices that summation preserves the invariant so nothing needs recomputing; states the interpretation of "side-heavy" instead of guessing; describes the in-place race concretely, including that the same-tree case throws mid-merge; sees that the canonical sibling ordering required for serialisation is what makes the 1 GB streaming merge possible — connecting follow-ups 1 and 5; and frames the merge as allocation-bound rather than compute-bound. Answering follow-up 4 by pointing at a decision made in the first two minutes is the strongest thing available on this question.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Children are keyed rather than positional, so at each node I have three cases: the key is in both, in the source only, or in the target only. In both, I recurse and sum. Otherwise I copy the branch across. That is the same shape as merging two sorted lists.
One thing worth noticing about the invariant. You said the value is the sum of the node and its children. That is additive, so if both inputs satisfy it, summing satisfies it too — I do not need a fix-up pass. If the invariant had been a maximum rather than a sum, I would.
Now a decision I want to make explicitly, because I think it answers several of your follow-ups. I am going to build a new tree and not mutate either input.
That is what makes your fourth follow-up — one thread using a tree as source while another uses it as target — not a problem at all. There is no target. Nothing is written to either input, so any number of threads can merge from the same trees with no locking.
If I had done it in place, that is a real race, and worth being specific about the damage: the reader sees some children summed and others not, so it gets a total that corresponds to no point in time. And if source and target are the same object, I am enumerating a dictionary while writing to it, which throws partway through and leaves the tree half-merged.
On serialisation — that is essentially LeetCode 297, but harder in one respect. Arity is unbounded, so I cannot use positional null markers; I need a child count or a terminator per node. I would use a length-prefixed binary format for size, or JSON if it crosses a boundary I do not own.
And critically, I have to canonicalise the sibling order, because a dictionary has none. Otherwise two serialisations of the same tree differ and I lose hashing and diffing.
That canonical ordering then pays off on your 1 GB follow-up. If both files have siblings in key order, the merge becomes a single synchronised pass over the two streams, writing output as it goes — O(depth) memory instead of O(n). That is the real answer at that size, and it is why the ordering decision matters.
I would also not recurse at that scale — the depth is the height, so I want an explicit stack.
On performance and memory: this is allocation-bound, not compute-bound. The biggest single cost is a Dictionary per node, about eighty bytes empty, which on a mostly-leaf tree exceeds the data. I would allocate it lazily. And in .NET, anything over 85 kilobytes goes on the Large Object Heap, which is not compacted, so I would pool buffers rather than allocate per node.
On side-heavy — that is not standard terminology, so let me say what I am assuming. I read it as left-child right-sibling: each node's left pointer is its first child and its right pointer is its next sibling. That turns the n-ary tree into a binary one reversibly. If you meant something else I would rather ask than guess.

Q2

Bipartite animal conflicts

MediumMedium priorityGRAPHBFSDFSHASHING
Why this priorityThe framing appears only in InterviewDB. The pattern is better attested: 323 Number of Connected Components and 261 Graph Valid Tree are both on the LinkedIn-tagged list you supplied, and both are the same "walk every component of an undirected graph" discipline this needs. Medium rather than high because no second source describes this question.
LeetCodeDifficultyRelation

1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Given a hashmap where: • Each key represents an animal. • The value is a list of animals that cannot be placed on the same side as the key animal. Determine whether all animals can be divided into two groups such that no conflicting animals are in the same group. Example: {1: [2], 3: [1, 2]} → Not valid {1: [2], 2: [1, 3]} → Valid
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Supplied with two worked examples, which is useful — and they are consistent with each other, which is worth checking rather than assuming. See the examples section.
My reading
Not found in any other source in this workspace. No Glassdoor, Blind, Taro, LeetCode Discuss or Exponent entry describes a LinkedIn bipartition question.
LeetCode
886 Possible Bipartition is this question with people and a dislike list, and 785 Is Graph Bipartite? is the same check given the adjacency list directly. Neither is in the supplied number list, which is itself mildly interesting — the supplied numbers include two other graph problems but not the two that match this one.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported. Not independently corroborated. The algorithm is standard and cheap to prepare, so the lack of corroboration matters less here than it would for a long design question.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickThe input is a map, and a map is a bad way to express a symmetric relation. The first example declares 3: [1, 2] but does not declare 1: [3]. So either conflicts are mutual and the input is allowed to state each one once, or they are directional and mean something else. Both examples are consistent with mutual, and "cannot be placed on the same side as" is symmetric in plain English, so mutual is the reading to take. Say that you are taking it. If conflicts were genuinely one-way the problem would be different and considerably stranger.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

You are seating animals on two sides, and some pairs must not share a side. The question is whether any such seating exists at all — not which one, and not how many.

What we are being asked to compute

A single boolean: does a valid two-way split exist. A natural follow-up asks for the two groups, which costs nothing extra.

Constraints that matter
  • Two groups exactly. Not "at most two", and not three — three changes the problem completely, as the follow-up covers.
  • An animal may appear only as a value, never as a key. It still has to be placed, so it still has to be in the graph.
  • The conflict graph may have several disconnected components. Each is coloured independently, and a single BFS from one start node would miss the rest.
  • The relation is symmetric even though the input may declare it once.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
Conflicts are mutual.If they were directional, "cannot be on the same side" would have to mean something asymmetric, and the problem is no longer bipartiteness. This is the assumption to state out loud.
An animal listed only as a value must still be placed.If it can be ignored, the answer can change from invalid to valid. Building the adjacency symmetrically handles it automatically — which is a good reason to build it that way rather than colour the input map directly.
A duplicate conflict (the same pair twice) is harmless.It produces a parallel edge. Colouring is unaffected. Worth one sentence, not more.
An animal conflicting with itself is unsatisfiable.It cannot avoid its own side. Returning false is the only sensible answer; silently ignoring it would return a "valid" split that violates the constraint.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Given{1: [2], 3: [1, 2]}Not valid
edges 1–2, 3–1, 3–2triangle → an odd cycle → no two-way split
Given{1: [2], 2: [1, 3]}Valid
edges 1–2, 2–3a path → {1, 3} on one side, {2} on the other
Mine{1: [2], 2: [3], 3: [4], 4: [1]}Valid — a 4-cycle is even
Mine{1: [2], 10: [11], 11: [12], 12: [10]}Not valid — the bad component is the second one
Where these came fromThe two supplied examples are consistent with each other and with the mutual reading, which I checked rather than assumed: the first is a triangle only if 3: [1, 2] implies 1: [3]. The two marked Mine are the cases a test targets — even cycles are fine, and the failure may be hiding in a component you never visited.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
The words "divided into two groups such that no conflicting pair shares a group" are the definition of a bipartite graph, and recognising that is the whole question. Everything after it is a standard walk. The reason it is worth saying rather than just doing: once it is a graph question, the failure condition becomes something you can state precisely. A two-way split exists if and only if the graph contains no odd cycle. Walk the graph assigning alternating sides; if you ever reach a node that already has the same side as the one you came from, you have closed a cycle of odd length, and no assignment can work — not this one, not any. That "not any" is the part a case-by-case attempt cannot give you. Trying to place animals greedily and backtracking would also find the answer, and would leave you unable to say why. The colouring argument tells you the answer is a property of the graph rather than of your search order. The two places this goes wrong have nothing to do with the algorithm. The input declares each conflict once, so build a symmetric adjacency list first. And the graph may be in several pieces, so loop over every uncoloured node, not just the first.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Try every assignment. Each animal goes left or right, so enumerate all 2ⁿ combinations and check each against the conflict list.

What it costs

O(2ⁿ · E) time, O(n) space.

Why it stops workingTwenty animals is a million assignments and forty is a trillion. But the better objection is that it tells you nothing: it answers "is there one" without ever explaining why not, so no follow-up about three groups or about which pair caused the failure can be answered from it. Backtracking with propagation is the same algorithm as the colouring, arrived at without noticing.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTDictionary<int, List<int>> — animal to the animals it conflicts with
↓
TRANSFORMATIONBuild a symmetric adjacency list, adding both directions and creating an entry for any animal seen only as a value
↓
DATA STRUCTUREDictionary<int, int> for the side of each animal, a Queue<int> for the BFS frontier
↓
ALGORITHMFor every uncoloured animal, BFS assigning 1 - side[current]; fail on an edge joining two animals already on the same side
↓
OUTPUTtrue if every component coloured cleanly, otherwise false
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
A separate adjacency dictionaryColouring the input map directly loses the animals that appear only as values, and forces you to check both directions on every edge. Building it once is cheaper and removes a whole class of bug.
Dictionary<int, int> for sidesServes as both the visited set and the colour. Two structures would have to be kept consistent; one cannot drift.
Queue (BFS) rather than recursionEither works. BFS avoids a stack overflow on a long chain, and the "colour is the opposite of the node I came from" rule reads more obviously in the iterative form. DFS with an explicit stack is equally fine — the recursion depth is the only real argument.
The algorithm
  1. Walk the input. For each key and each animal in its list, add the edge in both directions, creating either endpoint if it is new.
  2. Reject immediately if any animal conflicts with itself.
  3. For each animal in the adjacency list with no side yet: assign side 0 and push it.
  4. Pop a node. For each neighbour: if uncoloured, give it the opposite side and push it.
  5. If the neighbour already has a side and it equals the current node’s side, return false — an odd cycle.
  6. If every component finishes, return true.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

The first supplied example, {1: [2], 3: [1, 2]}. Watch the symmetric build create the edge that the input never stated.

StepAdjacencySidesNote
read 1: [2]1↔2—both directions added from one declaration
read 3: [1, 2]1↔2, 3↔1, 3↔2—1 now lists 3, although the input never said so
start at 11 → side 0first component
from 1, see 22 → side 1opposite side
from 1, see 33 → side 1opposite side
from 2, see 1already 0, current is 1different — fine
from 2, see 3already 1, current is 1same side → return false
What the state tells youThe failure is the edge 2–3 closing a triangle. Note that it was found on the third edge, not the first: the greedy assignment was locally fine twice before it was globally impossible. That is exactly why the answer is a property of the graph and not of the order you walked it.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The check 77 lines
Q02_Bipartite.cs
// Q2 — can the animals be split into two groups with no conflict inside a group? public static class AnimalGrouping { public static bool CanSplit(Dictionary<int, List<int>> conflicts) { if (conflicts == null || conflicts.Count == 0) return true; // 1. "cannot share a side" is mutual, but the input may declare it one way only. // Build a symmetric adjacency list before colouring anything. var adj = new Dictionary<int, List<int>>(); void Touch(int a) { if (!adj.ContainsKey(a)) adj[a] = new List<int>(); } foreach (var pair in conflicts) { Touch(pair.Key); foreach (int other in pair.Value ?? new List<int>()) { if (other == pair.Key) return false; // conflicts with itself: unsatisfiable Touch(other); adj[pair.Key].Add(other); adj[other].Add(pair.Key); } } // 2. Two-colour every component. A conflict edge must join different colours. var side = new Dictionary<int, int>(); foreach (int start in adj.Keys) { if (side.ContainsKey(start)) continue; // already coloured in an earlier component side[start] = 0; var queue = new Queue<int>(); queue.Enqueue(start); while (queue.Count > 0) { int cur = queue.Dequeue(); foreach (int next in adj[cur]) { if (!side.TryGetValue(next, out int colour)) { side[next] = 1 - side[cur]; queue.Enqueue(next); } else if (colour == side[cur]) { return false; // odd cycle: no two-way split exists } } } } return true; } // Same walk, but hands back the actual two groups — what a follow-up usually wants. public static (List<int> Left, List<int> Right) Split(Dictionary<int, List<int>> conflicts) { if (!CanSplit(conflicts)) throw new InvalidOperationException("Not bipartite."); var adj = new Dictionary<int, List<int>>(); void Touch(int a) { if (!adj.ContainsKey(a)) adj[a] = new List<int>(); } foreach (var pair in conflicts) { Touch(pair.Key); foreach (int other in pair.Value ?? new List<int>()) { Touch(other); adj[pair.Key].Add(other); adj[other].Add(pair.Key); } } var side = new Dictionary<int, int>(); foreach (int start in adj.Keys) { if (side.ContainsKey(start)) continue; side[start] = 0; var queue = new Queue<int>(); queue.Enqueue(start); while (queue.Count > 0) { int cur = queue.Dequeue(); foreach (int next in adj[cur]) if (!side.ContainsKey(next)) { side[next] = 1 - side[cur]; queue.Enqueue(next); } } } var left = side.Where(kv => kv.Value == 0).Select(kv => kv.Key).OrderBy(x => x).ToList(); var right = side.Where(kv => kv.Value == 1).Select(kv => kv.Key).OrderBy(x => x).ToList(); return (left, right); } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
Why Touch exists at all

It creates an empty adjacency entry for an animal, and it is called for both endpoints of every edge. That one call is what handles the animal appearing only as a value — without it, animal 2 in {1: [2]} would never be a key in adj, and the outer loop would never consider it. A quietly missing node is the hardest bug to see here, because the answer is still a plausible boolean.

The outer loop is the disconnected-components fix

foreach (int start in adj.Keys) with if (side.ContainsKey(start)) continue;. A single BFS from one node explores one component. In my second example the invalid triangle is in the second component, so a single-start solution returns true — confidently wrong.

Why side is both colour and visited set

side.TryGetValue answers "have I seen this" and "which side" in one probe. Keeping a separate HashSet<int> visited alongside a colour map means two structures that must agree, and the bug when they disagree is a node coloured twice.

The comparison that decides everything

colour == side[cur] on an already-coloured neighbour. If it differs, the edge is satisfied and there is nothing to do — note that we do not re-enqueue. If it matches, the graph has an odd cycle. One comparison is the entire failure detection.

Self-conflict, handled before the walk

if (other == pair.Key) return false; during the build. A self-loop would otherwise be coloured as "neighbour already has my side" and produce the right answer by accident; handling it explicitly means the reason is in the code.

Why Split re-walks instead of sharing state

It is duplication, and deliberate: CanSplit is the answer to the question asked, and keeping it free of output-collection machinery keeps it readable. In production the two would be one function returning a result object. In an interview I would write CanSplit first, get agreement, and only then extend.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
build adjacencyO(V + E)O(V + E)Each declared conflict adds two directed entries
CanSplitO(V + E)O(V)Every node enqueued once, every edge examined twice
SplitO(V + E)O(V)Same walk, plus the output lists
The part worth saying out loudV is the number of distinct animals, which is not the number of keys in the input map — animals appearing only as values count. That distinction is the complexity version of the same bug the Touch call prevents.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Empty or null mapReturns true. Nothing to place, so a valid split exists trivially.
An animal with an empty conflict listGets its own component of one node, coloured 0. Fine.
An animal only ever a valueCreated by Touch and coloured normally.
Self-conflict {1: [1]}Returns false during the build, before any colouring.
The same conflict declared twiceA parallel edge. The neighbour is already coloured and on the opposite side, so it is accepted and nothing is re-enqueued.
Several components, one invalidCaught, because the outer loop reaches every component. This is the case a naive solution fails.
An even cycleValid. Only odd cycles are impossible, and stating that distinction is the difference between knowing the theorem and remembering a recipe.
A long chain of 100,000 animalsBFS is iterative, so no stack overflow. Recursive DFS would need a depth limit or an explicit stack.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 4 follow-ups.
“Return the two groups, not just whether they exist.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The colouring already computed it — the two groups are the two colour classes. Nothing new is needed, which is worth saying so it does not look like luck.

Approach

Partition the side dictionary by value. Animals with side 0 form one group, side 1 the other. Note that the assignment is not unique: any component can be flipped independently, so with k components there are 2ᵏ valid answers and any one of them is correct.

Trade-off

No extra cost. The only decision is what to do when no split exists — throw, or return the conflicting pair. Returning the pair is more useful, because a caller can then explain the failure to a user, and it costs only carrying the edge out of the walk.

Returning the groups
Q02_Bipartite.cs
// Same walk, but hands back the actual two groups — what a follow-up usually wants. public static (List<int> Left, List<int> Right) Split(Dictionary<int, List<int>> conflicts) { if (!CanSplit(conflicts)) throw new InvalidOperationException("Not bipartite."); var adj = new Dictionary<int, List<int>>(); void Touch(int a) { if (!adj.ContainsKey(a)) adj[a] = new List<int>(); } foreach (var pair in conflicts) { Touch(pair.Key); foreach (int other in pair.Value ?? new List<int>()) { Touch(other); adj[pair.Key].Add(other); adj[other].Add(pair.Key); } } var side = new Dictionary<int, int>(); foreach (int start in adj.Keys) { if (side.ContainsKey(start)) continue; side[start] = 0; var queue = new Queue<int>(); queue.Enqueue(start); while (queue.Count > 0) { int cur = queue.Dequeue(); foreach (int next in adj[cur]) if (!side.ContainsKey(next)) { side[next] = 1 - side[cur]; queue.Enqueue(next); } } } var left = side.Where(kv => kv.Value == 0).Select(kv => kv.Key).OrderBy(x => x).ToList(); var right = side.Where(kv => kv.Value == 1).Select(kv => kv.Key).OrderBy(x => x).ToList(); return (left, right); }
TimeSpace
O(V + E)O(V)
“What if three groups were allowed?” · likely pushback
Thinking

The instinct is that it is the same problem with three colours. It is not, and this is the most valuable thing to know about this question.

Approach

Two-colouring is decidable in linear time. Three-colouring is NP-complete. There is no known polynomial algorithm, so the honest answer changes shape: backtracking with constraint propagation for small inputs, a heuristic such as DSATUR (colour the most-constrained node first) for larger ones, and if this were real, a SAT or CP solver rather than hand-rolled search.

Trade-off

Exact and exponential, or approximate and fast. What matters in the interview is not the algorithm choice — it is recognising that a one-word change to the problem statement moved it across the tractability line, and saying so instead of confidently generalising the BFS.

TimeSpace
Exponential in the worst case for exact 3-colouringO(V) for the recursion
“The conflict list arrives as a stream and never fits in memory. Now what?” · likely pushback
Thinking

Colouring needs the graph. A stream does not give you the graph, so either the answer becomes incremental or the data has to be partitioned.

Approach

Union-find with parity is the right tool: keep, for each animal, its representative and whether it is the same side as that representative. Merging two animals that already share a representative and the same parity is the odd cycle, detected the instant the edge arrives. Almost constant time per edge, and it needs one pass with no adjacency list.

Trade-off

Union-find answers "is it still satisfiable" incrementally, which BFS cannot. It does not give you the groups as cheaply, and it cannot be undone — there is no efficient way to remove an edge. Trade the groups and deletion for O(1) streaming updates.

TimeSpace
O(E · α(V)), effectively O(E)O(V)
“Why BFS rather than DFS? Does it matter?” · likely pushback
Thinking

It does not matter for correctness, and saying "it does not matter, here is what does" is a better answer than picking one and defending it.

Approach

Both visit every node once and both detect the same failure. BFS is iterative by construction, so a 100,000-node chain is fine; recursive DFS would overflow the stack. DFS has one genuine advantage: the recursion stack is the current path, so if you want to report the odd cycle itself, DFS hands it to you and BFS makes you reconstruct it from parent pointers.

Trade-off

BFS for robustness on deep graphs, DFS if the follow-up asks which cycle broke it. Choose on that basis rather than by habit.

TimeSpace
O(V + E) either wayO(V) for BFS; O(depth) stack for recursive DFS
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Problem recognition — mapping an English constraint onto a named graph property, which is the single thing being tested here.
  • Input hygiene — noticing that the map is an asymmetric encoding of a symmetric relation.
  • Graph traversal correctness — all components, not just the first.
  • Knowing the boundary of what you know — two colours is easy, three is NP-complete, and confidently generalising is the trap.
  • Stating the theorem — bipartite iff no odd cycle, rather than "it works out".
A senior engineer might say
Recognises bipartiteness, writes a correct BFS two-colouring, and handles both components and the symmetric build.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and additionally raises the asymmetric-input ambiguity as a clarification before coding, states the odd-cycle characterisation rather than describing the mechanics, knows that three groups is NP-complete, and offers union-find with parity for the streaming case. The signal is the ambiguity being raised first — it is the one part of this question that is genuinely about reading, not about graphs.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Before I code: the input gives me a map, and the first example declares that 3 conflicts with 1 but never declares that 1 conflicts with 3. I am going to read conflicts as mutual, because “cannot be on the same side as” is symmetric. Is that right?
Given that, this is asking whether the conflict graph is bipartite — can I two-colour it. And the precise answer is: yes, if and only if there is no odd cycle.
So first I build a symmetric adjacency list. That matters for a second reason: an animal might appear only in a value list, and it still has to be placed. Building the adjacency both ways creates it for free.
Then I walk. Each node gets the opposite side from the node I reached it through. If I reach a node that already has the same side as where I came from, I have closed an odd cycle and no assignment works — not just this one.
The outer loop matters. The graph can be in several pieces, and the invalid piece might not be the first one I touch, so I start a fresh colouring from every animal I have not coloured yet.
Returning the two groups is free if they want it — they are the two colour classes.
One thing worth flagging: if it were three groups instead of two, this approach does not generalise. Three-colouring is NP-complete. That is a genuinely different problem, not a harder version of this one.

Q4

Biased random generator

MediumMedium priorityRANDOMIZATIONDESIGN
Why this priorityNo other source in this workspace describes this question. Two randomisation problems are on the supplied LinkedIn-tagged list — 528 Random Pick with Weight and 384 Shuffle an Array — so the topic is attested even though the question is not. Medium, and worth preparing because it is short and because 528 is its mirror image: one turns uniform into biased, the other biased into uniform.
LeetCodeDifficultyRelation
384. Shuffle an ArrayMediumSimilar pattern
528. Random Pick with WeightMediumSimilar pattern
1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Input: getRandom01Biased() Returns 0 with probability p Returns 1 with probability (1-p) Output: getRandom06Uniform() Returns an integer from [0,6] with uniform probability.
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Stated precisely, with no ambiguity about the range: [0,6] inclusive, so seven outcomes.
My reading
Not found elsewhere in this workspace. It is a well-known interview problem in general (the classic form is "fair coin from a biased coin"), which is why it goes in GENERAL rather than REPORTED for anything beyond InterviewDB.
LeetCode
528 Random Pick with Weight is the exact inverse transformation and is on the supplied list. 384 Shuffle an Array tests the same instinct — a plausible shortcut that is subtly non-uniform.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported. Not independently corroborated as a LinkedIn question, though the family is well attested and the problem itself is a classic.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickIs p known? The signature says only that the generator is biased, not by how much. This matters, and the good answer does not need to know. A solution that works for unknown p is strictly better than one that uses it, and Von Neumann debiasing never refers to p at all. If p were known you could do better on the expected number of calls — arithmetic coding gets close to the entropy bound — but you would have bought a small constant factor with a dependency on a value that is usually only approximately known. Say that you are deliberately not using p. One thing does have to be assumed: 0 < p < 1. If p is 0 or 1 the source has no entropy and no algorithm can produce a fair bit from it.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

You have one source of randomness and it is unfair. You need seven equally likely outcomes out of it. Nothing about the source can be changed, and its bias may be unknown.

What we are being asked to compute

A single integer in 0..6, with each of the seven values having probability exactly 1/7.

Constraints that matter
  • Exactly uniform, not approximately. Any scheme that maps an unequal number of source outcomes onto the seven values is wrong, however small the error looks.
  • The number of calls to the source is not fixed. Any exact method must be able to give up and retry.
  • Seven is not a power of two, which is the second difficulty and is independent of the bias.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
Successive calls are independent.The whole construction relies on it. If the source were correlated — say it alternated — pairing two draws would produce a deterministic result, not a fair bit. Worth saying, because it is the one assumption that cannot be worked around.
p is fixed across calls.If the bias drifts, the pairing argument still holds within a pair but the expected cost changes over time. Correctness survives; performance predictions do not.
0 < p < 1.At the extremes the source is a constant and the task is impossible. Stating it is a one-line acknowledgement that you know where the method’s domain ends.
Unbounded worst-case time is acceptable.If it is not, no exact method exists. You would have to accept a tiny bias with a call cap, which is a legitimate engineering trade and should be named as one.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Pairingdraws 0,1accept → return 0
draws 1,0accept → return 1
draws 0,0 or 1,1reject → draw another pair
Assemblythree fair bits → 101value 5, in range → return 5
three fair bits → 111value 7, out of range → discard all three and redraw
Costp = 0.5≈13.7 biased calls per output, measured
p = 0.05≈72 biased calls per output, measured
Where these came fromThe two cost figures are measured, not derived on paper: the test harness draws 700,000 values at each of four different p and checks both the uniformity (chi-square over 6 degrees of freedom) and the observed call count against the predicted 24 / (7p(1-p)). They agree to within 5%.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
There are two separate problems here and conflating them is what makes this question hard. Solve them one at a time. Problem one: remove the bias. Call the source twice. The outcome 01 has probability p(1-p). The outcome 10 has probability (1-p)p. Multiplication commutes, so those two are exactly equal whatever p is. The other two outcomes, 00 and 11, are the ones carrying the bias — so throw them away and draw again. Return the first bit of any pair that disagrees, and you have a perfectly fair bit from a source whose bias you never measured. That is Von Neumann’s trick, and the whole of it is the observation that p(1-p) = (1-p)p. Problem two: get seven outcomes from fair bits. Three fair bits give eight equally likely values, 0 through 7. You want seven. Eight does not divide by seven, so no mapping of the eight onto the seven can be uniform — some value would have to receive two of the eight, making it twice as likely. The only correct move is to reject: if the three bits give 7, discard them and draw three more. This is rejection sampling, and the temptation it defeats is value % 7, which is wrong and looks fine. Both halves are the same idea applied twice: when the outcomes you have do not divide evenly into the outcomes you want, discard rather than squeeze. Neither half needs to know p, and neither has a bounded worst case — both facts are worth stating rather than hoping nobody asks.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Call the source three times, treat the bits as a binary number, take it modulo 7.

What it costs

O(1) calls, O(1) space, and wrong.

Why it stops workingTwo independent errors, and being able to name both is the point of the question. First, the bits are biased, so the eight patterns are not equally likely to begin with — 000 has probability p³ and 111 has (1-p)³. Second, even with perfectly fair bits, % 7 maps both 0 and 7 onto 0, so 0 would come out twice as often as everything else. The second error survives fixing the first, which is why the modulo shortcut is worth explicitly rejecting out loud.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTFunc<int> biasedBit returning 0 with probability p, 1 with probability 1−p
↓
TRANSFORMATIONPairs of draws → a fair bit, by keeping only the two outcomes that are equally likely by symmetry
↓
DATA STRUCTURENone. Two local integers.
↓
ALGORITHMDebias to a fair bit; take three fair bits as a value in 0..7; reject 7 and redraw
↓
OUTPUTA uniform integer in 0..6
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
No data structure at allWorth saying, because "reach for a structure" is the reflex this question punishes. The entire solution is two loops and a shift.
Pairs, not triples or longer runsPairs are the smallest window in which two distinct outcomes have provably equal probability. Longer windows admit more accepted patterns and are more efficient — that is the advanced-extraction follow-up — but pairs are the ones you can prove on a whiteboard in one line.
Three fair bits, not a loop up to 7Three bits is the smallest power of two above seven, so it wastes the least. Two bits cannot reach 6 at all.
Rejection, not modulo or scalingThe only exactly uniform option. Everything else trades a bounded call count for a bias, and the bias is the thing the question is about.
The algorithm
  1. FairBit(): draw two bits. If they differ, return the first. If they match, repeat.
  2. Next0To6(): build v = (b₂ << 2) | (b₁ << 1) | b₀ from three fair bits.
  3. If v <= 6, return it. Otherwise discard all three bits and start again.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

One call to Next0To6() with an unlucky run, showing both rejection loops firing. Suppose the raw draws arrive in this order.

DrawsWhat happensFair bits so farRunning value
0, 0pair matches → reject, draw again——
1, 1pair matches → reject again——
1, 0differ → fair bit 11v = 1
0, 1differ → fair bit 01, 0v = 2
1, 0differ → fair bit 11, 0, 1v = 5
—v = 5, which is ≤ 6return 5
(alt)had the bits been 1,1,1 → v = 7discard all three, start over
What the state tells youTen raw draws produced one output, and that was not unusual: at p = 0.5 the expected cost is about 13.7. Note that the second rejection throws away three fair bits, not one — they cannot be partially reused without introducing correlation, which is a subtle point worth knowing and not worth improvising.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The generator 28 lines
Q04_Random.cs
// Q4 — a uniform integer in [0,6] built from a biased coin. public class UniformFromBiased { private readonly Func<int> _biased; // returns 0 with probability p, 1 with probability 1-p public long BiasedCalls { get; private set; } public UniformFromBiased(Func<int> biasedBit) { _biased = biasedBit; } // Von Neumann debiasing. Draw two bits: // "01" has probability p(1-p); "10" has probability (1-p)p. Identical, whatever p is. // "00" and "11" carry the bias, so throw the pair away and draw again. public int FairBit() { while (true) { int a = _biased(); int b = _biased(); BiasedCalls += 2; if (a != b) return a; } } // Three fair bits give 0..7 uniformly. 7 values are wanted, so reject 7 and redraw. // Rejecting is what keeps it uniform: rescaling 8 outcomes onto 7 cannot be. public int Next0To6() { while (true) { int v = (FairBit() << 2) | (FairBit() << 1) | FairBit(); if (v <= 6) return v; } } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
if (a != b) return a; — why returning the first bit is correct

The two accepted patterns are 01 and 10, with equal probability. Returning the first bit returns 0 for one and 1 for the other, so each comes out half the time. Returning the second bit would be equally correct, inverted. What would not be correct is returning a on a match, which is where the bias lives.

Why the outer loop discards all three bits

On rejecting 7 the code loops and calls FairBit() three fresh times. Reusing two of the three — say keeping the top bits and redrawing the last — would correlate successive outputs, because the surviving bits are conditioned on having been part of a rejected triple. The conditioning is easy to miss and it is a real bug.

Why BiasedCalls is instrumented

It makes the expected cost measurable rather than asserted. The test compares the observed ratio against 24 / (7p(1-p)) at four values of p. In an interview I would not write the counter, but I would state the formula — and having actually measured it is why I am willing to state it.

The clock on the wall: termination

Both loops terminate with probability 1 and neither has a worst-case bound. For any 0 < p < 1 the accept probability per pair is 2p(1-p) > 0, so the number of attempts is geometric with finite mean. "Terminates almost surely" is the honest phrase; "O(1)" is not.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
FairBitexpected 1/(p(1−p)) drawsO(1)Each pair accepts with probability 2p(1−p); expected pairs is the reciprocal
Next0To6expected 24/(7p(1−p)) drawsO(1)3 fair bits × 8/7 for the rejection × 2 draws per pair
worst caseunboundedO(1)Rejection sampling has no upper bound on attempts
The part worth saying out loudAt p = 0.5 that formula gives 13.7 biased calls per output, and the measured value is 13.7. At p = 0.05 it predicts 72 and measures 72. The cost blows up as p approaches 0 or 1, because p(1-p) is the accept rate and it goes to zero — which is the honest answer to "how slow can this get".
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
p = 0 or p = 1The source is constant, every pair matches, and FairBit never returns. No algorithm can extract entropy that is not there — the right response is to state the precondition, not to add a retry limit that silently returns a wrong answer.
p very close to 0 or 1Correct but slow: cost scales as 1/(p(1−p)). At p = 0.001 that is roughly 3,400 draws per output.
Correlated sourceBreaks correctness, not just speed. An alternating source makes every pair differ, so FairBit becomes deterministic. This is the assumption to state.
A range that is a power of two, say [0,7]The outer rejection disappears entirely and the cost drops by 8/7. Worth mentioning as the reason seven is awkward.
Needing many values at onceGenerate them independently. Batching does not help the fair-bit extraction, but advanced extractors do — see the follow-up.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 4 follow-ups.
“Generalise it to [0, n-1] for any n.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The same two-step shape, with the number of bits chosen per n. The only trap is the case where n is already a power of two.

Approach

Take b = ceil(log2(n)) fair bits, giving a value in 0 .. 2^b - 1. Reject anything >= n and redraw all b bits. Accept probability is n / 2^b, which is always greater than 1/2, so the expected number of retries is under 2 regardless of n.

Trade-off

Simple and exact. The efficiency varies with how close n is to a power of two: n = 9 needs 4 bits and wastes 7 of 16 patterns, so it retries nearly half the time. A batched scheme that generates several values from one larger draw recovers most of that waste, at the cost of state between calls.

TimeSpace
Expected 2^b / n rounds, each of b fair bits; under 2 rounds in the worst caseO(1)
“Von Neumann throws away most of its input. Can you do better?” · likely pushback
Thinking

Yes, substantially, and knowing that there is a known-better answer matters more than being able to implement it under time pressure. Von Neumann discards both matching pairs entirely, and those discards still carry information — the sequence of which pairs matched is itself random.

Approach

Advanced extractors (Peres, or Elias’s method) recurse on the discarded information: the sequence of "was this pair a match" and "was the match 00 or 11" are two further biased sources to extract from. Peres’s iterated construction approaches the Shannon entropy bound, so at p = 0.5 you approach one fair bit per input bit rather than one per four.

Trade-off

Von Neumann is four lines and provably fair. Peres is recursive, needs buffering, and is much harder to convince a reviewer of. I would ship Von Neumann unless the source were genuinely expensive — a hardware entropy source, say — and then I would use a library, not my own implementation. The interview answer is to name the direction and the trade, not to write it.

TimeSpace
Approaches the entropy bound, ~1 fair bit per input bit at p = 0.5O(1) amortised with buffering
“p is known. Does that change your answer?” · likely pushback
Thinking

It permits a better answer and does not require one. The interesting part is whether you can say why you would still not use it.

Approach

With p known you can do arithmetic coding on the source and approach the entropy bound directly, which is a large constant-factor win at extreme p. You could also pick pairing windows to maximise the accept rate.

Trade-off

p is almost never known exactly — it is measured, and it drifts. A method whose correctness depends on p is fragile in a way that a method whose only speed depends on p is not. Von Neumann is exactly uniform for every p in (0,1), including p values you measured wrongly. I would keep it unless profiling proved the source was the bottleneck.

TimeSpace
Approaches the entropy boundO(1)
“Now the reverse: given a uniform generator, produce outcomes with given weights.” · likely pushback
Thinking

This is LeetCode 528 and it is on your supplied list, which makes the pair worth preparing together — the interviewer who asks one may well ask the other.

Approach

Build the prefix sums of the weights once, draw a uniform value in [0, total), and binary-search for the first prefix sum strictly greater than it. O(n) to build, O(log n) per draw. The alias method gets O(1) per draw with O(n) preprocessing if draws vastly outnumber builds.

Trade-off

Prefix sums plus binary search is the answer to give: simple, and the preprocessing/query split is the same argument as Q11 and Q13. Mention the alias method as the O(1) option rather than implementing it.

TimeSpace
O(n) build, O(log n) per drawO(n)
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Probability reasoning — specifically, spotting that p(1-p) = (1-p)p is the whole answer to the first half.
  • Knowing that "approximately uniform" is a wrong answer — and being able to say why % 7 is biased even with fair bits.
  • Decomposition — two separate problems, solved separately. Candidates who attack it as one problem tend not to finish.
  • Honesty about termination — no worst-case bound, terminates with probability 1.
  • Not over-engineering — the correct answer uses no data structure and does not use p.
A senior engineer might say
Gets Von Neumann debiasing, gets three bits with rejection of 7, and can state the expected cost.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and additionally: rejects the % 7 shortcut out loud with the reason, notes that the solution deliberately does not use p and why that is a feature, states the independence assumption as the one thing that cannot be worked around, is precise that the worst case is unbounded rather than calling it O(1), and knows that better extractors exist without pretending to implement one. The single strongest signal is the independence assumption — almost nobody states it, and it is the assumption the whole construction rests on.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
There are two problems in here, and I want to solve them separately rather than together.
First: turn a biased bit into a fair one. I call the source twice. The outcome 0 then 1 has probability p times (1-p). The outcome 1 then 0 has (1-p) times p. Those are the same number whatever p is. So if the two draws differ, I return the first one, and if they match I throw the pair away and draw again.
That gives me a provably fair bit, and notice I never needed to know p. I think that is a feature — a solution that depends on p would be fragile, because p is usually measured rather than known.
Second: seven outcomes from fair bits. Three fair bits give me eight equally likely values. I want seven, and eight does not divide by seven, so there is no way to map eight onto seven without making one of them twice as likely. So I reject: if the three bits give me 7, I throw all three away and draw again.
I want to explicitly rule out taking the value modulo 7, because it is the tempting answer and it is wrong twice — the bits are biased to begin with, and even with fair bits 0 and 7 both map to 0.
On cost: at p = 0.5 this is about fourteen calls to the source per output. In general it is 24 over 7p(1-p), so it degrades as p approaches 0 or 1.
And I should be precise about termination. This has no worst-case bound. It terminates with probability 1, not within any fixed number of draws. If a bounded number of calls were a hard requirement, no exact method exists and we would have to accept a small bias deliberately.
One assumption I am relying on throughout: successive calls are independent. If the source were correlated — alternating, say — every pair would differ and my fair bit would be deterministic.
Video lesson plan · LinkedIn Coding — Fair Randomness from a Biased Coin · ~15 min

Separate the two sub-problems, prove each in one line, and show why the obvious shortcut is wrong twice over.

Q7

Scheduled executor service
