1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Implement the equivalent of: public void schedule( Runnable command, long delay, TimeUnit unit) The operation schedules a one-shot action that becomes enabled after the specified delay. Implement the concept in C#. Explain: priority queue, delayed tasks, worker thread, blocking/waiting, condition signaling, cancellation, shutdown, thread safety, spurious wakeups where relevant. This is a concurrency/design question, not simply a syntax translation from Java.
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Supplied with a full list of the aspects to cover, which is effectively the marking scheme. Nine things are named, and only two of them are the data structure. The other seven are concurrency.
Reported elsewhere
"Design a job scheduler" — Blind, LinkedIn Senior SWE Infrastructure, Sep 2025, first-hand. Same problem, design round rather than coding round, and already worked in this workspace.
Reported elsewhere
A medium string-manipulation question with a concurrency follow-up — reported separately, which tells you concurrency does appear in LinkedIn coding rounds rather than only in design.
LeetCode
1188 Design Bounded Blocking Queue is this minus the delays: the same lock plus Monitor.Wait/PulseAll skeleton. 1117 Building H2O is condition signalling with nothing else in the way. Both are on the supplied list, which is a reasonable hint that the primitives get tested.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported, with the same problem independently reported first-hand at design scale. The InterviewDB framing — write the class — rests on one source; the topic does not.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickThree things the statement leaves open, and the third is the one that catches people. How many worker threads? One is enough for correctness and is the right first answer, because it makes the concurrency reviewable. A pool is the follow-up. What does schedule return? Java returns a ScheduledFuture, which is how you cancel. The supplied signature returns void, so cancellation — which the aspect list explicitly asks about — has nowhere to live. I return a handle and say why, because a void signature and a cancellation requirement are incompatible. Should Task.Delay count? In C# the four-line answer is await Task.Delay(delay); work();. It is legitimate production code and it is almost certainly not what is being asked, given that the statement says "not simply a syntax translation" and lists spurious wakeups. Say that the library answer exists, say why you are not giving it, then build the thing.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A one-thread scheduler. Callers hand it work and a delay; it runs each piece of work once, no earlier than its deadline, and it does so without burning CPU while it waits.

What we are being asked to compute

Not a value — a component. The behaviour to get right is: correct ordering by deadline, no busy-waiting, a caller-visible way to cancel, a clean shutdown, and no data race on the shared queue.

Constraints that matter
  • Tasks run in deadline order, not submission order. Submitting a 10ms task after a 1000ms one must not delay it.
  • The worker must not poll. Sleeping in a loop and checking is the answer that fails this question.
  • A task scheduled sooner than whatever the worker is currently waiting for has to preempt that wait. This is the single most-missed requirement.
  • Equal deadlines should run in submission order, which a bare priority queue does not guarantee.
  • Schedule may be called from any thread, concurrently, including from inside a running task.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
One worker thread.With a pool, tasks no longer run one at a time, so a slow task stops delaying the next one — but the work must then be handed off rather than executed by the timing thread. Single worker first; say that you know the trade.
Schedule returns a handle, despite the void in the signature.Otherwise cancellation is impossible, and the statement asks for cancellation. This is a contradiction in the question and worth naming rather than resolving silently.
A task that throws must not kill the scheduler.Nothing in the statement says so. If exceptions should propagate to the caller, that needs a Task-based API, which is a different design.
Millisecond resolution, from a monotonic clock.Monitor.Wait takes milliseconds, so sub-millisecond scheduling is not achievable this way regardless. Environment.TickCount64, not DateTime.Now — a wall-clock step would reorder deadlines.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Orderingschedule(A, 150ms); schedule(B, 40ms); schedule(C, 90ms)runs B, C, A
Preemptionschedule(X, 400ms); wait 30ms; schedule(Y, 30ms)Y runs at ~60ms, not after X
Cancelh = schedule(Z, 120ms); h.Cancel()Z never runs; a second Cancel() returns false
Throwinga task throws, then another is scheduledthe second still runs — the worker survives
Shutdownschedule(W, 30ms); Shutdown(drain: true)W runs; further Schedule throws
Load500 tasks with delays 0–49mseach runs exactly once
Where these came fromAll six are in the test suite and all six are behaviours rather than return values, which is what makes this question different from the rest of the page. The preemption case is the one that fails on a naive implementation: a worker sleeping until the 400ms deadline will not notice the 30ms task unless the scheduling call wakes it.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
There are two mechanisms and the question is really about the second one. The easy half. Tasks run in deadline order, so keep them in a min-heap keyed on absolute deadline. Absolute, not remaining, so nothing has to be decremented. PriorityQueue<TElement, TPriority> in .NET does this, and tie-breaking on a monotonic sequence number keeps equal deadlines in submission order — a bare heap does not promise that, and it is the kind of thing a test targets. The hard half. How does the worker wait? Two wrong answers are tempting. Spinning burns a core. Sleeping in fixed increments trades latency against CPU and gets both wrong. The right answer is a timed condition wait: the worker takes the lock, looks at the head of the heap, and calls Monitor.Wait(gate, msUntilDue). It consumes nothing while waiting, and it can be woken early. That last property is the observation the whole design turns on. Schedule calls Monitor.Pulse after enqueueing, because the new task may be due sooner than whatever the worker is currently waiting for. Without the pulse, a task scheduled for 30ms behind a task scheduled for 400ms waits 400ms. It is one line, it has no visible effect in any single-threaded test, and omitting it is the most common failure on this question. And the reason the wait sits inside a while (true) re-checking the head, rather than trusting what it saw before waiting: a wake can come from the timeout, from that Pulse, or from nothing at all — a spurious wakeup. The loop makes all three identical, which is why you never need to distinguish them. That is the actual answer to "spurious wakeups" in the supplied aspect list: not "handle them", but "structure the code so they need no handling".
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

One thread per task: on Schedule, start a thread that sleeps for the delay and then runs the work.

What it costs

O(1) per schedule, and O(n) threads.

Why it stops workingIt is correct, and it is the answer to give first precisely because it is correct — then explain why it does not survive. A thread costs about a megabyte of stack committed on demand plus a scheduler entry, so ten thousand pending timers is ten thousand threads, and the OS scheduler starts to thrash long before that. Task.Delay is a much better version of the same idea because it uses a timer rather than a thread, and is what I would actually ship. What neither version gives you is a place to stand for the follow-ups — cancellation, ordering guarantees, shutdown semantics and introspection all want a single owned queue.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTSchedule(Action work, TimeSpan delay) from any thread
↓
TRANSFORMATIONDelay becomes an absolute deadline: now + delay. A sequence number is attached for tie-breaking.
↓
DATA STRUCTUREPriorityQueue<ScheduledTask, (long DueAt, long Seq)> guarded by one lock; a single background worker thread
↓
ALGORITHMThe worker loops: look at the head, timed-wait until it is due, pop and run it outside the lock. Schedule enqueues and pulses.
↓
OUTPUTThe work runs once, no earlier than its deadline; the caller gets a cancellation handle
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
Min-heap on (DueAt, Seq)O(log n) insert and O(1) peek, which is what lets the worker decide how long to sleep in constant time. The Seq component is what makes equal deadlines FIFO.
Absolute deadlinesNothing needs decrementing when the worker wakes, and a wake for any reason recomputes the remaining time correctly from the clock.
One lock, not a concurrent collectionConcurrentQueue would be useless here: the worker needs to wait on a condition (“the head is due”), and lock-free collections give you atomicity without signalling. Monitor.Wait/Pulse is the primitive that matches the requirement.
A single worker threadMakes the concurrency small enough to reason about: exactly one thread touches the heap outside Schedule. Throughput comes later, by handing work to the thread pool.
volatile bool Cancelled with lazy removalRemoving an arbitrary element from a heap is O(n). Marking it and skipping it when it surfaces is O(1), at the cost of holding the record until its deadline. For a scheduler that is the right trade; the follow-up covers when it is not.
The algorithm
  1. Schedule: compute DueAt = now + delay, take the lock, reject if shut down, assign the next sequence number, enqueue, Monitor.Pulse, release, return a handle.
  2. Worker: take the lock and enter the inner loop.
  3. If shut down and there is nothing to drain, return — the thread ends.
  4. If the heap is empty, Monitor.Wait with no timeout and re-check.
  5. Peek the head. If it is cancelled, pop it and re-check.
  6. If its deadline has passed, pop it and break out to run it.
  7. Otherwise Monitor.Wait(gate, msUntilDue) and re-check from the top.
  8. Outside the lock, re-check the cancelled flag and invoke the work inside a try/catch.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

The preemption case, which is the one that separates a working scheduler from a plausible one. X is scheduled for +400ms, then 30ms later Y is scheduled for +30ms.

t (ms)ThreadHeap headWhat happens
0caller—Schedule(X, 400) → heap [(400, X)], Pulse
0worker(400, X)head not due; Monitor.Wait(gate, 400)
30caller(400, X)Schedule(Y, 30) → heap [(60, Y), (400, X)], Pulse
30worker(60, Y)woken by the Pulse, 370ms early. Re-reads the head — it changed
30worker(60, Y)not due yet; Monitor.Wait(gate, 30)
60worker(60, Y)timeout; due → pop Y, release lock, run Y
60worker(400, X)back in the loop; Monitor.Wait(gate, 340)
400worker(400, X)timeout; pop X and run it
What the state tells youTake out the Pulse in Schedule and nothing here changes except one line of the trace: at t=30 the worker stays asleep, and Y runs at t=400 instead of t=60. No exception, no test failure unless you specifically test preemption, and a scheduler that is silently wrong by up to the length of the longest pending delay. That is why this is the line to point at when explaining the design.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The scheduler 114 lines
Q07_Scheduler.cs
// Q7 — the C# equivalent of ScheduledExecutorService.schedule(Runnable, long, TimeUnit). public sealed class DelayScheduler : IDisposable { // internal, not private: the public Handle below takes one in its constructor, and C# // rejects a less-accessible parameter type (CS0051). internal sealed class ScheduledTask { public long DueAt; // absolute milliseconds public long Seq; // tie-break, so equal deadlines run in submission order public Action Work; public volatile bool Cancelled; } public sealed class Handle { private readonly ScheduledTask _task; internal Handle(ScheduledTask task) { _task = task; } // Returns false if it had already been cancelled. A task already running cannot be stopped. public bool Cancel() { if (_task.Cancelled) return false; _task.Cancelled = true; return true; } } private readonly object _gate = new(); private readonly PriorityQueue<ScheduledTask, (long DueAt, long Seq)> _queue = new(); private readonly Func<long> _clock; private readonly Thread _worker; private bool _shutdown; private bool _drain = true; private long _seq; public DelayScheduler(Func<long> clock = null) { _clock = clock ?? (() => Environment.TickCount64); _worker = new Thread(Loop) { IsBackground = true, Name = "delay-scheduler" }; _worker.Start(); } public Handle Schedule(Action work, TimeSpan delay) { if (work == null) throw new ArgumentNullException(nameof(work)); var task = new ScheduledTask { Work = work, DueAt = _clock() + (long)Math.Max(0, delay.TotalMilliseconds) }; lock (_gate) { if (_shutdown) throw new InvalidOperationException("Scheduler is shut down."); task.Seq = ++_seq; _queue.Enqueue(task, (task.DueAt, task.Seq)); // The worker may be sleeping until a LATER deadline. Wake it so it re-reads the head. Monitor.Pulse(_gate); } return new Handle(task); } private void Loop() { while (true) { ScheduledTask ready = null; lock (_gate) { while (true) { if (_shutdown && (!_drain || _queue.Count == 0)) return; if (_queue.Count == 0) { Monitor.Wait(_gate); // nothing to do: wait to be poked continue; } ScheduledTask head = _queue.Peek(); if (head.Cancelled) { // lazy removal: drop it when it surfaces _queue.Dequeue(); continue; } long waitMs = head.DueAt - _clock(); if (waitMs <= 0) { ready = _queue.Dequeue(); break; } // Timed wait. Re-check the condition on every wake: this loop is what makes // a spurious wake, an earlier Pulse from Schedule, and a real timeout // all behave identically. Monitor.Wait(_gate, (int)Math.Min(waitMs, int.MaxValue)); } } // Run OUTSIDE the lock. Holding it here would block every Schedule call for the // duration of the task, and a task that itself schedules work would deadlock. if (!ready.Cancelled) { try { ready.Work(); } catch { /* one bad task must not take the worker thread with it */ } } } } // drainPending: true -> run what is already scheduled, accept nothing new (shutdown) // false -> discard everything queued (shutdownNow) public void Shutdown(bool drainPending = true) { lock (_gate) { _shutdown = true; _drain = drainPending; if (!drainPending) _queue.Clear(); Monitor.PulseAll(_gate); } } public bool AwaitTermination(TimeSpan timeout) => _worker.Join(timeout); public void Dispose() { Shutdown(drainPending: false); _worker.Join(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(2)); } }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
Monitor.Pulse(_gate) in Schedule — the load-bearing line

The worker may be in a timed wait for a later deadline. The pulse makes it re-read the head. Without it, correctness degrades by up to the longest pending delay, silently. If one line in this class is worth narrating out loud, it is this one.

Why the wait is inside while (true)

After any wake the code starts again from "is the scheduler shut down, is the heap empty, what is the head, is it due". So a timeout, a pulse from Schedule, and a spurious wakeup all take the same path and none needs to be distinguished. That is the whole answer to the spurious wakeup item on the aspect list — the structure removes the case rather than handling it.

Running the task outside the lock

ready is captured inside the lock, and invoked after the lock block closes. Running inside would block every Schedule for the duration of the task, and a task that schedules more work would deadlock on a non-reentrant pattern. This is the second most common bug on this question and the more serious one.

internal sealed class ScheduledTask — why not private

The public Handle takes one in its constructor, and C# rejects a parameter type less accessible than the method (CS0051). A small, real detail: it came out of the compiler, not out of taste. The alternative is a factory method on the scheduler.

Lazy cancellation, and the volatile

Removing an element from the middle of a heap is O(n), so Cancel just sets a flag. The worker skips cancelled tasks when they reach the head and checks again after leaving the lock, since cancellation can land in between. volatile is what makes the write visible to the worker without taking the lock — Cancel is deliberately lock-free so it can never block behind a running task.

The tie-break Seq

Priority is the tuple (DueAt, Seq). Without Seq, two tasks with the same deadline come out in whatever order the heap happens to hold them, which is not FIFO and is not stable between runs. Assigning Seq inside the lock is what makes it monotonic.

Shutdown(bool drainPending) — two different Java methods

shutdown() runs what is queued and accepts nothing new; shutdownNow() discards. Rather than guessing which was meant, both are here behind one flag, and _drain is read in the same condition the worker already evaluates. Note the consequence: draining a task scheduled an hour out means the shutdown waits an hour — worth stating as a known limitation rather than pretending it is a feature.

catch { } around the invocation

Deliberately swallowing. One task throwing must not take the worker thread with it, because that would silently stop every future task. In production this logs; an empty catch in an interview should come with the sentence "and this would log".

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
ScheduleO(log n)O(1)One heap insert, under the lock
CancelO(1)O(1)Sets a flag; no heap surgery, and no lock
worker per taskO(log n)O(1)One pop, plus the wait which costs nothing
ShutdownO(1) or O(n)O(1)O(n) only when discarding, which clears the heap
space—O(n)One record per pending task, including cancelled ones not yet reached
The part worth saying out loudCancelled tasks keep their memory until their deadline arrives, which is the price of O(1) cancellation. If most tasks are cancelled — a timeout-per-request pattern, where the normal case is that the request finishes first — the heap fills with tombstones and this is the wrong trade. Then you want either an index from handle to heap position for O(log n) real removal, or a timing wheel where cancellation unlinks from a bucket list in O(1) with no tombstone at all. Knowing which regime you are in is the point.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Delay of zero or negativeClamped to 0, runs immediately. Still goes through the queue, so ordering is preserved rather than jumping the line.
Schedule after ShutdownThrows InvalidOperationException, matching RejectedExecutionException in Java.
Cancel a task already runningReturns true but has no effect — the work has already started. Documented rather than pretended away; stopping it would need cooperative cancellation via a token.
Cancel twiceSecond call returns false.
A task that throwsCaught; the worker survives and the next task runs.
A task that schedules more workWorks, because the invocation is outside the lock. This is the case that deadlocks a naive implementation.
Two tasks with identical deadlinesRun in submission order, via the Seq tie-break.
Empty queueThe worker waits untimed and costs nothing until a Pulse arrives.
Delay longer than int.MaxValue msMath.Min(waitMs, int.MaxValue) caps the wait; the loop simply waits again. ~24 days per iteration.
Clock stepping backwardsDeadlines would be mis-ordered relative to new arrivals. Environment.TickCount64 is monotonic, which is why it is the default rather than DateTime.UtcNow.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 5 follow-ups.
“One slow task delays everything behind it. Fix it.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The timing thread and the execution thread are doing two different jobs, and they have been conflated. Separate them and the problem disappears.

Approach

The worker stops executing and starts dispatching: when a task becomes due, hand it to the thread pool (ThreadPool.UnsafeQueueUserWorkItem, or Task.Run) and go straight back to waiting. The timing thread then does O(log n) work per task and never blocks.

Trade-off

You gain isolation between tasks and lose ordering of execution — tasks become due in order but may finish in any order, and two tasks can now run concurrently, so anything they share needs its own synchronisation. That is a real semantic change and Java makes the same one: a single-threaded ScheduledExecutorService guarantees sequential execution and a pooled one does not. Say which guarantee you are offering.

TimeSpace
O(log n) per task on the timing threadO(n) plus the pool
“Support a repeating task — scheduleAtFixedRate.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The interesting part is what "fixed rate" means when a run overtakes its own period, and Java has two different answers for a reason.

Approach

After running, re-enqueue with the next deadline. Fixed rate computes it from the original start (due += period), so the schedule does not drift. Fixed delay computes it from completion (due = now + period), so a slow task simply spaces out.

Trade-off

Fixed rate preserves long-run frequency and can burst: if one run takes three periods, three executions are immediately due and fire back to back, which for a health check is exactly wrong. Fixed delay never bursts and drifts. Java additionally guarantees a fixed-rate task never runs concurrently with itself, which is worth matching. I would default to fixed delay and offer fixed rate explicitly — the burst behaviour surprises people in production.

TimeSpace
O(log n) per repetitionO(1) per repeating task
“There are a million pending timers. Is the heap still right?” · likely pushback
Thinking

O(log n) at a million is twenty comparisons, so the heap is not obviously wrong. The question is really about the churn — insert and cancel rates — and about the tombstones.

Approach

A timing wheel: an array of buckets, each a list of tasks due in that slot, with a cursor advancing one slot per tick. Insert is O(1), and cancel is O(1) by unlinking from the bucket’s list with no tombstone. Long delays use hierarchical wheels — hours, minutes, seconds — cascading down, which is what Netty and Kafka both do.

Trade-off

The wheel gives O(1) insert and true O(1) cancel, and costs you exact ordering within a tick plus memory proportional to the number of slots rather than the number of tasks. It wins when delays cluster in a known range and churn is high. The heap wins when delays are arbitrary and wide, because a wheel covering milliseconds to days either needs a hierarchy or a vast array. The condition, not the name, is the answer.

TimeSpace
O(1) insert, O(1) cancel, O(tasks in slot) per tickO(slots + tasks)
“Why not `Task.Delay` and four lines?” · likely pushback
Thinking

In production that is very often the right answer, and saying so is not a concession. The statement explicitly says this is not a syntax translation, so the question is whether you know what you are giving up.

Approach

await Task.Delay(delay, token); work(); gets you delayed execution, cancellation via the token, and no thread per timer — it is backed by a timer queue, so it is not the thread-per-task brute force. What it does not give you is one owned queue you can inspect, ordering guarantees across tasks, drain-versus-discard shutdown semantics, or a place to change the timing structure later.

Trade-off

I would ship Task.Delay for a handful of timers and build this for a scheduler that is a component of the system — anything that needs to answer "what is pending", "shut down cleanly", or "a million of these". What I would not do is build it while pretending the library option does not exist.

TimeSpace
O(1) per timerO(1) per timer, plus the runtime timer queue
“How would you test this? It is time-dependent and concurrent.” · likely pushback
Thinking

Worth raising unprompted, because untestable concurrency is how this kind of component rots, and the fix is a design decision rather than a testing technique.

Approach

The clock is already injected as Func<long>, so ordering logic can be tested against a virtual clock with no sleeping at all. What the injected clock cannot fake is Monitor.Wait’s timeout, which is real time — so the behavioural tests (preemption, cancellation, survival of a throwing task, 500 tasks each running exactly once) do use real delays, with generous margins so they are not flaky on a loaded machine.

Trade-off

A fully virtual clock would need the wait itself to be abstracted behind an interface, which is more indirection than this class earns. Splitting it — virtual clock for the decisions, real time for the waiting — gets most of the determinism for one constructor parameter. Whether the clock is injectable at all is the tell for whether someone has maintained a component like this.

TimeSpace
——
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Concurrency primitives — lock, Monitor.Wait, Monitor.Pulse, and why a concurrent collection is not a substitute for condition signalling.
  • Condition-variable discipline — the re-checking loop, and understanding that it is what makes spurious wakeups a non-issue.
  • The preemption insight — pulsing on schedule. This is the single discriminating detail.
  • Lock scope — running the task outside the lock.
  • Lifecycle design — cancellation semantics, drain versus discard, what happens to a task already running.
  • Knowing the library — and being able to say why you are not using it.
  • Testability — whether the clock is injectable.
A senior engineer might say
Gets the heap on deadline, one worker thread, a timed Monitor.Wait, a lock around the queue, and lazy cancellation. That is a working scheduler and most candidates do not get there.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and: pulses on schedule and explains the failure mode without it; runs the task outside the lock and says which bug that avoids; names the void-versus-handle contradiction in the supplied signature; distinguishes shutdown from shutdownNow rather than implementing one; says that lazy cancellation leaves tombstones and names the workload where that becomes the wrong trade; offers the timing wheel with the condition under which it wins; and injects the clock so the thing can be tested. The preemption pulse and the lock scope are the two that separate people who have written this from people who have read about it.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
Before I build this — in C# the four-line answer is await Task.Delay then run the work, and for a handful of timers that is what I would ship. Your statement says this is not a syntax translation, so I will build the component, but I wanted to say the library option exists.
Also, the signature returns void, and you have asked about cancellation. Those are incompatible — there is nowhere for a cancellation handle to go. Java returns a ScheduledFuture for exactly that reason, so I will return a handle.
The structure is a min-heap keyed on absolute deadline. Absolute, not remaining, so nothing has to be decremented when the worker wakes up. And I tie-break on a sequence number, so two tasks with the same deadline run in submission order.
The interesting half is how the worker waits. Spinning burns a core. Sleeping in fixed increments trades latency against CPU and loses both. So: take the lock, look at the head, and call Monitor.Wait with a timeout of however long until it is due.
Now the line that actually matters. When Schedule enqueues, it pulses. Because the worker might be asleep waiting for a deadline 400 milliseconds out, and the task I just scheduled is due in 30. Without that pulse it sleeps through it. No crash, no exception — the scheduler is just silently late by up to the longest pending delay.
The wait sits inside a loop that re-reads the head from scratch. That means a timeout, my pulse, and a genuine spurious wakeup all take the same path. I do not have to tell them apart, which is the real answer to handling spurious wakeups — structure it so there is nothing to handle.
I run the task outside the lock. Holding it would block every Schedule call for as long as the task takes, and a task that schedules more work would deadlock.
Cancellation sets a volatile flag and the worker skips it when it surfaces. Removing from the middle of a heap is O(n), so I take O(1) cancellation and accept that the record sits there until its deadline. If most tasks were cancelled — a per-request timeout, say — that is the wrong trade, and I would want a timing wheel where cancel unlinks from a bucket.
For shutdown I would support both Java behaviours: drain what is queued, or discard it. I would not guess which you meant.
One limitation worth saying: a single worker means a slow task delays everything behind it. The fix is for the worker to dispatch to the thread pool rather than execute, and the cost is that tasks no longer run sequentially.
Video lesson plan · LinkedIn Coding — Build a Delay Scheduler · ~18 min

Build the component, then spend most of the lesson on the two details that separate a working scheduler from a plausible one: the pulse on schedule, and the lock scope.

Q9

Interval tree — covered size

HardMedium priorityINTERVAL TREESEGMENT TREEDATA STRUCTURE DESIGNDESIGNBINARY SEARCH
Why this priorityInterviewDB twice: "Interval Tree" was one of the visible titles on the partially-paywalled list before you bought access, and now the full statement confirms it with the O(log n) / O(1) constraint. Separately a merge-intervals variant where you choose the data structure is reported, and 57 Insert Interval is on the supplied number list. Medium rather than high because the framing is single-source, but the constraint makes it the most technically interesting question on the page.
LeetCodeDifficultyRelation
57. Insert IntervalMediumVariation
715. Range ModuleHardClosest public twin — not in the supplied list
1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Implement: void addInterval(int start, int end) Must be O(log n). int getCoveredSize() Must be O(1). Use a segment tree or interval tree. Discuss assumptions around: coordinate ranges, overlapping intervals, adjacent intervals, duplicates, removal if relevant.
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Supplied with explicit complexity requirements, which is unusual and is the whole question. It also names the structures to consider and lists the assumptions to discuss — effectively the marking scheme.
InterviewDB
"Interval Tree" was already a visible title on the interviewdb list before access was bought, and was worked in this workspace from the title alone. The statement now confirms the guess and adds the constraint that makes it hard.
Reported elsewhere
A merge-intervals variant where the candidate chooses the data structure is reported in the existing research — the same problem shape, without the complexity bound.
LeetCode
57 Insert Interval is on the supplied list and is the same merge-on-insert logic, returning the list instead of maintaining a total. 715 Range Module (Hard, not on the supplied list) is the closest real match, and its difficulty rating is the honest one for this.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported twice, with an independently reported near-neighbour. The complexity constraint is the InterviewDB-specific part and is where the answer lives.
Sources disagree — preserved, not resolvedThe stated O(log n) for addInterval is not achievable as a worst case, and saying so is part of the answer. A single insert can absorb an unbounded number of existing intervals — add [0,1), [2,3), [4,5) … then add [0,1000) and one call removes 500 of them. The bound is amortised O(log n), because each interval is removed at most once over the life of the structure. Claiming strict O(log n) is wrong and is trivially disproved; saying "amortised, and here is the adversarial input" is the strongest thing you can do with this question.
The statement is ambiguous — raise this, do not silently pickIs the interval closed [start, end] or half-open [start, end)? It changes the answer to getCoveredSize() and it changes whether [1,5] and [5,9] merge. I take half-open, so length is end - start and touching intervals coalesce. That is the convention that makes the arithmetic clean and matches how intervals are represented almost everywhere in practice. Under the closed reading, [1,5] has length 5 rather than 4, and [1,5] followed by [6,9] should merge because the integers are adjacent — which means the merge condition needs prev.End + 1 >= start instead of prev.End >= start. This is not pedantry: it is one of the assumptions the statement explicitly asks you to discuss, and it is the difference between two implementations that both look right.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A running total of covered ground. Intervals arrive one at a time, they overlap arbitrarily, and at any moment you must be able to say how much of the line is covered — counting overlapped regions once.

What we are being asked to compute

getCoveredSize() returns the total length of the union of everything added. Not the sum of the lengths — the length of the union.

Constraints that matter
  • getCoveredSize() must be O(1). That single requirement is what forces the design: the answer cannot be computed on demand, so it must be maintained.
  • addInterval must be O(log n), which means finding where the interval belongs by search rather than by scan.
  • Intervals may overlap, nest, touch, or duplicate. All four have to be handled and they are not the same case.
  • Coordinates are int, so the total can exceed int.MaxValue and must accumulate in a long.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
Half-open [start, end); length is end - start.Under the closed convention every length is one larger and the merge condition needs a +1. This is the assumption to state first — the statement asks about adjacent intervals precisely because of it.
end <= start is an empty interval and is ignored.Alternatively throw. Ignoring is friendlier and makes the structure total; either way, say which, because a test will pass (7,7).
No removal in the base problem.The statement says "removal if relevant". Removal is genuinely harder: it can split one stored interval into two, so the interval count can grow on a remove, which breaks the amortised argument that makes insertion cheap.
Coordinates are arbitrary int, not a small bounded range.This is what rules out a classic array-backed segment tree, which needs a bounded universe. It is the reason the answer is a sorted set of merged intervals rather than the structure the statement suggests — and explaining that choice is most of the value here.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Sequenceadd(1,5)covered 4
add(10,12)covered 6 — disjoint
add(3,11)covered 11 — bridges both into one interval [1,12)
add(2,4)covered 11 — fully contained, adds nothing
add(1,12)covered 11 — exact duplicate, adds nothing
add(12,15)covered 14 — adjacent, so it coalesces under the half-open reading
Ignoredadd(7,7) or add(9,3)covered 14 — empty and reversed
Where these came fromNo examples were supplied; these are mine, and they are chosen to hit each of the four overlap cases the statement asks about. The implementation is additionally checked against a brute-force bitmap over 300 random trials of 25 intervals each, verifying both the total and that the stored set stays disjoint and sorted — which is the only way I would trust merge logic this fiddly.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
Read the two constraints together and they tell you the design. getCoveredSize() is O(1). There is no structure over n intervals that can compute a union length in constant time on demand, so the answer cannot be computed at query time at all. It has to be maintained as a field, updated on every mutation. That is the first half, and it is the half people miss because it sounds too simple. For the total to be maintainable, the stored intervals must be disjoint and merged at all times — an invariant, not a cleanup step. If overlapping intervals could sit in the structure, the union length would not be the sum of their lengths and the running total would be meaningless. So addInterval does the merging, and getCoveredSize becomes return _covered;. Now, addInterval in O(log n). The new interval merges with its immediate predecessor if that one reaches it, and swallows every interval starting inside its extended span. Finding the predecessor is a search, not a scan, which is where the log comes from. Each swallowed interval is subtracted from the total as it is removed, and the merged result is added once. And here is the part worth being precise about: a single insert can remove many intervals, so one call is not O(log n). It is O(k log n) for k absorbed. But each interval can only ever be removed once, so across any sequence of m inserts the total removal work is bounded by m — the cost is amortised O(log n). That is the honest statement of the bound, and the adversarial input that proves the worst case is one line: insert a thousand tiny disjoint intervals, then one huge one. Finally, why not the structure the statement suggests. A classic array-backed segment tree needs a bounded coordinate universe — you size the array to the coordinate range. With arbitrary int coordinates that is four billion slots. The fixes are coordinate compression (which needs all the intervals up front, and they arrive one at a time) or a dynamic, lazily-allocated segment tree (which works and is much more code for the same asymptotics). A sorted set of merged intervals is the right answer here, and being able to say why the suggested structure does not fit is better than using it because it was suggested.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Keep every interval in a list as given. On getCoveredSize(), sort by start, sweep, merge on the fly and sum.

What it costs

addInterval O(1), getCoveredSize O(n log n). Space O(n).

Why it stops workingIt violates the one constraint the question is built on, and it does so in the expensive direction: queries are usually far more frequent than inserts for a structure like this. The fix is to move the work from the query to the insert — which is the general shape of every "maintain an aggregate" problem, and worth naming as such. A middle option, caching the total and invalidating on insert, is worse than both: it is O(n log n) on the first query after any insert, so a read-write-read pattern gets the bad case every time.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTAddInterval(int start, int end), arriving one at a time
↓
TRANSFORMATIONThe new interval is merged with every interval it touches, producing one wider interval
↓
DATA STRUCTURESortedSet<(int Start, int End)> holding disjoint, merged intervals, plus a long _covered running total
↓
ALGORITHMFind the predecessor via GetViewBetween; absorb it if it reaches the new start; then repeatedly absorb the lowest interval starting inside the span; insert the result and adjust the total
↓
OUTPUTGetCoveredSize() returns the field
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
SortedSet<(int, int)> rather than SortedList or SortedDictionarySortedList is array-backed, so insertion is O(n) from the memmove — it looks right and breaks the bound. SortedDictionary is a red-black tree with O(log n) insert but no predecessor query, which is the one operation needed. SortedSet.GetViewBetween is O(log n) and gives both Min and Max of a range, so it is the only one of the three that fits. This is a genuinely C#-specific piece of knowledge and worth stating as the reason.
Sorting on the tuple, which orders by Start then EndSafe because the intervals are disjoint — for disjoint intervals, ordering by start is the same as ordering by end, so the default tuple comparer cannot produce a wrong order. That is a consequence of the invariant, not a coincidence, and it is why no custom comparer is needed.
A long _covered fieldThe entire reason getCoveredSize is O(1). long because int coordinates can sum past int.MaxValue.
Merged intervals as an invariantNot a tidy-up. The total is only meaningful if the stored intervals are disjoint, so the invariant is what the O(1) query rests on.
The algorithm
  1. end <= start → return; nothing to cover.
  2. Set lo = start, hi = end.
  3. Find the last interval with Start <= start. If its End >= start, it reaches the new interval: extend lo and hi to cover it, and drop it.
  4. Loop: find the lowest interval with Start in [lo, hi]. If none, stop. Otherwise extend hi to cover it and drop it.
  5. Insert (lo, hi) and add its length to the total.
  6. Drop both removes from the set and subtracts from the total, so the two can never disagree.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

The bridging case, which is the one that exercises everything: [1,5) and [10,12) are stored, and add(3, 11) arrives and swallows both.

Steplo, hiSetCoveredNote
start3, 11[1,5) [10,12)4 + 2 = 6
predecessor of 3last interval with Start ≤ 3 is [1,5)
its End 5 ≥ 31, 11[10,12)6 − 4 = 2absorbed; lo pulled back to 1
lowest Start in [1,11][10,12) — 10 is in range
absorb it1, 12(empty)2 − 2 = 0hi pushed out to 12
lowest Start in [1,12]none — stop
insert1, 12[1,12)0 + 11 = 11one interval, covered 11
What the state tells youThe total dipped to 0 mid-operation and came back to 11, which is fine because nothing observes it between the two. That is worth noticing though: the invariant holds between operations, not during one. Under concurrency a reader could see 0, which is why the thread-safety follow-up is not academic. Note also that the loop re-queries after extending hi — growing the span can bring new intervals into range, and querying once would miss them.
8C# IMPLEMENTATIONRead from src/cs/qbank/ — the source that compiled and passed the tests
The structure 53 lines
Q09_Covered.cs
// Q9 — AddInterval in O(log n), GetCoveredSize in O(1). // Intervals are half-open [start, end), so length is end - start and touching intervals coalesce. public class CoveredIntervals { // Disjoint, merged, sorted by Start. Because they are disjoint, sorting by Start also // sorts by End, which is what makes the tuple comparer safe to use directly. private readonly SortedSet<(int Start, int End)> _set = new(); private long _covered; // The whole trick: the total is maintained as a field on every mutation, never recomputed. public long GetCoveredSize() => _covered; public int IntervalCount => _set.Count; public void AddInterval(int start, int end) { if (end <= start) return; // empty or reversed: nothing to cover int lo = start, hi = end; // 1. The interval immediately at or before `start`. If it reaches start, it merges. var before = _set.GetViewBetween((int.MinValue, int.MinValue), (start, int.MaxValue)); if (before.Count > 0) { var prev = before.Max; if (prev.End >= start) { lo = prev.Start; hi = Math.Max(hi, prev.End); Drop(prev); } } // 2. Every interval starting inside [lo, hi] is swallowed. Each one is removed exactly // once over the life of the structure, which is what makes the amortised cost O(log n) // even though a single call can remove many. while (true) { var inside = _set.GetViewBetween((lo, int.MinValue), (hi, int.MaxValue)); if (inside.Count == 0) break; var next = inside.Min; hi = Math.Max(hi, next.End); Drop(next); } _set.Add((lo, hi)); _covered += hi - lo; } private void Drop((int Start, int End) iv) { _set.Remove(iv); _covered -= iv.End - iv.Start; } public List<(int Start, int End)> Snapshot() => _set.ToList(); }
9CODE WALKTHROUGH
GetViewBetween is the whole reason this is SortedSet

GetViewBetween((int.MinValue, int.MinValue), (start, int.MaxValue)).Max is "the last interval starting at or before start" — a predecessor query, in O(log n). SortedDictionary cannot do this at all, and SortedList can via BinarySearch but then pays O(n) on insert. The choice of collection is the technical content of this question in C#.

Why the absorb loop re-creates the view each iteration

Absorbing an interval can push hi further right, which brings intervals into range that were previously outside it. The view is a range query, so it has to be re-taken after hi changes. Taking it once and enumerating would miss the cascade — and would also mutate the set while enumerating a view of it, which throws.

Drop does both jobs, deliberately

It removes from the set and subtracts from _covered in one place. The invariant "_covered equals the sum of the stored lengths" then cannot drift, because there is exactly one path that removes an interval. Separate remove and subtract calls would give two places for them to disagree, and the resulting bug is a silently wrong total with a perfectly correct set.

Why the default tuple comparer is safe

Sorting (Start, End) compares End only when two intervals share a Start, which cannot happen while the set is disjoint. So the comparer never sees the ambiguous case. This follows from the invariant rather than from luck, and if the invariant were ever broken the ordering would break too — which is a good argument for the invariant being maintained by one method rather than by convention.

prev.End >= start, not >

Under the half-open convention, [1,5) and [5,9) are adjacent with no gap, so they should merge into [1,9). >= merges them; > leaves two intervals that happen to touch. Both give the same covered total, but the second lets the interval count grow without bound on adjacent inserts, which slowly destroys the search performance. The total being right either way is what makes this bug hard to notice.

long _covered

Two int coordinates can span more than int.MaxValue, and summing several such spans certainly can. int would overflow silently to a negative total.

Snapshot() exists for the tests

It lets the test assert that the stored set really is disjoint and sorted after 300 random trials, not just that the total is right. A correct total with a corrupt set is a bug waiting for the next insert, so both are checked.

10COMPLEXITY
OperationTimeSpaceWhere it comes from
GetCoveredSizeO(1)O(1)Returns a field. This is the requirement, met literally.
AddIntervalO(log n) amortisedO(1)One predecessor query plus k removals, each O(log n); k is amortised O(1)
AddInterval worst caseO(k log n)O(1)A single interval spanning k stored intervals absorbs all k
space—O(n)n is the number of merged intervals, which can be far below the number added
The part worth saying out loudThe amortised argument is the answer to the question, not a footnote. Each interval is inserted once and removed at most once, so over m inserts the total removal work is O(m log n) and the per-operation average is O(log n). The worst single call is unbounded in k, and the adversarial input is trivial: insert [0,1), [2,3), [4,5) … 1,000 times, then insert [0,2000). That one call removes 1,000 intervals. Stating the bound as strict O(log n) is wrong; stating it as amortised and producing the counterexample is the strongest available answer, because the statement asked for something that cannot be delivered literally.
11EDGE CASES
CaseWhat the code does
Empty structureGetCoveredSize() returns 0.
end <= startIgnored. (7,7) and (9,3) both change nothing.
Fully contained intervalAbsorbed by the predecessor; the total does not change.
Exact duplicateSame as contained — no change.
Adjacent, [1,5) then [5,9)Merges to [1,9) under the half-open reading. Under the closed reading [1,5] and [6,9] should also merge, needing +1 in the condition.
Bridging intervalAbsorbs the predecessor and every interval it reaches, cascading. The walkthrough case.
Negative coordinatesWork. int.MinValue sentinels in the view bound mean the predecessor query still finds them.
int.MinValue / int.MaxValue as real coordinatesThe sentinel bounds collide with genuine data at the extremes. If those values are legal, the view bounds need to be long-typed or the comparer needs a sentinel flag. Worth stating; unlikely to be tested; wrong to claim it is handled.
Total exceeding int.MaxValueAccumulated in a long.
Many adjacent insertsCoalesce, so the interval count stays low and the search stays fast. This is the payoff for using >= rather than >.
12FOLLOW-UP QUESTIONSAnswers always supplied. 5 follow-ups.
“Support removeInterval. What breaks?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

Removal is not the mirror of insertion, and the asymmetry is the whole answer. Insertion only ever merges, so the interval count goes down or up by one. Removal can split.

Approach

Find every stored interval overlapping the removed range. For each: subtract its length from the total, remove it, then re-insert whatever survives — the part to the left of the removed range, the part to the right, or both. Removing [4,6) from [1,10) leaves [1,4) and [6,10), so one interval became two.

Trade-off

Two things break. The interval count can now grow on a removal, so the set can be larger than the number of inserts — memory is no longer bounded by insert count. And the amortised argument for insertion weakens: intervals can be created by removals, so "each interval is removed at most once" no longer bounds the work by the number of AddInterval calls. The bound becomes amortised in the total number of operations rather than inserts. The O(1) query survives untouched, which is the good news. This is LeetCode 715 Range Module, and it is rated Hard — which is the honest difficulty for the pair.

TimeSpace
O(k log n) for k overlapped, amortised O(log n) per operationO(n), no longer bounded by insert count
“Why not the segment tree you were told to use?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

The statement suggests a segment tree, and the useful answer explains what about the input rules it out, rather than either obeying or ignoring the suggestion.

Approach

A classic segment tree is an array sized to the coordinate universe. With arbitrary int coordinates that is 2³² leaves, which is not allocatable. Two fixes exist. Coordinate compression maps the coordinates that actually appear onto a small range — but it needs all the intervals up front, and here they arrive one at a time, so it does not apply. A dynamic segment tree allocates nodes lazily as coordinates are touched, which does work: O(log C) per operation for coordinate range C, with a covered count maintained at the root. A segment tree becomes the better answer when the queries get richer — "how much is covered between x and y" rather than "how much in total" — because that is a range query the sorted set cannot answer in logarithmic time.

Trade-off

The sorted set is far less code, has space proportional to the number of merged intervals rather than to the coordinate range, and meets both stated bounds. The dynamic segment tree buys range queries and lazy propagation, and costs substantially more code and O(log C) with a larger constant. I would build the sorted set and say exactly this — including that I would switch if range queries were added.

TimeSpace
O(log C) per operation for a dynamic segment treeO(operations · log C) nodes
“What about the coordinate range, and negative coordinates?” · supplied with the question
Thinking

One of the assumptions the statement explicitly lists, and it is the one that decides the structure.

Approach

The sorted set is agnostic: coordinates are only ever compared, never used as indices, so negatives and the full int range work with no special handling. The one genuine wrinkle is that int.MinValue and int.MaxValue are used as sentinels in the GetViewBetween bounds, so if those exact values can be real coordinates the bounds need widening to long or the comparer needs a sentinel flag.

Trade-off

This agnosticism is precisely the advantage over the array-backed segment tree, whose size is the coordinate range. It is the strongest argument for the choice and it follows directly from an assumption the statement asked about — so it is worth connecting the two out loud.

TimeSpace
unchangedO(n) in intervals, independent of the coordinate range
“Make it thread-safe.” · likely pushback
Thinking

The walkthrough already showed the exposure: the total dipped to 0 in the middle of one insert. Between operations the invariant holds; during one it does not.

Approach

A ReaderWriterLockSlim fits the access pattern: GetCoveredSize is a read and AddInterval is a write, and reads are expected to dominate. Reads are then concurrent and writes are exclusive. There is a cheaper option specifically because the query is a single field: compute the new total into a local during the insert and publish it once with a single Volatile.Write, so a reader taking no lock at all sees either the old value or the new one and never an intermediate. That makes GetCoveredSize truly lock-free.

Trade-off

ReaderWriterLockSlim is simple and obviously correct, and it has real acquisition cost on a read-heavy path. The single-publish trick makes reads free and requires care that no intermediate value is ever written to the field — which means restructuring Drop so it accumulates into a local rather than mutating the field as it goes. I would start with the lock and only do the second if the read path were measured as hot.

TimeSpace
O(1) read, O(log n) amortised write, plus synchronisationunchanged
“Also answer "is point p covered?" and "how much is covered in [x, y)?"” · likely pushback
Thinking

The first is easy and the second is the question that changes the structure, which is worth separating.

Approach

Is p covered is O(log n) on the existing set: find the predecessor of p and test whether its end passes p. Nothing else needed. How much is covered in [x, y) cannot be done in logarithmic time by this structure — it needs the sum of clipped lengths over a range, which is O(k) in the intervals inside it. To get O(log n) you need an order-statistic tree augmented with subtree length sums, or the dynamic segment tree from the earlier follow-up, where the sum is already maintained per node.

Trade-off

This is the requirement that would make me switch structures, and that is the point worth making: the sorted set is the right answer to the question as asked, and the wrong answer to a slightly richer one. Naming the condition under which you would change your mind is more useful than defending the first choice.

TimeSpace
O(log n) for point cover; O(k) or O(log n) for a range sum depending on the structureO(n)
13WHAT THE INTERVIEWER IS TESTING
The skills under the question
  • Reading a constraint as a design instruction — O(1) query means "maintain, do not compute", and that is the entire first move.
  • Invariant thinking — disjoint and merged at all times, maintained in one place.
  • Amortised analysis — and the integrity to say the stated bound is not achievable as a worst case, with the counterexample.
  • Knowing the standard library properly — why SortedSet and not SortedList or SortedDictionary. This is C#-specific and it is the difference between O(log n) and O(n).
  • Convention discipline — half-open versus closed, which the statement asks about directly.
  • Knowing when the suggested tool is wrong — and being able to say what about the input rules the segment tree out.
A senior engineer might say
Keeps a sorted structure of merged intervals, maintains a running total, and gets the merge cases right including adjacency and containment.
A staff engineer should be thinking
Does that, and: states the half-open convention before writing anything, because it changes the merge condition; says the O(log n) insert is amortised and produces the adversarial input; explains why SortedSet.GetViewBetween is the only one of the three sorted collections that fits, and what is wrong with the other two; explains why a classic segment tree does not fit unbounded coordinates and what the dynamic version would buy; notes that removal can split an interval and therefore weakens the amortised bound; and names the range-sum query as the requirement that would make them change structure. Pushing back on the stated complexity — politely, with the counterexample — is the single strongest move available on this question.
14HOW I WOULD EXPLAIN ITSay it in this order. Short sentences; stop after each and let them steer.
First, a convention question, because it changes the code: are the intervals closed or half-open? I will assume half-open, so length is end minus start, and [1,5) and [5,9) merge because there is no gap between them. Under the closed reading every length is one larger and the merge condition needs a plus one.
Now the constraints, because they tell me the design. getCoveredSize has to be O(1). There is no structure that can compute a union length in constant time on demand, so I cannot compute it at query time — I have to maintain it as a field.
For that field to be meaningful, the intervals I store have to be disjoint and merged at all times. That is an invariant, not a cleanup pass. So addInterval does the merging and getCoveredSize just returns the number.
For the insert: the new interval merges with its predecessor if that one reaches it, then it swallows every interval starting inside its extended span, cascading as the span grows. Every removal subtracts from the total and the final merged interval adds back once.
I want to be straight about the complexity, though. A single insert can absorb an unbounded number of intervals — add a thousand tiny disjoint ones, then add one that spans them all, and that call removes a thousand. So the O(log n) you asked for is amortised, not worst case. It holds because each interval can only ever be removed once, so over m inserts the total removal work is bounded by m.
On the structure: in C# I would use SortedSet of a start-end tuple, specifically for GetViewBetween, which gives me the predecessor in O(log n). SortedList would be O(n) on insert because it is array-backed, and SortedDictionary has no predecessor query at all. So it is the only one of the three that actually fits.
You suggested a segment tree, and I want to say why I am not using one. A classic segment tree is sized to the coordinate universe, and with arbitrary int coordinates that is four billion slots. Coordinate compression would fix it but needs all the intervals up front, and here they arrive one at a time. A dynamic, lazily-allocated segment tree would work and is much more code for the same asymptotics.
Where I would change my mind: if you wanted to ask how much is covered between x and y, rather than the total, the sorted set cannot do that in log time and the segment tree can. So that requirement would flip the decision.
On removal — that is genuinely harder, because removing from the middle of an interval splits it into two. So the interval count can grow on a remove, and the amortised argument for insertion gets weaker, since intervals can now be created by something other than an insert.
Video lesson plan · LinkedIn Coding — O(1) Covered Size · ~18 min

Read the two complexity constraints as the design, then be honest that one of them cannot be met literally — and show why that honesty is the answer.

1THE QUESTION, AS SUPPLIEDPreserved word for word. Everything below this line is my explanation.
Given: KNOWN_WORDS = [ "careers", "linkedin", "hiring", "interview", "linkedgo" ] Given a phone number, return all known words matching the phone keypad mapping. Examples: 2273377 → ["careers"] 54653346 → ["linkedin", "linkedgo"]
Due diligence against the existing research
InterviewDB
Supplied with a dictionary and two examples, and the second example is the whole point: two different words produce the same number, so the answer is a list rather than a word. I verified both examples digit by digit against the standard keypad; both check out.
Reported elsewhere
17 Letter Combinations of a Phone Number is on your own list. It is the opposite direction — generate every possible letter string — which is exponential and is the wrong instinct here.
Reported elsewhere
244 Shortest Word Distance II is reader-reported and is the same design lesson: preprocess once, answer many queries in O(1).
LeetCode
208 Implement Trie is the structure for the prefix follow-up. 205 Isomorphic Strings, on the supplied list, is the same canonical-mapping idea.
ClassificationInterviewDB reported. The inverse problem is independently reader-reported, which is suggestive but is not evidence for this framing. Prepare both directions — they share nothing but the keypad.
2PROBLEM UNDERSTANDING

A keypad maps letters onto digits, many to one. Given a number, find which of a known set of words someone could have been typing.

What we are being asked to compute

Every word in the dictionary whose digit signature equals the input. A list, because the mapping is many-to-one — linkedin and linkedgo are indistinguishable on a keypad.

Constraints that matter
  • The dictionary is known up front, which is the constraint the whole design turns on.
  • Word length equals number length in the base problem — an exact match, not a prefix.
  • Many words collapse onto one signature, so the return type is plural.
  • Queries are expected to outnumber dictionary changes by a wide margin. That ratio is the argument for preprocessing.
Assumptions I am making
AssumptionWhat breaks if it is wrong
Letters only, case-insensitive, mapped a–z.A word with an apostrophe or a hyphen has no keypad representation. I skip such words at build time rather than throwing, so one odd dictionary entry cannot break the service.
Digits 2–9 only; 0 and 1 carry no letters.A number containing 0 or 1 can never match a word, so it returns empty. Worth saying, because it is a free early exit.
Exact-length match in the base problem.The prefix version — which is what a phone actually does while you type — needs a trie, not a hash map. That is the follow-up.
The dictionary is large and the query rate is high.The justification for O(N·L) preprocessing. If the dictionary changed on every query, the preprocessing would be waste and a scan would be better — state the ratio you are assuming.
3EXAMPLES
InputResult
Given2273377["careers"]
Given54653346["linkedin", "linkedgo"]
Checkc-a-r-e-e-r-s2-2-7-3-3-7-7 ✓
Checkl-i-n-k-e-d-i-n5-4-6-5-3-3-4-6 ✓
Checkl-i-n-k-e-d-g-o5-4-6-5-3-3-4-6 ✓ — g=4 like i, o=6 like n, so the last two digits collide
Mine9999999[] — no word matches
Mine227337[] — a prefix is not a match in the base problem
Mineprefix 5465 with the trie2 words — the follow-up
Where these came fromThe two supplied examples are verified digit by digit in the test suite, along with hiring → 447464 and interview → 468378439. The linkedin/linkedgo collision is the load-bearing one: positions 7 and 8 are i,n versus g,o, and i→4, n→6, g→4, o→6 — so both end 4,6 and the signatures are identical.
4KEY OBSERVATIONThe step that turns the problem into a known shape
The reflex, especially if you have seen LeetCode 17, is to generate every letter string the number could spell and test each against the dictionary. 54653346 has 3×3×3×3×3×3×3×3 ≈ 6,500 candidates for eight digits, and a longer number is far worse. That is the wrong direction, and noticing that is the insight. Go the other way. The letter → digit mapping is a function: every word has exactly one digit signature, and computing it is O(L). The number is a signature. So precompute the signature of every dictionary word, index the words by it, and a query becomes a single hash lookup. Two things make this the right answer and both are worth saying. The direction with the many-to-one mapping is the cheap direction — you compute the canonical form rather than enumerating its preimages. And the work depends only on the dictionary, not on the query, so it happens once. That is the same move as Q8’s wildcard index and the same as 244 Shortest Word Distance II: find a canonical key under which the expensive comparison becomes a hash lookup. Three questions on this page use it, which is why it is worth naming as a pattern. The hash map is exact-match only. A real phone shows candidates while you type, which is a prefix query, and a hash map cannot do prefixes at all. That is what the trie is for — and it is worth introducing it as the answer to a requirement rather than as the fancier structure.
5BRUTE FORCE
How the naive version works

Two options, both bad in different ways. Generate: enumerate every letter combination of the number and check each against the dictionary. Scan: for each query, compute every dictionary word’s signature and compare.

What it costs

Generate is O(4^L) — up to 4 letters per digit. Scan is O(N · L) per query.

Why it stops workingGenerating is exponential and is the trap the LeetCode-17 instinct leads you into: it enumerates up to 65,000 strings for a nine-digit number to find at most a handful of real words. Scanning is merely wasteful, and the waste has a name — it recomputes per query something that is a property of the dictionary. Both are worth stating, because the reason each is wrong is different, and the second reason is the one that leads to the answer.
6OPTIMAL APPROACH
INPUTA known word list at construction; a digit string per query
↓
TRANSFORMATIONEach word → its digit signature, via a 26-entry lookup table
↓
DATA STRUCTUREDictionary<string, List<string>> from signature to the words sharing it
↓
ALGORITHMBuild once; a query is one hash lookup
↓
OUTPUTEvery known word matching the number
Why each structure was chosen
StructureWhy that one
Dictionary<string, List<string>>, value a listBecause the mapping is many-to-one — the supplied example proves it. A Dictionary<string, string> would silently keep only the last word per signature and pass the first example.
A 26-entry int[] for letter → digitBuilt once, statically. Array indexing beats a dictionary lookup or a chain of range comparisons, and it makes Signature a tight loop.
StringComparer.OrdinalSignatures are digit strings, so culture-aware comparison is meaningless overhead — and in the general case it can treat distinct strings as equal, which would merge buckets.
Signature returns null for an untypable wordRather than throwing. One word with an apostrophe should not fail the whole build. Skipping is the total, defensive choice.
A trie only for the prefix follow-upThe hash map is strictly better for exact match: O(L) versus O(L) but with no per-character node hop and far less memory. The trie earns its place when prefixes are required, and not before.
The algorithm
  1. Build the 26-entry digit table from the eight keypad groups.
  2. For each known word, compute its signature; skip it if untypable.
  3. Append the word to _bySignature[signature].
  4. Query: look up the number. Return a copy of the bucket, or an empty list.
7STEP-BY-STEP WALKTHROUGH

Building the index from the five supplied words, then answering both supplied queries.

WordSignatureBucket after insert
careers2273377{2273377: [careers]}c=2 a=2 r=7 e=3 e=3 r=7 s=7
linkedin54653346… {54653346: [linkedin]}l=5 i=4 n=6 k=5 e=3 d=3 i=4 n=6
hiring447464… {447464: [hiring]}h=4 i=4 r=7 i=4 n=6 g=4
interview468378439… {468378439: [interview]}
linkedgo54653346{54653346: [linkedin, linkedgo]}g=4 o=6 — same last two digits as i,n
